Almost minimal models of log surfaces Thanks: The author was supported by the National Science Centre, Poland, grants number 2015/18/E/ST1/00562 and 2021/41/B/ST1/02062. For the purpose of Open Access, the author has applied a CC-BY public copyright license to any Author Accepted Manuscript version arising from this submission.
Karol Palka

Abstract
. We generalize Miyanishi’s theory of almost minimal models of log smooth surfaces with reduced boundary to the case of arbitrary log surfaces defined over an algebraically closed field. Given an MMP run of a log surface ( X , D ) (X,D) we define and construct its almost minimal model, whose underlying surface has singularities not worse than X X and which differs from a minimal model by a contraction of some curves supported in the boundary only. For boundaries of type r ​ D rD , where D D is reduced and r ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] ∩ ℚ r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q} , we show that if X X is smooth or r ∈ [ 0 , 1 2 ] r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}] then the construction respects ( 1 − r ) (1-r) -divisorial log terminality and ( 1 − r ) (1-r) -log canonicity. We show that the assumptions are optimal, too.



1.
Main results

An important tool in the study of quasi-projective surfaces and of log surfaces is the logarithmic version of the Minimal Model Program (MMP)
[
KM98
]
, which finds a birational model whose log canonical divisor has uniform numerical properties. It is well known that in dimensions bigger than two a minimal model of a smooth projective variety can be singular
[
Mat02
, Example 3.1.3]
. The same problem appears naturally for quasi-projective surfaces and log surfaces with nonzero boundary
[
KM98
, Example 3.49]
. Whenever the MMP is used to understand the geometry of a smooth variety, passing to a singular model too quickly makes the analysis more difficult. To avoid this, for log smooth surfaces with reduced boundary Miyanishi developed the notion of an
almost minimal model

[
Miy01
, 2.3.11]
. It is related to a minimal model by a well described morphism, which we call a
peeling
, see Definition
3.14
, contracting only some curves supported in the boundary. For a log smooth surface with reduced boundary, an almost minimal model, unlike a minimal model, has always a smooth underlying surface. Moreover, one proves that an almost minimal model it is in fact log smooth. Understanding the process of almost minimalization gives an effective tool to analyze log surfaces. In particular, it helped to obtain various structure theorems, see
[
Miy01
, §2-3]
.

We show that the idea of almost minimalization can be used more widely. Given an MMP run
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
on a log surface defined over an algebraically closed field of arbitrary characteristic we define its
almost minimalization

f^{\#}
as the unique
K_{X}
-MMP over
\overline{X}
; we call
(f^{\#}X,f^{\#}_{*}D)
an
almost minimal model
of
(X,D)
. By construction, when measured in terms of log discrepancies,
f^{\#}X
is not more singular than
X
. In particular, if
X
is smooth then
f^{\#}X
is smooth. However, describing log singularities of an almost minimal model requires a detailed analysis of the almost minimalization morphism. This amounts to the analysis of reordering of contractions of log exceptional curves. For this we introduce general notions of peeling, squeezing, redundant and almost log exceptional curves, see Section
3D
. The non-almost-minimality of a log surface is witnessed by the existence of an almost log exceptional curve, necessarily not supported in the boundary, see Corollary
3.11
. Almost log exceptional curves keep some geometric properties of log exceptional curves, which makes them well-behaved and important. In particular, their intersection with the boundary is well controlled.

We extend the theory to the case of MMP runs of the second kind, in which curves intersecting the log canonical divisor trivially may be contracted, too; see Section
3F
. This extension requires additional care, as contractions of log exceptional curves of the second kind may destroy
\mathbb{Q}
-factoriality and may lead to non-algebraic surfaces.

As an application of these general ideas we work out several characterizations of redundant boundary components and of almost log exceptional curves for log surfaces with uniform boundaries, that is, the ones of type
rD
, where
D
is reduced and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, see Section
5C
. It is known that if
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-divisorially log terminal (
(1-r)
-dlt) or
(1-r)
-log canonical (
(1-r)
-lc), see Definition
2.7
, then so is its image under the contraction of every log exceptional curve, hence its minimal model, too. In general these properties of log singularities are not respected by the process of almost minimalization, see Example
5.12
. Nevertheless, we prove that they are inherited by almost minimal models in case
X
is smooth or
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\cup\{1\}
. For
X
singular and
r\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
we construct counterexamples; see Examples
5.19
and
5.20
. For the notion of
intermediate
models see Definition
2.5
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface with a reduced boundary and let
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-lc and that
X
is smooth or
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

Every almost minimal model of
(X,rD)
of the second kind is
(1-r)
-lc.

(2)

If
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt then every almost minimal model of
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt.

(3)

If
X
is smooth and
\frac{1}{r}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
then every almost minimalization of
(X,rD)
of the second kind can be decomposed into elementary contractions so that every intermediate model is
(1-r)
-lc.

Part (2) of the theorem for
r=1
implies the following result by Miyanishi, see
[
Miy01
, p. 105]
.

Corollary 1.2
.

An almost minimal model of a log smooth surface with a reduced boundary is log smooth.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface with a reduced boundary. Since
(X,0)
is terminal, by Lemma
2.10
and by the definition of almost minimalization the underlying surface of an almost minimal model of
(X,D)
is terminal, hence smooth. Since
(X,D)
is dlt, Theorem
1.1
(2) implies that an almost minimal model is dlt, hence log smooth.

∎

The new parameter
r
gives additional flexibility to the theory of almost minimal models. Previously we treated the case
r=\frac{1}{2}

[
Pal19
]
. This instance of the construction turned out to be especially useful for the difficult class of rational surfaces of log general type, which share many properties with projective surfaces of general type but at the same time have rich birational geometry. The analysis of the process of almost minimalization for
r=\frac{1}{2}
and affine
X\setminus D
was a key tool in the recent proof of the Coolidge-Nagata conjecture
[
Pal14
]
,
[
KP17
]
and in obtaining classification results for rational cuspidal curves
[
PP17
]
,
[
PP19
]
,
[
KP22
]
. Recently, it allowed to obtain strong classification results for
\mathbb{Q}
-acyclic surfaces
[
Peł23
]
, an class of plane-like surfaces studied for a long time, see
[
Miy01
, §3.4]
.

The content of the article is as follows. We discuss necessary properties of the MMP in the class of generalized log canonical surfaces introduced by Fujino, which contains
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial and log canonical surfaces. We discuss the geometry of log exceptional curves of the first and second kind; see Definitions
2.3
,
2.16
. In Section
3A
we discuss the process of reordering of contractions of an MMP run of the first and second kind, including a characterization of MMP runs for surfaces as birational morphisms increasing discrepancies of the contracted curves, see Corollary
3.3
. We discuss the uniqueness of almost minimalization and its properties for compositions in Sections
3B
and
3C
. To effectively describe the process we generalize Miyanishi’s ’theory of peeling’, defining the peeling of a boundary as a composition of a maximal sequence of contractions of log exceptional curves supported in the boundary and its images, see Definition
3.14
. We then describe how to construct an almost minimal model in steps, successively contracting redundant components of
D
and almost log exceptional curves, which are proper transforms of log exceptional curves, see Definition
3.15
and Corollary
3.21
. In Section
3F
we discuss analogous properties for contractions of log exceptional curves of the second kind, that is, the ones intersecting the log canonical divisor trivially.

In Section
4
we review some properties of log canonical surface singularities and in Section
4D
we work out characterizations of peeling, redundant and almost log exceptional curves of the first and second kind for a reduced boundary in a generality which later allows to use it in the analysis of uniform boundaries. Since almost log exceptional and redundant curves are not necessarily log exceptional themselves, log singularities can get worse in the process of almost minimalization. For uniform boundaries we are able to give a complete description of such situations, see Section
5C
, and hence we are able to control the behavior of log singularities under almost minimalization.

Theorem
1.1
is proved in Section
5E
, where we also discuss the assumptions in detail constructing several interesting examples. Finally, in Section
6
we conclude with a complete description of the process of almost minimalization in case
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.

In the Appendix (Section
7
) we reprove some and slightly improve results by Alexeev concerning log canonical singularities and their log discrepancies.

Acknowledgments.
We thank Tomasz Pełka for his help in preparing pictures and for a careful reading of the manuscript.

2.
Preliminaries

We work over an algebraically closed field of arbitrary characteristic.

2A.
Log Minimal Model Program for surfaces

Given a normal surface, a
boundary
is a Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor whose coefficients of irreducible components are between
0
and
1
. A
log surface

(X,D)
consists of a normal projective surface
X
and a boundary
D
such that
K_{X}+D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, where
K_{X}
denotes a canonical divisor. We use the Minimal Model Program, which works in the class of
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial log surfaces and in the class of log canonical log surfaces, see
[
Fuj12
]
,
[
Tan14
]
and
[
FT12
]
, cf.
[
KK94
]
. Recently it has been generalized to the class of GMRLC log surfaces
[
Fuj21
, Theorem 1.5]
, which contains both of them; see Definition
2.1
.

Given a log surface
(X,D)
and a proper birational morphism from a normal surface
f\colon Y\longrightarrow X
, the
log pullback
of
D
is defined as the unique Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D_{Y}
on
Y
such that

(2.1)

K_{Y}+D_{Y}\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}f^{*}(K_{X}+D)\text{\ \ and\ \ }f_{*}D_{Y}=D.

The exceptional divisor of
f
, denoted by
\operatorname{Exc}f
, is the sum of curves contracted by
f
. By a
curve
we mean an irreducible and reduced variety of dimension
1
.

Definition 2.1

(
[
Fuj21
, 1.4]
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface. If there exists a normal
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial algebraic surface
Y
and a proper birational morphism
Y\longrightarrow X
such that
D_{Y}
is a boundary then we say that
(X,D)
is a
generalized MR log canonical (GMRLC) surface
.

A log surface is
log smooth
if
X
is smooth and
D
has simple normal crossings. A birational morphism
f\colon Y\longrightarrow X
is a
log resolution
of
(X,D)
if
(Y,f_{*}^{-1}D+\operatorname{Exc}f)
is log smooth. Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow S
be a projective morphism from a normal surface
X
onto an algebraic variety
S
and let
D
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor on
X
. We say that
D
is
f
-nef
if
D\cdot E\geqslant 0
for every curve
E
contracted by
f
and we say that it is
f
-ample
if
D\cdot E>0
for every
E\in\overline{\operatorname{NE}(X/Y)}\setminus\{0\}
, where
\operatorname{NE}(X/Y)
is the cone of effective
1
-cycles contracted by
f
, cf.
[
KM98
, Theorem 1.44]
. We note that if
\operatorname{dim}S=1,2
then
D
is
f
-ample if and only if
D\cdot E>0
for every curve
E
contracted by
f
. We say that
D
is
f
-semi-ample
if there exists a morphism onto an algebraic variety
g\colon X\longrightarrow Y
over
S
such that
D\sim g^{*}A
for some
f\circ g^{-1}
-ample
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor
A
on
Y
(cf.
[
Fuj11a
, Lemmas 4.13, 4.14]
). We will use the following facts.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
Fuj21
, 4.3, 4.4]
)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a GMRLC surface.

(1)

If
f\colon X\longrightarrow Z
is a proper birational morphism onto a normal surface such that
-(K_{X}+D)
is
f
-nef then
(Z,f_{*}D)
is a GMRLC log surface.

(2)

If
D^{\prime}
is a boundary such that
D^{\prime}\leqslant D
then
(X,D^{\prime})
is a GMRLC log surface. In particular,
K_{X}
and all components of
D
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

By a
contraction
we mean a morphism between normal varieties with connected fibers. Writing a birational contraction between log surfaces as
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
we always assume that
D^{\prime}=f_{*}D
.

Definition 2.3

(A log exceptional curve)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface. A curve
{l}\subseteq X
is
log exceptional
if

(2.2)

{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)<0\quad\mbox{and}\quad{l}^{2}<0.

Note that although
{l}
is only a Weil divisor, the self-intersection number
{l}^{2}
is well defined, see Remark
2.9
. By the logarithmic contraction theorem
[
Fuj21
, Theorem 5.5]
there exists a birational contraction of
{l}
, which we denote by
\operatorname{ctr}_{l}
. It is known that
{l}
is a rational curve and
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)\geqslant-2

[
Fuj21
, Theorem 5.6]
. We emphasize that the cone theorem and the contraction theorem hold for arbitrary projective log surfaces, also in the relative form. In this generality a log exceptional curve does not have to be
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, hence the direct image of a log canonical divisor can be non-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, see Example
2.19
.

Remark 2.4

(Direct images of
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors)
.

Let
{l}
be a log exceptional curve on a log surface
(X,D)
and let
f
be its contraction. The following hold.

(1)

If
{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier then
f_{*}
and
f^{-1}_{*}
map
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors.

(2)

{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier if and only if
K_{f(X)}+f_{*}D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(3)

If
(X,D)
is GMRLC then
{l}
and
K_{X}+{l}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

Put
X^{\prime}=f(X)
and
D^{\prime}=f_{*}D
.

(1) If
G
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier on
X^{\prime}
then
f^{*}(G)
, and hence
f^{-1}_{*}G
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier on
X
. Assume
G
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor on
X
. Then
G+a{l}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Q}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and intersects
{l}
trivially, hence by the contraction theorem
[
Fuj21
, Theorem 5.5(iii)]

G+a{l}=f^{*}C
for some
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor
C
on
X^{\prime}
. Then
f_{*}G=C
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(2) Since
K_{X}+D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, the linear equivalence
K_{X}+D\sim f^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})+a{l}
, where
a>0
, implies that
{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier if and only if
f^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, which by (1) holds if and only if
K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(3) By Lemma
2.2
the divisor
K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, hence (2) implies that
{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. By Lemma
2.2
(2)
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, hence
K_{X}+{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

∎

If
{l}
is a log exceptional curve on a GMRLC log surface
(X,D)
then, since the coefficient of
{l}
in
D
is at most
1
and
D\geqslant 0
, we get
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+{l})<0
. In case the log surface is log canonical or
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial we have
{l}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, see
[
Tan14
, Theorem 3.19]
, cf.
[
KK94
, Lemma 2.3.5]
.

Definition 2.5

(A run of a Minimal Model Program)
.

A
partial MMP run on a log surface
(X,D)
(a
partial
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP) is a birational morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
which can be decomposed as a sequence of birational contractions between log surfaces

(2.3)

(X,D)=(X_{1},D_{1})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{\varphi_{n}}(X_{n+1},D_{n+1})=(\overline{X},\overline{D})

such that each
\varphi_{i}
is a contraction of a log exceptional curve on
(X_{i},D_{i})
. An MMP run is a maximal partial MMP run. We call
\varphi_{i}
the
elementary contractions
and
(X_{i},D_{i})
the
intermediate models
of (this decomposition of)
\psi
.

By Remark
2.4
(2) if
(X_{i},D_{i})
is a log surface then
(X_{i+1},D_{i+1})
is a log surface if and only if
\operatorname{Exc}\varphi_{i}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Hence every curve contracted by any partial MMP run is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. An MMP run is
complete
if the resulting log surface is
minimal
, that is, it contains no log exceptional curve.

Assume that
(X,D)
is GMRLC. Then every log exceptional curve is automatically
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier by Remark
2.4
(3). Its contraction leads to a GMRLC log surface by Lemma
2.2
(1). It follows that every MMP run on a GMRLC log surface is complete. Moreover, if
(X,D)
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial (respectively, log canonical) then each
(X_{i},D_{i})
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial (respectively log canonical). By
[
Fuj21
, Theorem 1.5]
on a minimal GMRLC log surface either the log canonical divisor is semi-ample or its negative is
f
-ample for some contraction of positive relative dimension and relative Picard rank
1
.

Remark 2.6

(Projectivity)
.

We note that a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial algebraic surface is quasi-projective
[
Fuj12
, Lemma 2.2]
and that a complete normal surface whose singularities are contained in one affine open subset is projective
[
Kle66
, Corollary 4]
. Also, every log terminal surface is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial, see Remark
2.9
.

2B.
Coefficients and discrepancies

Singularities of log surfaces and their changes under a run of an MMP are conveniently measured in terms of log discrepancies, see
[
KM98
, §2.3]
.

Given a Weil divisor
D
and its irreducible component
E
we denote by
\operatorname{coeff}_{E}(D)
the coefficient of
E
in the irreducible decomposition of
D
. Given a log surface
(X,D)
and a proper birational morphism from a normal surface
f\colon Y\longrightarrow X
, for a prime divisor
E
on
Y
we define the
coefficient
of
E
over
(X,D)
and the
log discrepancy
of
E
over
(X,D)
, as, respectively (see (
2.1
))

(2.4)

\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)=\operatorname{coeff}_{E}(D_{Y})\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{ld}(E;X,D)=1-\operatorname{coeff}_{E}(D_{Y}).

They depend only on the valuation of the field of rational functions on
X
associated with
E
, not on
f
. Let
\mathcal{E}(f)
denote the set of curves contracted by
f
. Then
\operatorname{Exc}f=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}E
. We have a linear equivalence over
\mathbb{Q}
:

(2.5)

K_{Y}+f_{*}^{-1}D+\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)E\sim f^{*}(K_{X}+D).

We write
\operatorname{cf}(E)
instead of
\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
if
X
and
D
are clear from the context. We put

(2.6)

\operatorname{cf}_{Y}(X,D)=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)E\text{\quad and \quad}\operatorname{ld}_{Y}(X,D)=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}\operatorname{ld}(E;X,D)E

and we call
\operatorname{cf}_{Y}(X,D)
the
coefficient divisor
. A
divisor over
X
is a divisor on some
Y
as above. It is
exceptional
if its image on
X
has codimension bigger than
1
. We define the coefficient and the log discrepancy of
(X,D)
as

(2.7)

\operatorname{cf}(X,D)=\sup\{\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D):E\text{\ is exceptional over\ }X\}\text{\quad and \quad}\operatorname{ld}(X,D)=1-\operatorname{cf}(X,D).

The
total coefficient
(X,D)
is

(2.8)

\operatorname{tcf}(X,D)=\sup\{\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D):E\text{\ is a divisor over\ }X\}.

Thus
\operatorname{tcf}(X,D)=\max(\operatorname{cf}(X,D),\{\operatorname{cf}(E)\colon E\text{\ is a component of\ }D\})
. We put
\operatorname{cf}(X)=\operatorname{cf}(X,0)
and
\operatorname{ld}(X)=\operatorname{ld}(X,0)
. For a set
\mathcal{S}
of divisors over
X
we put
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{S};X,D)=\sup\{\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)\colon E\in\mathcal{S}\}
.

Definition 2.7

(
\varepsilon
-lc surfaces)
.

Let
\varepsilon\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. A log surface
(X,D)
is
\varepsilon
-log canonical
(
\varepsilon
-lc) if
\operatorname{tcf}(X,D)\leqslant 1-\varepsilon
. It is
\varepsilon
-divisorially log terminal
(
\varepsilon
-dlt) if it is
\varepsilon
-log canonical and
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(f))<1-\varepsilon
for some log resolution
f
.

In particular, if
(X,D)
is
\varepsilon
-lc then the coefficients of components of
D
do not exceed
1-\varepsilon
. However, even if
(X,D)
is
\varepsilon
-dlt, the boundary
D
can have components with coefficient equal to
1-\varepsilon
. Note also that for
\varepsilon\neq 0
an
\varepsilon
-lc log surface is log terminal, hence
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial, see Remark
2.9
.

Remark 2.8
.

When we blow up the point of intersection of two components of a boundary of a log smooth surface with log discrepancies
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
then the new exceptional curve has log discrepancy
u_{1}+u_{2}
. It follows that the supremum in (
2.8
) can be computed on the set consisting of components of
D
and exceptional curves of any chosen log resolution. Hence
\varepsilon
-divisorial log terminality for
\varepsilon\neq 0
and
\varepsilon
-log canonicity can be verified using any log resolution. For
\varepsilon=0
this is not the case, as the exceptional curve of a blowup of a log smooth surface with reduced boundary at a point belonging to two boundary components has
\operatorname{ld}=0
.

Remark 2.9

(Numerically lc surfaces and their singularities)
.

(1)

Let
X
be a normal surface. Since the intersection matrix of the exceptional divisor of resolution of singularities of
X
is negative definite
[
Mum61
]
, every Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor has a uniquely determined pullback intersecting trivially all exceptional curves. This determines uniquely a
\mathbb{Q}
-valued intersection product of Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors on
X
consistent with the projection formula. Then the formula (
2.5
) defining coefficients extends to pairs
(X,D)
, where
D
is a boundary, for which
K_{X}+D
is just a Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor. In particular, it allows to define
numerically dlt (numerically lc)
surfaces as the ones for which coefficients of some log resolution are smaller (smaller or equal) than
1
, see
[
KM98
, §4.1]
. However, it turns out that in both cases
K_{X}+D
is in fact
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, see
[
KM98
, 4.11]
,
[
FT12
, 6.3]
. The proof uses classification of
graphs of minimal resolutions of numerically lc surfaces - see
[
Kol92
, §3]
and
[
KM98
, §4.1]
.

(2)

Dlt surfaces have rational singularities and are
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial, see
[
KM98
, 4.11, 4.12]
,
[
FT12
, 6.3, 6.4]
. Knowing the resolution graphs, rationality can be in fact verified directly
[
Art66
, Theorem 3]
and then the
\mathbb{Q}
-factoriality follows by
[
Lip69
, 17.1]
, too.

We need the following results. Note that for surfaces intersections of Weil divisors with curves are well defined by Remark
2.9
, so the numerical definitions of
f
-nef and
f
-ample divisors given in Subsection
2A
extend to Weil
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors. We remark that the two lemmas below work in any dimension under the assumption that
K_{X}+D
,
K_{X^{\prime}}+f_{*}D
and
A
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. For surfaces these assumptions can be dropped.

Lemma 2.10

(Negativity lemma
[
KM98
, 3.39]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be a proper birational morphism between normal surfaces and let
A
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
. If
f_{*}A
is effective and
-A
is
f
-nef then
A
is effective and
\operatorname{Supp}A=f^{-1}(\operatorname{Supp}f(A))
.

We note that given a proper birational morphism between normal surfaces
f\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
and a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D
, putting
D^{\prime}=f_{*}D
we can rewrite (
2.5
) as

(2.9)

(K_{X}+D)-f^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}a_{E}E\text{\ \ where }a_{E}=\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)-\operatorname{cf}(E;X^{\prime},D^{\prime}).

Applying the above lemma to
A=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}a_{E}E
one obtains the following result.

Lemma 2.11

(
[
KM98
, 3.38]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be a proper birational morphism between normal surfaces and let
D
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
.
If
-(K_{X}+D)
is
f
-nef then for every exceptional divisor
E
over
X^{\prime}
we have

\operatorname{cf}(E;X^{\prime},f_{*}D)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D).

If additionally
-(K_{X}+D)
is
f
-ample and
f
is not an isomorphism over a general point of the center of
E
on
X^{\prime}
then the inequality is strict.

Corollary 2.12

(
f
-nef canonical divisor)
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a birational morphism between normal surfaces such that
K_{X}
is
f
-nef. Then
f^{*}K_{Y}\sim K_{X}+E
for some effective divisor
E
such that
\operatorname{Supp}E\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}\operatorname{Exc}f
. Moreover, if
\operatorname{Exc}f
is connected then
\operatorname{Supp}E=\operatorname{Supp}\operatorname{Exc}f
, unless
E=0
.

Apply Lemma
2.10
to
A=f^{*}K_{Y}-K_{X}
.

∎

Lemmas
2.11
and
2.2
(1) give the following corollary.

Corollary 2.13
.

Let
(X,D)
be an
\varepsilon
-lc log surface for some
\varepsilon\geqslant 0
and
f\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
a birational morphism onto a normal surface such that
-(K_{X}+D)
is
f
-nef. Then
(X,f_{*}D)
is an
\varepsilon
-lc log surface. If
(X,D)
is
\varepsilon
-dlt and
-(K_{X}+D)
is
f
-ample then
(X^{\prime},f_{*}D)
is
\varepsilon
-dlt.

It follows from Corollary
2.13
that the outcome of a partial MMP run on an
\varepsilon
-dlt log surface is
\varepsilon
-dlt. Example
2.14
reminds that in the above corollary it is important to have an upper bound on boundary coefficients, as a contraction of a boundary component may increase the coefficient (but not the total coefficient) of the log surface.

Example 2.14

(The coefficient of the log surface may increase under MMP)
.

Let
X=\mathbb{F}_{n}:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(n))
for
n\geqslant 3
. Let
X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the contraction of the negative section
D
. We have
(K_{X}+D)\cdot D<0
,
f_{*}D=0
and
\operatorname{cf}(D;X^{\prime},f_{*}D)=1-\frac{2}{n}<\operatorname{coeff}_{D}(D)
, in agreement with Lemma
2.11
. At the same time we have
\operatorname{cf}(X^{\prime},f_{*}D)=1-\frac{2}{n}>0=\operatorname{cf}(X,D)
.

Example 2.15

(Non-GMRLC log surfaces)
.

(1)

Let
\overline{X}
be a projective cone over a smooth normally embedded curve
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n}
of genus
g\geqslant 1
. Then
\overline{X}
is GMRLC if an only if
g=1
. To see this let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the minimal resolution and let
E
be the exceptional curve. It follows from
[
Sam61
, §2, Remark]
that the local ring of the vertex of the cone is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial if and only if the restriction homomorphism
\operatorname{Cl}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}(\overline{E})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is surjective. The latter fails for curves of positive genus, as then
{\operatorname{dim}(\operatorname{Cl}(\overline{E})\otimes\mathbb{Q})=1+\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{Q}}(J(\overline{E})\otimes\mathbb{Q})}
, where
J(\overline{E})
is the Jacobian variety of
\overline{E}
(having dimension equal to the genus of
\overline{E}
), viewed as an abelian group. The latter group is divisible
[
Mum08
, p. 62]
, in particular it has nonzero rank; cf.
[
Sta23
, 0BA0]
. Thus
\pi
is a minimal
\mathbb{Q}
-factorialization of
\overline{X}
. Writing
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}+(1+u)E
and using adjunction we compute
u=\frac{2}{-E^{2}}(g-1)
, which is positive for
g>1
and zero for
g=1
.

(2)

Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the minimal resolution of a projective cone over an elliptic curve and let
E
be the exceptional curve. Let
\overline{D}
be a nonzero sum of lines through the vertex. Choosing the lines over torsion points of the elliptic curve we may assure that the components of
\overline{D}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Since
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}+E
, the cone is GMRLC. Put
\overline{D}^{\prime}=\pi_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}
. We have
\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})=K_{X}+E+\overline{D}^{\prime}+eE
, where
e=E\cdot\overline{D}^{\prime}/(-E^{2})>0
, so
E
appears in
\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})
with coefficient bigger than
1
. It follows that
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is not GMRLC.

2C.
Curves of the second kind

Definition 2.16
.

A curve
{l}\subseteq X
is
log exceptional of the second kind
on
(X,D)
if

(2.10)

{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)=0\quad\mbox{and}\quad{l}^{2}<0.

Such curves appear in the construction of a log canonical model. By
[
Tan14
, Theorem 3.19]
, on log canonical and on
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial surfaces they are either isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
satisfy
\mathcal{O}(m(K_{X}+{l}))|_{{l}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{{l}}
for every positive
m
for which
m(K_{X}+{l})
is Cartier. As an example,
[
Miy01
, 2.4.4-12]
gives a description of log exceptional curves of the second kind on a log smooth surface with reduced snc-minimal boundary.

Remark 2.17
.

A log exceptional curve
{l}
of the second kind on a GMRLC log surface
(X,D)
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, unless
{l}\cap D=\emptyset
and
{l}\cdot K_{X}=0
.

By Lemma
2.2
(2)
X
is GMRLC and we may assume that
{l}
is not a component of
D
. We have
{l}\cdot K_{X}=-{l}\cdot D\leqslant 0
. In particular, if
{l}\cdot K_{X}=0
then
{l}
is disjoint from
D
. We may therefore assume that
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
, hence
{l}
is log exceptional on
(X,0)
. By Remark
2.4
(3)
{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

∎

Example 2.18

(Non-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier log exceptional curve of the second kind)
.

Let
X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the blowup of a smooth point
x\in\overline{X}
of a projective cone over an elliptic curve
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Denote the line through
x
on
\overline{X}
by
\overline{{l}}
. Let
\pi\colon Y\longrightarrow X
be the minimal resolution of singularities and let
E
denote the unique exceptional curve. By
[
Har77
, V.2.11.4]
and
[
Har77
, Prop V.2.9]
we have
E^{2}=-\deg\overline{E}=-3
. We compute
\pi^{*}K_{X}=K_{Y}+E
and
\pi^{*}{l}={l}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{3}E
, where
{l}^{\prime}
is the proper transform of
{l}
, hence
{l}\cdot K_{X}=0
and
{l}^{2}=({l}^{\prime})^{2}+\frac{1}{3}=-\frac{2}{3}<0
. By adjunction the canonical divisor of the cone is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. The curve
{l}
is log exceptional on
(X,0)
of the second kind. Let
\eta\colon\overline{X}\setminus\{q\}\longrightarrow\overline{E}
, where
q\in\overline{X}
is the vertex, be the projection. If
\overline{x}:=\eta(x)\in\overline{E}
is non-torsion then
\overline{{l}}
, and hence
{l}
, is not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

Let us recall an argument for the latter claim. Assume the line
\overline{{l}}\subset\overline{X}
over
\overline{x}\in\overline{E}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. If
n\overline{{l}}
is Cartier for some
n>0
then it is trivial in
\operatorname{Cl}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X},q})
, hence is in the kernel of the natural homomorphism
\operatorname{Cl}(\overline{E})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}(\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X},q})
induced by the projection. By
[
Har77
, Exercise II.6.3(b),(d)]
the latter homomorphism is surjective and the kernel is generated by the class of
\overline{E}\cdot L
, where
\overline{E}
is identified with the hyperplane section at infinity of
\overline{X}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{3}
and
L\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a line. Choosing
L
to be tangent to a flex point
o\in\overline{E}
we have
\overline{E}\cdot L=3o
, so
n\overline{x}
is linearly equivalent to a multiple of
3o
. Then
n\overline{x}=0
in the group law of
\overline{E}
, so
\overline{x}
is torsion.

Example 2.19

(Non-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier log exceptional curve)
.

Let the situation be as in Example
2.18
but choose
x\in\overline{X}
so that
\overline{x}\in\overline{E}
is non-torsion. Then
\overline{{l}}
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Let
\overline{y}=-\overline{x}
in the group law of
\overline{E}
and let
\overline{{m}}
be the line over
\overline{y}
. Put
\overline{D}=\overline{{l}}+\overline{{m}}
. Since
\overline{x}+\overline{y}\sim 2o
, we infer that
3\overline{D}\sim 6{l}_{o}=2(3{l}_{o})
, where
{l}_{o}
is the line over
o
. Since
3o
is principal,
3{l}_{0}
is Cartier and hence
\overline{D}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Thus
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a log surface. Let
{l}
,
{m}
,
D
be the proper transforms of
\overline{{l}}
,
\overline{{m}}
and
\overline{D}
on
X
. Consider the log surface
(X,D)
. As above, we have
{l}^{2}=-\frac{2}{3}<0
. We have
\pi^{*}{m}={m}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{3}E
, where
{m}^{\prime}
is the proper transform of
{m}
, hence
{l}\cdot{m}=\frac{1}{3}
and
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)=-\frac{1}{3}
. Thus
{l}
is a non-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier log exceptional curve on the log surface
(X,D)
. Despite that, by the contraction theorem there exists a birational morphism
\operatorname{ctr}_{l}\colon X\longrightarrow Z
onto an algebraic variety contracting exactly
{l}
.

We say that a curve
{l}
on a normal projective surface
X
is
of elliptic type
if
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+{l})=0
, where the intersection is computed for Weil divisors. Given a boundary divisor
D
on
X
we say that
{l}
is an
isolated reduced component of
D
if
{l}
is a connected component of
\operatorname{Supp}D
such that
\operatorname{coeff}_{l}(D)=1
. If
(X,D)
is GMRLC then
{l}
, being a component of
D
, is necessarily
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier by Remark
2.17
.

Lemma 2.20

(Contractibility of log exceptional curves of the second kind)
.

Let
{l}
be a log exceptional curve of the first or second kind on a log surface
(X,D)
such that
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then one of the following holds.

(1)

{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, log exceptional on
(X,{l})
, hence there exists a contraction
\operatorname{ctr}_{l}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
and
(Y,B)
is a log surface with
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
K_{Y}
.

(2)

{l}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier isolated reduced component of
D
of elliptic type (hence is of the second kind),

(3)

{l}
is non-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. If
(X,D)
is GMRLC then
{l}
is
K_{X}
-trivial and disjoint from
D
.

Moreover, if the contraction of
{l}
exists then
\kappa(K_{X}+D)=\kappa(K_{Y}+B)
. If
(X,D)
is GMRLC then
(Y,B)
is a GMRLC log surface.

Assume that
{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then
K_{X}+{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. If
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+{l})<0
then the existence of the contraction in case (1) follows from the logarithmic contraction theorem, see
[
Fuj21
, Theorem 5.5]
. Since
{l}
is log exceptional, its contraction maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors, hence
K_{Y}+B
and
K_{Y}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Hence we may assume that
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+{l})\geqslant 0
. Let
c=\operatorname{coeff}_{l}(D)
. Then

0\geqslant{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)={l}\cdot(K_{X}+{l})+(1-c)(-{l}^{2})+{l}\cdot(D-c{l}),

so since each term is non-negative,
{l}
is an isolated reduced component of
D
of elliptic type, which gives (2).
Assume that
{l}
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and
(X,D)
is GMRLC. Then by Remark
2.17
we obtain (3).

Let
n
be an integer such that
n(K_{Y}+B)
is Cartier. Since
\pi_{*}(K_{X}+D)=K_{Y}+B
, we get
h^{0}(n(K_{X}+D))\leqslant h^{0}(n(K_{Y}+B))
. Write
\pi^{*}(K_{Y}+B)=K_{X}+D-a{l}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Q}
. Since
{l}
is log exceptional of the first or second kind, we have
a\geqslant 0
, so
h^{0}(n(K_{Y}+B))\leqslant h^{0}(n(K_{X}+D)-na{l})\leqslant h^{0}(n(K_{X}+D))
. Thus
\kappa(K_{X}+D)=\kappa(K_{Y}+B)
.

If
(X,D)
is GMRLC then
K_{X}
is automatically
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and the log surface
(Y,B)
is GMRLC by Lemma
2.2
(1).

∎

An example of a curve of type (3) on a GMRLC log surface is given in Example
2.18
. In Example
2.24
we show that log exceptional curves of the second kind as in (2) and (3) in Lemma
2.20
may indeed be non-contractible.

Definition 2.21

(A run of a Minimal Model Program of the second kind)
.

A
partial MMP run of the second kind on a log surface
(X,D)
is a birational morphism
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
which can be decomposed as a sequence of birational contractions between log surfaces (
2.3
) such that each
\varphi_{i}
is a contraction of a log exceptional curve of the first or second kind on
(X_{i},D_{i})
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier on
X_{i}
. An MMP run of the second kind is a maximal partial MMP run of the second kind.

If
\psi
is a partial MMP run of the second kind then it follows by induction with respect to the number of components of
\operatorname{Exc}\psi
that components of
\operatorname{Exc}\psi
are all
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Note that even if a minimal log surface
(X,D)
admits no nontrivial MMP run of the second kind, there can exist log exceptional curves of the second kind on
(X,D)
which are not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier or which are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier but are either non-contractible in the category of algebraic varieties or are contractible but the direct image of a log canonical divisor is not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, cf. Example
2.24
.

To make a clear distinction between an MMP of the second kind and an MMP in the sense of Section
2A
, we sometimes refer to the latter as an MMP
of the first kind
. Lemma
2.11
gives the following corollary.

Corollary 2.22

(Coefficients decrease under MMP)
.

Let
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be a partial MMP run of the first or second kind. Then

(2.11)

f^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})=(K_{X}+D)-A,\text{\ \ where }A\geqslant 0\text{\ \ and\ \ }f_{*}A=0.

In particular, for every curve
L\subseteq X^{\prime}
we have

(2.12)

L\cdot(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})\leqslant f_{*}^{-1}L\cdot(K_{X}+D).

If
(X,D)
is GMRLC then by Lemma
2.2
(1) a contraction of a log exceptional curve of the second kind leads to a GMRLC log surface, hence each
(X_{i},D_{i})
is GMRLC (note that the assumption that the curve is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier is redundant here). Since a contraction of a log exceptional curve of the second kind as in Lemma
2.20
(2) does not always exist, the MMP of the second kind on a GMRLC log surface may stop even though a log exceptional curve of the second kind exists (and is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier). Since log exceptional curves of the second kind have trivial coefficients, it follows that if the initial log surface is log canonical then so is the final one. On the other hand, the process does not respect
\mathbb{Q}
-factoriality, which is why it is better to work with GMRLC surfaces when contracting log exceptional curves of the second kind.

We will now show that the exceptions in Lemma
2.20
(2) may occur. We begin with the following example; part (1) is due to Hironaka, see
[
Har77
, Example 5.7.3]
.

Example 2.23

(Contractibility of some curves of genus
1
)
.

(1)

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
for
n\geqslant 10
be distinct points on an elliptic curve
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
and let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a blowup at all those points. Denote by
E\subseteq X
the proper transform of the elliptic curve
\overline{E}
. We have
K_{X}+E\sim 0
and
E^{2}=9-n<0
.

Assume there exists a contraction
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow Y
of
E
(onto an algebraic surface). The surface
Y
is projective by Remark
2.6
. Let
H
be a very ample divisor on
Y
not passing through
\overline{E}
and let
\overline{H}=\sigma_{*}\pi^{*}H
. In the sense of intersection theory we have
\overline{H}\cap\overline{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}p_{i}
for some positive integers
m_{1},\ldots,m_{n}
, which gives
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}p_{i}=0
in the group law of
\overline{E}
. Indeed, since
\overline{H}\sim dL
, where
d=\deg\overline{H}
and
L
is a line on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
tangent to some flex point
o\in\overline{E}
, we get
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}p_{i}\sim d(L\cap\overline{E})=3do
, hence
\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}(p_{i}-o)\sim 0
. Therefore, the existence of the contraction implies that the points
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly dependent.

(2)

Choosing the points in (1) to be
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent we get
E
which is not contractible. Such a choice is possible as long as the rank of the elliptic curve,
\operatorname{dim}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(\sigma(E))\otimes\mathbb{Q})
, is not smaller than
n
, for instance infinite. For algebraically closed fields the latter holds if and only if the field is not an algebraic closure of a finite field, see
[
FJ74
, Theorem 10.1]
and
[
Tan14
, Fact 2.3]
.

(3)

Let
C_{d}
be curve of degree
d\geqslant 4
meeting an elliptic curve
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
normally and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be the intersection points. Let
\sigma
be as in (1). Then the linear system of
C_{d}^{\prime}:=\sigma_{*}^{-1}C_{d}
contains
E+(d-3)\sigma_{*}^{-1}L
, where
L\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a line not passing through the points of
C_{d}\cap\overline{E}
, hence it contains no curve in the base locus. It follows that some
|kC_{d}^{\prime}|
,
k>0
has no base points. Since for any curve
U\neq E
we have
U\cdot C_{d}^{\prime}=U\cdot E+(d-3)\sigma(U)\cdot L>0
, we see that
|kC_{d}^{\prime}|
induces the contraction of
E
.

Example 2.24

(Problems with contractions of curves of the second kind)
.

(1)

Let
(X,E)
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be as in Example
2.23
(2). Then
(X,E)
is log smooth,
E
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and log exceptional of the second kind, but is not contractible (in the category of algebraic varieties); cf. Lemma
2.20
(2).

(2)

A contraction of a log exceptional curve of the second kind may destroy
\mathbb{Q}
-factoriality. Let
X\longrightarrow Y
be the minimal resolution of a projective cone over an elliptic curve
\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
and let
E
be the exceptional divisor. Being smooth,
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
factorial. Clearly,
E\cong\overline{E}
and
E\cdot(K_{X}+E)=0
by adjunction, so
E
is log exceptional of the second kind (and
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier). Moreover,
Y
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial, because the line over any non-torsion point of
\overline{E}
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Note that despite
Y
being non
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial,
K_{Y}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier by adjunction,
[
KM98
, Theorem 5.50]
.

(3)

A log exceptional curve of the second kind on a GMRLC surface with no boundary may be non-contractible. Let
d\geqslant 1
and let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{3d-1}\in\overline{E}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
be
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly independent points on an elliptic curve. We claim that may choose and irreducible curve
C_{d}
of degree
d
meeting
\overline{E}
normally and passing through
p_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,3d-1
.

To see this we note that the dimension of the space of planar curves
C_{d}
of degree
d
passing through all
p_{i}
,
i<3d
is at least
\binom{d+3-1}{3-1}-1-(3d-1)=\frac{1}{2}(d-1)(d-2)
. Suppose that
C_{d}
is reducible and
\overline{E}
is not one of its components. The intersection cycle
C_{d}\cap\overline{E}
is not supported in
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{3d-1}\}
, as the points would be
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly dependent by the claim in Example
2.23
(1). Write
C_{d}\cap\overline{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{3d}m_{i}p_{i}
, where
p_{3d}\in\overline{E}
and
m_{i}
are positive integers. Since
\deg(C_{d}\cap\overline{E})=3d
, we get
m_{i}=1
for each
i
. Since
m_{3d}=1
, one of the components of
C_{d}
does not pass through
p_{3d}
, which gives a dependence relation for
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{3d-1}\}
; a contradiction. Therefore, if
C_{d}
is reducible then
\overline{E}
is one of its components, hence the space of reducible
C_{d}
has dimension
\binom{d-3+2}{2}-1=\frac{1}{2}(d-3)d
, hence has positive codimension.

We have
C_{d}\cap\overline{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{3d}p_{i}
, where all
p_{i}\in\overline{E}
are distinct. Assume that
d\geqslant 4
. Let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
E
be as in Example
2.23
(1) and let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be the contraction of
E
; it exists by Example
2.23
(3). Let
{l}^{\prime}\subseteq X
be the exceptional curve over
p_{3d}
and let
{l}=\pi({l}^{\prime})
. Since
-(K_{X}+E)
is
\pi
-nef,
(Y,0)
is GMRLC by Lemma
2.2
(1), so
K_{Y}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. We have
\pi^{*}K_{Y}=K_{X}+E
and
\pi^{*}{l}={l}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{e}E
, where
e=\frac{1}{-E^{2}}=\frac{1}{3d-9}>0
. It follows that
{l}\cdot K_{Y}=0
and
{l}^{2}=\frac{1}{e}-1<0
, so
{l}
is a log exceptional curve of the second kind on
(Y,0)
. It is easy to see that
{l}
cannot be contracted. Indeed, if there exists a contraction
\eta\colon Y\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
of
{l}
onto a complete (algebraic) surface then by Remark
2.6

Y^{\prime}
has a very ample irreducible divisor
A
not passing through the image of
{l}
and then the intersection of
\sigma_{*}\pi^{*}\eta^{*}A
and
\overline{E}
is supported on
p_{1},\ldots,p_{3d-1}
. But this gives a non-trivial dependence relation between the latter points in the group law of the cubic, contrary to the assumption. We conclude also that
{l}
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, as otherwise
{l}
, being a log exceptional curve of the first kind on
(X,{l})
, could be contracted according to the contraction theorem.

3.
Almost minimalization

3A.
Relative MMP and reordering contractions

Although a run of an MMP on a log surface
(X,D)
improves log singularities (Lemma
2.11
), singularities of the underlying surface
X
may easily get worse for contractions of exceptional curves contained in the boundary. For instance, even if
X
is smooth, its image may be singular, see Example
3.17
. Passing to almost minimal models allows to delay such contractions. Before going into details, we need some preparations.

Lemma 3.1

(Sub-contractions)
.

Let
f_{i}\colon X\longrightarrow X_{i}
for
i=1,2
be birational contractions between normal surfaces such that
\operatorname{Exc}f_{1}\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}f_{2}
. Then
f_{2}\circ(f_{1})^{-1}\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
is a birational contraction.

We may assume that
\operatorname{Exc}f_{2}
is connected, so its image is a point. Hence we may assume that
X_{2}
is affine. The rational map
f_{2}\circ(f_{1})^{-1}
is defined off the image of the exceptional divisor of
f_{1}
, which has codimension
2
, hence is regular by
[
Eis95
, Corollary 11.4]
.

∎

Recall that given a log variety
(X,D)
and a projective morphism of normal varieties
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
one defines the
relative MMP over
Y
. For surfaces we simply require additionally that in the sequence (
2.3
) the contracted curves are contained in the fibers of
f
. Then each
(X_{i},D_{i})
has an induced projective morphism
f_{i}\colon X_{i}\longrightarrow Y
. The following result will be used frequently, cf.
[
Kol13
, 1.35]
and
[
Fuj11b
]
.
Recall that
\mathcal{E}(f)
is the set of curves contracted by
f
.

Lemma 3.2

(The minimal model of a birational morphism)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface and
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a birational morphism onto a normal surface. Then there is a unique
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP run over
Y
, which we denote by
f^{\#}_{D}
. Moreover, if all curves in

\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f):=\{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)\colon\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,f_{*}D)<\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)\}

are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, then
f^{\#}_{D}
factors through a partial
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP which contracts
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
. The latter condition holds for instance if
(X,D)
is GMRLC.

Assume first that all curves in
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. We show that there exists a partial
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP run over
Y
which contracts exactly
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
. Put
B=f_{*}D
(it does not have to be
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier). By (
2.9
)

(3.1)

K_{X}+D-f^{*}(K_{Y}+B)=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}(f)}a_{E}E,

where
a_{E}=\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)-\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,B)
. We may assume that
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
is nonempty. Since the intersection matrix of the divisor
A:=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)}a_{E}E
is negative definite, we get
0>A^{2}=\sum_{E\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)}a_{E}E\cdot A
, hence
a_{E}E\cdot A<0
for some
E\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
. Then
E\cdot(K_{X}+D)\leqslant E\cdot A<0
, so
E
is log exceptional on
(X,D)
. By assumption the curve
E
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then by Remark
2.4
(1) the direct image of
K_{X}+D
under the contraction of
E
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, too. By induction we obtain a partial
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP over
Y
contracting exactly
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
.

Let
{l}
,
{m}
be two distinct log exceptional curves on
(X,D)
which are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and are contracted by
f
. Let
g\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the contraction of
{m}
. By Remark
2.4

g({l})
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a log surface. Since
\operatorname{Exc}f
is negative definite,
g({l})^{2}<0
. We have
g^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})=K_{X}+D-u{m}
, where
u=({m}\cdot(K_{X}+D))/{m}^{2}>0
, hence

g({l})\cdot(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})={l}\cdot g^{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})\leqslant{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)<0.

Thus
g({l})
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and log exceptional on
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
.

Let
f_{i}\colon X\longrightarrow X_{i}
,
i=1,2
be two
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP runs over
Y
. Write
f_{2}=f_{2}^{\prime}\circ\sigma
, where
\sigma
is a contraction of a log exceptional curve and
f_{2}^{\prime}
is an MMP run over
Y
. The above argument shows that
\operatorname{Exc}\sigma\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}f_{1}
. Put
f_{1}^{\prime}:=f_{1}\circ\sigma^{-1}
. We have
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{\sigma(D)}(f_{1}^{\prime})=\mathcal{E}(f_{1}^{\prime})
by Lemma
2.11
. By the definition of an MMP run the successive contractions are allowed between log surfaces only, which by Remark
2.4
implies that the log exceptional curves and their proper transforms are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Thus all curves in
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{\sigma(D)}(f_{1}^{\prime})
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, hence
f_{1}^{\prime}
is an MMP run over
Y
. By induction on the number of curves contracted by
f
we have
f_{1}^{\prime}=f_{2}^{\prime}
, hence
f_{1}=f_{2}
. This proves that
f_{D}^{\#}
is unique and factors through the contraction of
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
.

Finally, assume that
(X,D)
is GMRLC. We argue that curves in
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. We may assume that
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
is non-empty. Then the log exceptional curve
E\in\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
found by the argument in the first paragraph of the proof is automatically
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier by Lemma
2.4
and hence the image of
(X,D)
under its contraction is GMRLC by Lemma
2.2
(1). Write
f=f^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{ctr}_{E}
. Then
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)\setminus\{E\}
is the proper transform of
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f^{\prime})
, so we proceed by induction using Remark
2.4
(1).

∎

A birational morphism of log surfaces
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
is called
log crepant
(respectively, crepant) if
f^{*}(K_{Y}+B)=K_{X}+D
(respectively,
f^{*}K_{Y}=K_{X}
). For the definition of an MMP run see Definition
2.21
.

Corollary 3.3

(A characterization of MMP runs)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface and let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be a birational morphism onto a normal surface. Put
B=f_{*}D
. Then the following hold.

(1)

f
is a partial MMP run on
(X,D)
if and only if every component
E
of
\operatorname{Exc}f
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and satisfies
\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,B)<\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
.

(2)

Assume that
K_{X}
and
K_{Y}+B
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier or
(X,D)
is GMRLC. Then
f
is a partial MMP run on
(X,D)
of the second kind if and only if every component
E
of
\operatorname{Exc}f
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and satisfies
\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,B)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
.

(3)

If
f
is a partial MMP run of the second kind and
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier then
f=f_{2}\circ f_{1}
, where
f_{1}
is a partial MMP run (of the first kind),
K_{f_{1}(X)}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and
f_{2}
is a log crepant partial MMP run of the second kind.

(1) If
f
is a partial MMP run then the above properties follow from Lemma
2.11
and Remark
2.4
(1),(2). The converse implication follows from Lemma
3.2
.

(2) Assume
f
is an MMP run of the second kind. By Lemma
2.11
coefficients do not increase. We argue that
f_{*}^{-1}
maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors and that components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. By induction we may assume that
\operatorname{Exc}f
is an irreducible curve, in which case it is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier by Definition
2.21
, so the claim is clear.

Conversely, assume that components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and satisfy
\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,B)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
. By Lemma
2.2
(2)
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. By Lemmas
3.2
,
2.4
and
2.2
(1) we may assume that
f
is log crepant. Contracting log exceptional curves of the second kind which are of type (1) in Lemma
2.20
we may assume that each log exceptional curve of the second kind on
(X,D)
contracted by
f
is as in part (2) or (3) of that lemma. Since components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, they are of type (2), i.e. they are connected components of
D
of elliptic type. Let
E
such a component. By Lemma
2.2
(1) we may assume that
K_{Y}+B
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Gluing
X-E
with
Y-f(\operatorname{Exc}f-E)
we see that there exists a contraction of
E
. Since
f
is log crepant and
K_{Y}+B
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, the contraction is log crepant and its image is a log surface. Thus we may contract components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
one by one, obtaining an MMP run of the second kind.

(3) Since components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, the decomposition is given by Lemma
3.2
and the fact that
f_{2}
is a partial MMP run of the second kind follows from (2).

∎

With the same arguments, using Lemma
2.20
we prove the following version of Corollary
3.3
(2).

Remark 3.4
.

Let
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
be a birational morphism between log surfaces such that
\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,B)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
for all
E\in\mathcal{E}(f)
. If
(X,D)
is GMRLC, then
f=\sigma\circ f^{\prime}
, where
f^{\prime}
is an MMP run of the second kind and
\sigma\colon(X^{\prime},f^{\prime}_{*}D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
is a crepant morphism whose exceptional curves are non-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and disjoint from
f^{\prime}_{*}D
.

Corollary 3.5

(Reordering MMP contractions)
.

Let
f_{i}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{i},D_{i})
,
i=1,2
be birational morphisms between log surfaces such that
\operatorname{Exc}f_{1}\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}f_{2}
. Put
\alpha:=f_{2}\circ(f_{1})^{-1}\colon(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
. Then

(3.2)

\kappa(K_{X}+D)=\kappa(K_{X_{1}}+D_{1})=\kappa(K_{X_{2}}+D_{2}).

Moreover, if
(f_{1})_{*}
maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors (for instance it is an MMP run on
(X,D^{\prime})
for some boundary divisor
D^{\prime}
) then the following hold.

(1)

If
f_{2}
is a partial MMP run then
\alpha
is a partial MMP run.

(2)

If
f_{2}
is a partial MMP run of the second kind and
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier then
\alpha
is a partial MMP run of the second kind.

Given a birational morphism
\sigma\colon X_{1}\longrightarrow X_{2}
between normal surfaces and an effective Cartier divisor
B
on
X_{2}
we have an induced injection
H^{0}(X_{1},\sigma^{-1}_{*}B)\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X_{1},\sigma^{*}B)=H^{0}(X_{2},B)
. The rational map
\alpha\colon(X_{1},D_{1})\longrightarrow(X_{2},D_{2})
is a morphism by Lemma
3.1
, so we get
\kappa(K_{X}+D)\leqslant\kappa(K_{X_{1}}+D_{1})\leqslant\kappa(K_{X_{2}}+D_{2})
. Now since
f_{2}
is a composition of contractions of log exceptional curves of the first kind and of log crepant morphisms, we have
\kappa(K_{X}+D)=\kappa(K_{X_{2}}+D_{2})
by Lemma
2.20
, hence all three Kodaira dimensions are equal.

(1) Let
E\in\mathcal{E}(\alpha)
. We have
\operatorname{cf}(E;X_{2},D_{2})<\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
by Lemma
2.11
. Since
D_{1}=(f_{1})_{*}D
, we have
\operatorname{cf}(E;X_{1},D_{1})=\operatorname{coeff}_{E}(D_{1})=\operatorname{coeff}_{E}(D)=\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
, so
\operatorname{cf}(E;X_{2},D_{2})<\operatorname{cf}(E;X_{1},D_{1})
. The claim follows now from Corollary
3.3
(1).

(2) We have now
\operatorname{cf}(E;X_{2},D_{2})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E;X_{1},D_{1})
for all
E\in\mathcal{E}(\alpha)
. Moreover,
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha=(f_{1})_{*}\operatorname{Exc}f_{1}
and
K_{X_{1}}=(f_{1})_{*}K_{X}
, so components of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
and
K_{X_{1}}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then the claim is a consequence of Corollary
3.3
(2).

∎

The following example reminds that in the situation of Corollary
3.5
in general we cannot expect
f_{1}
to be an MMP run on
(X,D)
.

Example 3.6

(Reordering contractions)
.

Let
{l}_{1}
be a
(-1)
-curve on a smooth projective surface
X
and let
{l}_{2}
be a
(-2)
-curve meeting
{l}_{1}
once. The contraction of
{l}_{1}+{l}_{2}
is a composition of the contraction of
{l}_{1}
followed by the contraction of the image of
{l}_{2}
, which is a
(-1)
-curve, hence both resulting surfaces are smooth. But it is also a composition of the contraction of
{l}_{2}
onto a singular (canonical) surface
Y
followed by the contraction of
{l}_{1}^{\prime}
, the image of
{l}_{1}
. Here
{l}_{2}
is not log exceptional on
X
, but
{l}_{1}^{\prime}
is log exceptional on
Y
.

3B.
Relative minimalization

Given a normal surface
X
with
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
K_{X}
(and no boundary) and a birational morphism onto a normal surface
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
we denote the unique relative MMP run given by Lemma
3.2
by
f^{\#}=f^{\#}_{0}\colon X\longrightarrow X_{f}
and the resulting minimal model of
f
by
f_{\min}:=f_{0,\min}\colon X_{f}\longrightarrow Y
. This gives a commutative diagram:

(3.3)

Given a divisor
D
on
X
we put
D_{f}:=f^{\#}_{*}D
. If all components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier or if
X
is GMRLC then
X_{f}
is relatively minimal over
Y
, that is,
K_{X_{f}}
is
f_{\min}
-nef, and hence
f_{\min}^{*}K_{Y}\geqslant K_{X_{f}}
by Corollary
2.12
.

Lemma 3.7

(Relative minimalization and composition)
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
and
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
be birational morphisms between normal projective surfaces such that
K_{X}
and
K_{Y}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then

(3.4)

(g\circ f)^{\#}=(g\circ f_{\min})^{\#}\circ f^{\#}.

and
(g\circ f_{\min})^{\#}
factors through
(g^{\#}\circ f_{\min})^{\#}
. If all components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier or
X
is GMRLC then
(g\circ f)^{\#}
contracts the proper transform of
\operatorname{Exc}g^{\#}
.

By definition
(g\circ f_{\min})^{\#}\circ f^{\#}
is a
K_{X}
-MMP run over
Z
, hence by uniqueness, see Lemma
3.2
, we have
(g\circ f)^{\#}=(g\circ f_{\min})^{\#}\circ f^{\#}
. Moreover
(g^{\#}\circ f_{\min})^{\#}\circ f^{\#}
is a
K_{X}
-MMP run over
Y_{g}
, hence a partial
K_{X}
-MMP run over
Z
, so
(g\circ f)^{\#}
factors through it by uniqueness. This gives a commutative diagram

(3.5)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.53471pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.66151pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
