0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{X_{g\circ f}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.22743pt\raise-55.92224pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.64763pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 67.56615pt\raise-22.67163pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-4.30058pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g^{\#}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.11647pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.27617pt\raise-52.18745pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.68576pt\raise-59.06302pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 97.11647pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.45659pt\raise-55.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.3765pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 44.66151pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 99.68576pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 166.3765pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

By the definition of an MMP and by Remark
2.4
components of
\operatorname{Exc}f^{\#}
and
\operatorname{Exc}g^{\#}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Assume that
X
is GMRLC or that components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. In the first case by Lemma
2.2

X_{f}
is GMRLC. In the second case components of
\operatorname{Exc}f_{\min}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. As noted above, in both cases
K_{X_{f}}
is
f_{\min}
-nef.

To show that
(g^{\#}\circ f_{\min})^{\#}
contracts the proper transform of
G:=\operatorname{Exc}g^{\#}
, it is sufficient to construct a partial
K_{X_{f}}
-MMP run over
Y_{g}
which contracts the proper transform of
G
. Put
\alpha=f_{\min}
. We may assume that
\operatorname{Exc}g^{\#}\neq 0
. Take a component
{m}
of
G
intersecting
K_{Y}
negatively (it is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier by the definition of
g^{\#}
) and let
\sigma\colon Y\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
be its contraction. By Corollary
2.12
we have
K_{X_{f}}=\alpha^{*}K_{Y}-E
for some effective
\alpha
-exceptional divisor
E
. Put
{m}^{\prime}=\alpha_{*}^{-1}{m}
. Then
{m}^{\prime}\cdot K_{X_{f}}\leqslant{m}^{\prime}\cdot\alpha^{*}K_{Y}={m}\cdot K_{Y}<0
, so
{m}^{\prime}
is log exceptional on
X_{f}
. If
X_{f}
is GMRLC then it follows that
{m}^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Similarly, if all components of
\operatorname{Exc}(f_{\min})
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier then the total transform of
{m}
, and hence
{m}^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. By Remark
2.4
it follows that there exists a
K_{X_{f}}
-MMP over
Y^{\prime}
contracting
{m}^{\prime}
whose outcome is a birational morphism
\alpha^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
for which
K_{X^{\prime}}
is
\alpha^{\prime}
-nef. Thus we may replace
X_{f}
,
Y
,
\alpha\colon X_{f}\longrightarrow Y
,
g^{\#}\colon Y\longrightarrow Y_{g}
with
X^{\prime}
,
Y^{\prime}
,
\alpha^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
and
g^{\#}\circ\sigma^{-1}\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y_{g}
and we continue the construction until the whole proper transform of
G
is contracted.

∎

Lemma 3.8

(Relative minimality and composition)
.

Let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
and
g\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
be birational morphisms between normal projective surfaces such that
K_{X}
and
K_{Y}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. If
X
is minimal over
Z
then
X
is minimal over
Y
and
Y
is minimal over
Z
.

The first part of the statement is obvious. By Lemma
2.12

f^{*}K_{Y}=K_{X}+E
for some effective
f
-exceptional divisor
E
, hence for any curve
L
on
Y
we have
L\cdot K_{Y}=f_{*}^{-1}L\cdot(K_{X}+E)\geqslant f_{*}^{-1}L\cdot K_{X}
, which gives the second part of the statement.

∎

Remark 3.9

(Relative minimality and composition)
.

Let the notation be as in Lemma
3.7
. We note that
(g\circ f)^{\#}
may contract more than
(g^{\#}\circ f_{\min})^{\#}\circ f^{\#}
. For instance, if
X
is minimal over
Y
and
Y
is minimal over
Z
then the latter morphism is the identity. But it can happen that at the same time
X
is not minimal over
Z
, so
(g\circ f)^{\#}
is not an isomorphism; see Example
3.10
.

By
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]
we denote an snc divisor (on a smooth complete surface) which is a chain of smooth rational curves with subsequent self-intersection numbers equal to
-a_{1},-a_{2},\ldots,-a_{n}
, respectively; cf. Section
4A
.

Example 3.10

(Relative minimality and composition)
.

Consider a smooth projective surface
X
with a divisor
[n_{1},1,n_{2}]
on it, where
n_{1},n_{2}
are positive integers such that
\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Let
A
be the
(-1)
-curve and let
D_{i}
,
i=1,2
be the component with self-intersection
-n_{i}
. Let
h\colon X\longrightarrow Z
be the contraction of
A+D_{1}+D_{2}
and let
f\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be the contraction of
D_{1}+D_{2}
. Then
g=h\circ f^{-1}\colon Y\longrightarrow Z
is the contraction of
f_{*}A
. We have
f^{*}K_{Y}=K_{X}+(1-\frac{2}{n_{1}})D_{1}+(1-\frac{2}{n_{2}})D_{2},
hence
f_{*}A\cdot K_{Y}=1-\frac{2}{n_{1}}-\frac{2}{n_{2}}\geqslant 0.
It follows that
K_{X}
is
f
-nef and
K_{Y}
is
g
-nef, but
K_{X}
is not
h=g\circ f
-nef. Still,
h^{*}K_{Z}-K_{X}=f^{*}(g^{*}K_{Z}-K_{Y})+(f^{*}K_{Y}-K_{X})
is effective by Corollary
2.12
.

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a birational morphism of log surfaces. Assume that
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(1)

If
f
is a partial MMP run then
f_{\min}
is a partial MMP run,
X_{f}
is minimal over
\overline{X}
and
\operatorname{Exc}f_{\min}
consists of some components of
D_{f}
.

(2)

Assume that
f
is a partial MMP run of the second kind. Then
f_{\min}
is a partial MMP run the second kind on
(X_{f},D_{f})
,
X_{f}
is minimal over
\overline{X}
and
\operatorname{Exc}f_{\min}
consists of some components of
D_{f}
and
K_{X}
-trivial
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier components disjoint from
D_{f}
.

Since almost minimalization is a partial MMP run on
(X,0)
, by Remark
2.4
it maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors. By Corollary
3.5
it follows that
f_{\min}
is a partial MMP run of the first kind in (1) and of the second kind in (2). We may thus assume that
f=f_{\min}
and
X=X_{f}
. Since components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier,
X
is now minimal over
\overline{X}
.

(1) Let
E
be a log exceptional curve on
(X,D)
. We have
E\cdot D<-E\cdot K_{X}\leqslant 0
, so
E
is a component of
D
. Since it is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, its contraction, call it
\tau
, maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors. We have
\tau^{*}(\tau_{*}K_{X})=K_{X}+uE
for some
u\geqslant 0
, hence
\tau_{*}K_{X}
is
f\circ\tau^{-1}
-nef. We obtain the claim by induction.

(2) Write
f=f_{2}\circ f_{1}
, where
f_{1}
is the unique
(K_{X}+D)
-MMP over
\overline{X}
given by Lemma
3.2
. It contracts
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
, so
f_{2}
is log crepant. By part (1) we have
\operatorname{Exc}f_{1}\leqslant D
. Put
(Y,B)=(f_{1}(X),(f_{1})_{*}D)
. Then
K_{Y}
and the components of
\operatorname{Exc}f_{2}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, and
K_{Y}
is
f_{2}
-nef. Let
E
be a component of
\operatorname{Exc}f_{2}
. We have
E\cdot B=-E\cdot K_{Y}\leqslant 0
. In particular, if
E
is not a component of
B
then it is
K_{Y}
-trivial and disjoint from
B
.

∎

3C.
The definition of an almost minimalization

An inductive construction of an almost minimal model and a characterization of almost log exceptional curves were given by Miyanishi in the special case of log smooth surfaces with reduced boundary, see
[
Miy01
, p. 107]
. In
[
Pal19
]
we generalized this construction in an analogous way to smooth completions of smooth affine surfaces with half-integral boundaries. Here we give a simple general definition.

Definition 3.12

(Almost minimalization)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface with
K_{X}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and let
f\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a partial MMP run. Then
f^{\#}\colon X\longrightarrow X_{f}
, that is, the unique
K_{X}
-MMP over
\overline{X}
, is called an
almost minimalization of
f
. If
f
is an MMP run (of the first kind) then the log surface
(X_{f},D_{f})
is called an
almost minimal model
of
(X,D)
.

Remark 3.13
.

Remark
2.4
(1) implies that
f^{\#}
maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors, hence
K_{X_{f}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and
(X_{f},D_{f})
is a log surface. The morphism
f_{\min}
is a partial MMP run by Corollary
3.11
(1). If
X
is GMRLC then by Lemma
2.2
the surface
X_{f}
is GMRLC. Similarly, if
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial (respectively, log canonical) then
X_{f}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial (respectively, log canonical).

The geometry of
D_{f}
is to be studied. It follows from the definition that an almost minimalization is a composition of contractions of some of the proper transforms of log exceptional curves on
(X,D)
and its images under the contractions constituting
f
. The analysis of the resulting boundary
D_{f}
comes down to the analysis of reordering MMP contractions for
(X,D)
constituting
f
. It is well-known that in general changing the order of contractions may lead to worse singularities of the intermediate surfaces, see Example
3.6
.

3D.
Peeling and squeezing

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface with
K_{X}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Given a partial MMP run
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, by Corollary
3.11
its almost minimalization
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}:=\psi^{\#}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X_{\psi},D_{\psi})
induces a partial MMP run
\psi_{\min}=\psi\circ\psi_{\mathrm{am}}^{-1}\colon(X_{\psi},D_{\psi})\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
such that
K_{X_{\psi}}
is
\psi_{\min}
-nef and
\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{\min}\leqslant D_{\psi}
:

(3.6)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.64651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(X,D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.30615pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi_{\mathrm{am}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.26761pt\raise-22.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.0847pt\raise-25.51671pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 41.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(X_{\psi},D_{\psi})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.3625pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi_{\min}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.3625pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 52.0847pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\overline{X},\overline{D})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

Conversely, using Lemma
3.7
we can construct an almost minimal model of
(X,D)
in steps in parallel to a construction of an MMP run
\psi
. We now discuss how to conveniently group these steps. The distinction between log exceptional curves contained and not contained in the boundary leads to the definition of a peeling, cf.
[
Miy01
, 2.3.3.6]
.

Definition 3.14

(Peeling, almost minimality)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface.

(1)

A
partial peeling
of
(X,D)
(called also a partial peeling of
D
) is a partial MMP run
\alpha
on
(X,D)
for which
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}D
. A
peeling
is a maximal partial peeling, i.e. a partial peeling which cannot be extended to a partial peeling with a strictly bigger number of contracted curves.

Let
\alpha
be a partial peeling of
(X,D)
with
K_{X}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.
Then:

(2)

\alpha
is called
pure
if
K_{X}
is
\alpha
-nef. In this case we say that
(X,D)
is
\alpha
-squeezed
.

(3)

If
(X,D)
is
\alpha
-squeezed and
(\alpha(X),\alpha_{*}D)
is minimal then
(X,D)
is called
\alpha
-almost minimal
. (In this case
\alpha
is necessarily a peeling.)

(4)

\alpha_{\mathrm{am}}
is called an
\alpha
-squeezing
of
(X,D)
; see (
3.6
).

We say that
(X,D)
is
squeezed
if it is
\alpha
-squeezed for some peeling
\alpha
. It is
almost minimal
if it is
\alpha
-almost minimal for some (pure) peeling
\alpha
.

Definition 3.15

(Redundant and almost log exceptional curves)
.

Let
\alpha
be a pure partial peeling of a log surface
(X,D)
and let
{l}\subseteq X
be a curve.

(1)

We say that
{l}
is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional
if
{l}\nleq D
and
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional on
(\alpha(X),\alpha_{*}D)
,

(2)

Assume
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. We say that
{l}
is
\alpha
-redundant
if
{l}\leqslant D
,
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
and
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional on
(\alpha(X),\alpha_{*}D)
.

A curve is
almost log exceptional
if it is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional for some pure peeling
\alpha
. A curve is
redundant
if it is
\alpha
-redundant for some pure partial peeling
\alpha
.

We use terms like ’
\alpha
-redundant’ and ’redundant with respect to
\alpha
’ interchangeably. A peeling restricts to an isomorphism of
X\setminus D
onto its image. Since a redundant curve intersects
K_{X}
negatively, for smooth
X
it is in particular a
(-1)
-curve. A redundant component of
D
or an almost log exceptional curve on
(X,D)
is in general not log exceptional itself, so even if it can be contracted, the effect of this contraction on (coefficients of) log singularities requires a more careful analysis. Squeezing should be thought of as a useful preparation for running an almost MMP by contracting some components of
D
without making the singularities of the underlying surface
X
worse.

Corollary 3.16
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface for which
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(1)

Let
\alpha
be a peeling of
(X,D)
. Then
(X,D)
is
\alpha
-squeezed (i.e.
\alpha
is pure) if
D
contains no
\alpha^{\prime}
-redundant component for any pure partial peeling
\alpha^{\prime}
with
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha^{\prime}\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. An
\alpha
-squeezed
(X,D)
is
\alpha
-almost minimal if there are no
\alpha
-almost log exceptional curves on
(X,D)
.

(2)

(X,D)
is almost minimal if and only if there exists an MMP run
\psi
on
(X,D)
for which
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
is an isomorphism.

(1) follows from definitions. (2) If
(X,D)
is
\alpha
-almost minimal then we take
\psi=\alpha
. Now
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
is an isomorphism, because
K_{X}
is
\psi
-nef. Conversely, if
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
is an isomorphism then
K_{X}
is
\psi
-nef, hence
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}D
by Corollary
3.11
, so
\psi
is a pure peeling, hence
(X,D)
is
\psi
-almost minimal.

∎

Example 3.17

(Peeling and almost minimality)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{n}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}(n))
for
n\geqslant 2
be a Hirzebruch surface. Let
F_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,N
,
N\geqslant 1
be distinct fibers of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
\mathbb{F}_{n}
and let
C
be the negative section. Put
D=cC+\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}F_{i}
, where
c,w_{1},\ldots,w_{N}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
. The peeling morphism is either the identity or it contracts
C
. In each case the log surface
(\mathbb{F}_{n},D)
is almost minimal. The peeling morphism for
(\mathbb{F}_{n},D)
does contract
C
if and only if
C\cdot(K+D)<0
, that is, if
n(1-c)+\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}<2
. For instance, if all coefficients of
D
are equal to
r
then the condition reads as
n(1-r)+Nr<2
, so peeling contracts
C
if and only if
N=1
and
r\in[1-\frac{1}{n-1},1]
.

Example 3.18

(An almost minimal surface with an
\alpha
-almost log exceptional curve)
.

Blowing up three times on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
we construct a smooth
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibered surface
X
with a unique degenerate fiber
T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+L
where
T_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
are
(-2)
-curves and
L
is a
(-1)
-curve meeting
T_{2}
. Let
F_{1},F_{2}
be two smooth fibers and
T_{0}
be a section meeting
T_{3}
and having self-intersection number equal to
0
. Put
D=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{0}+F_{1}+F_{2}
, see Figure
1
.

T_{0}
0
F_{1}
0
F_{2}
0
-2

T_{3}
-2

T_{2}
-2
T_{1}
L
-1

Figure 1
.
The boundary
D
in Example
3.18
.

The curve
L
is a unique
(-1)
-curve on
X
intersecting
D
once. Let
\alpha
be the contraction of
T_{1}+T_{2}
. It is a pure partial peeling. The curves

\alpha(T_{3})
and
\alpha(L)
are log exceptional on
(\alpha(X),\alpha_{*}D)
. Let
\psi
be the contraction of
T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}
. It is an MMP run with singular image
\psi(X)
. (Another MMP run
\psi^{\prime}
has
\operatorname{Exc}\psi^{\prime}=T_{1}+T_{2}+L
, in which case
\psi^{\prime}(X)
is smooth). We infer that
(X,D)
is a log smooth almost minimal log surface. Since
L
is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional,
(X,D)
is not
\alpha
-almost minimal. Thus, on an almost minimal log surface there may exist curves which are almost log exceptional with respect to some pure partial peeling.

Almost log exceptional (respectively,
\alpha
-redundant) curves will be also called
almost log exceptional
(respectively,
\alpha
-redundant
)
curves of the first kind
. Their numerical characterization will be discussed in the next sections. For their analogues of the second kind see Definition
3.29
. For convenience the following result is formulated jointly for both kinds.

Lemma 3.19

(Almost log exceptional vs redundant)
.

Let
\alpha
be a pure partial peeling of a log surface
(X,D)
with
K_{X}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. If
A\subseteq X
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\alpha
-almost log exceptional curve of the first or second kind then

(1)

A\cdot K_{X}<0
,

(2)

for every
s\in(0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
the morphism
\operatorname{ctr}_{\alpha(A)}\circ\alpha
is a peeling of
(X,D+sA)
,

(3)

for every
s\in(0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
the curve
A
is
\alpha
-redundant on
(X,D+sA)
of the first kind.

Let
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the image of
\alpha
. The curve
\alpha(A)
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Let
\varphi\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow Y
be its contraction. By Corollary
2.12
we have
A\cdot K_{X}\leqslant A\cdot\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}})=\alpha(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}
. Since
A\nleq D
, we have
\alpha(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}\leqslant\alpha(A)\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})\leqslant 0
. For a curve of the first kind the latter inequality is strict and for a curve of the second kind
A\cdot K_{X}\neq 0
by definition, which gives (1). By Lemma
2.11
for every prime divisor
E
contracted by
\alpha
we have

\operatorname{cf}(E;Y,\varphi_{*}\overline{D})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},\overline{D})<\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)=\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D+sA).

On the other hand,

\operatorname{cf}(A;Y,\varphi_{*}\overline{D})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(A;\overline{X},\overline{D})=0<s=\operatorname{cf}(A,X,D+sA).

Since
\alpha
is a partial MMP run, at each step the contracted curve is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, and hence components of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier; see Remark
2.4
(2),(1). By Corollary
3.3
(1) the morphism
\varphi\circ\alpha\colon(X,D+sA)\longrightarrow(Y,\varphi_{*}D)
is a peeling, which gives (2). Since
\alpha
is pure,
A
is the only curve in
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha+A
intersecting
K_{X}
negatively, hence there exists
\alpha^{\prime}
- a pure partial peeling of
(X,D+sA)
with
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha^{\prime}\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
such that
A
is
\alpha^{\prime}
-redundant of the first kind on
(X,D+sA)
. But then it is
\alpha
-redundant of the first kind by Corollary
2.22
, hence (3).

∎

3E.
Effective almost minimalization

The following lemma shows how to conveniently construct an almost minimal model of a log surface
(X,D)
in parallel to a minimal model. It shows also that the construction generalizes Miyanishi’s construction for log smooth surfaces (with the squeezing of the boundary being an analogue of an snc-minimalization), see
[
Miy01
, 2.3.11]
, cf. Remark
3.24

Lemma 3.20

(Effective almost minimalization)
.

Assume that
\overline{\psi}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is a partial MMP run on a log surface with
K_{X}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Let
n
denote the number of
\overline{\psi}
-exceptional curves not contained in
D
. Choose a decomposition
\overline{\psi}=\overline{\psi}_{n}\circ\ldots\circ\overline{\psi}_{0}
, where
\overline{\psi}_{0}\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X}_{1},\overline{D}_{1})
is a partial peeling and
\overline{\psi}_{i}\colon(\overline{X}_{i},\overline{D}_{i})\longrightarrow(\overline{X}_{i+1},\overline{D}_{i+1})
for
i\geqslant 1
, where
(\overline{X}_{n+1},\overline{D}_{n+1})=(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, is a composition of a contraction of a single log exceptional curve
\overline{A}_{i}\nleq\overline{D}_{i}
followed by a partial peeling. Then there exists a commutative diagram

where

(1)

Each
(X_{i},D_{i})
,
i\geqslant 1
is a log surface with
K_{X_{i}}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(2)

\alpha_{i}
is a pure partial peeling for
i\geqslant 1
,

(3)

\psi_{0}=(\overline{\psi}_{0})_{\mathrm{am}}
and
\psi_{i}=(\overline{\psi}_{i}\circ\alpha_{i})_{\mathrm{am}}
for
i\geqslant 1
, hence
\psi_{i}
is a composition of a contraction of an
\alpha_{i}
-almost log exceptional curve
A_{i}:=(\alpha_{i})_{*}^{-1}\overline{A}_{i}\subseteq X_{i}
followed by a
\tau_{i}
-squeezing, where
\overline{\psi}_{i}\circ\alpha_{i}=\tau_{i}\circ\operatorname{ctr}_{A_{i}}
.

(4)

\psi:=\psi_{n}\circ\ldots\circ\psi_{0}=\overline{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}}
and
(X_{n+1},D_{n+1})
is
\alpha_{n+1}
-almost minimal. In particular, if
(\overline{X}_{n+1},\overline{D}_{n+1})
is minimal then
(X_{n+1},D_{n+1})
is an almost minimal model of
(X,D)
.

Moreover, for every such diagram we have:

(5)

\operatorname{cf}(X_{i+1})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(X_{i})
for
i\geqslant 0
, where
X_{0}=X
,

(6)

\kappa(K_{X_{i}}+D_{i})=\kappa(K_{X}+D)
for
i\geqslant 1
,

We define inductively
\psi_{i}
to be an almost minimalization as in (3) and hence by Corollary
3.11
and Remark
2.4
(1) we get that
K_{X_{i+1}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and the induced
\alpha_{i+1}
is as in (2). Note that
\psi_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}
map
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors by Remark
2.4
(1). By Lemma
3.19

A_{i}\cdot K_{X_{i}}<0
, so
\psi_{i}
is a composition of a contraction of
A_{i}
with a
\tau_{i}
-squeezing. Part (4) follows from equation (
3.4
). Part (5) is a consequence of the definition of almost minimalization and of Lemma
2.11
. Part (6) follows from Corollary
3.5
.

∎

Corollary 3.21

(Summary)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface with
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
K_{X}
, which admits a complete MMP run, for instance
(X,D)
is GMRLC. The above lemma shows that an almost minimal model of
(X,D)
can be constructed inductively as follows. All contractions below are assumed to be over the chosen minimal model, so that the intermediate surfaces admit complete MMP runs, too.

(1)

Choose a maximal pure partial peeling morphism
\alpha
of
(X,D)
and consider the triple
(X,D,\alpha)
.

(2)

If there exists a curve
{l}\subseteq X
such that
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional then put
D^{\prime}:=D
in case
{l}\leqslant D
and
D^{\prime}:=D+{l}
otherwise. Let
\sigma
be an
\operatorname{ctr}_{\alpha({l})}\circ\alpha
-squeezing of
(X,D^{\prime})
and let
\alpha^{\prime}
be a maximal pure partial peeling extending
\operatorname{ctr}_{\alpha({l})}\circ\alpha\circ\sigma^{-1}
. Replace
(X,D,\alpha)
with
(\sigma(X),\sigma_{*}D^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
and repeat.

(3)

If there is no
{l}
as in (2) then the resulting
(X,D)
is an almost minimal model and
\alpha
is a pure peeling morphism onto a minimal model.

Remark 3.22
.

Note that in part (2) of Corollary
3.21

{l}
is necessarily a redundant component of
D
or an
\alpha
-almost log exceptional curve, hence a redundant component of
D^{\prime}
; cf. Lemma
3.19
. Also,
\sigma
is the composition of successive contractions of redundant components of
D^{\prime}
and its respective images which are contained in
{l}+\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
and its respective images.

Remark 3.23
.

In Lemma
3.20
assume that
\overline{\psi}
is a complete MMP run and choose
\overline{\psi}_{0}
to be a peeling and
\overline{\psi}_{i}
for
i\geqslant 1
to be a composition of a contraction of a single log exceptional curve
\overline{A}_{i}\nleq\overline{D}_{i}
and a peeling. Then in Lemma
3.20

\alpha_{i}
are (pure) peeling morphisms and
(X_{i},D_{i})
are squeezed.

Remark 3.24

(Comparison with Miyanishi’s construction)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface with a reduced boundary. In this case there is already a definition of an almost minimal model by Miyanishi
[
Miy01
, 2.3.11]
which is very close the one above. The difference is that in our construction
\psi
(and
\overline{\psi}
) contracts slightly fewer components of the boundary. To see this difference let
C\leqslant D
be a superfluous component, that is, a
(-1)
-curve meeting at most two other components of
D
, each at most once in the sense of intersection theory. In particular
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 2
. If
\beta_{D}(C)\leqslant 1
or if
\beta_{D}(C)=2
and
C
meets some maximal admissible (rational) twig or fork of
D
(see Section
4
) then it is almost log exceptional and hence is contracted by almost minimalization. However, if
\beta_{D}(C)=2
and
C
meets no maximal admissible (rational) twig or fork of
D
then in Miyanishi’s construction we do contract
C
(which is reasonable, as this respects log smoothness), but we do not do that according to our definition. Indeed, in the latter case
C
is not almost log exceptional in our sense. In fact, it is not possible to assure in general that the contraction of
C
of the latter type is a part of some MMP run. Still, if one wants to contract such curves too, then in our approach this can be done after an almost minimal model in the sense of our definition is reached. Indeed, note that we have
C\cdot(K_{X}+D)=0
, so
C
is log exceptional of the second kind and is disjoint form the exceptional locus of a peeling of
(X,D)
, see Definition
2.10
. Then it is easy to see that after such additional contraction of
C
the image of
(X,D)
remains almost minimal; cf. Lemma
3.25
.

3F.
Analogues for curves of the second kind

We now discuss analogues of the above definitions for curves of the second kind. The following lemma implies that contractions of log exceptional curves of the second kind (if they exist, see Example
2.24
) can be delayed until a minimal model is reached and then they respect minimality.

Lemma 3.25

(Delaying contractions of the second kind)
.

Let
\gamma\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a log crepant MMP run of the second kind and let
\overline{\psi}\colon(\overline{X},\overline{D})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
be a partial MMP run (of the first kind). Assume that
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then there exists
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
, a partial MMP run of the first kind with
\gamma_{*}^{-1}(\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\psi})\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}\psi
, such that
K_{Y}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and the induced birational morphism
\overline{\gamma}\colon(Y,B)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
is a log crepant MMP run of the second kind. In particular, if
(X,D)
is minimal then
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is minimal.

The morphism
f=\overline{\psi}\circ\gamma
is an MMP run of the second kind. Since components of
\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\psi}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, so are the components of
\operatorname{Exc}f
. By Lemma
3.2

f
factors through a unique MMP run on
(X,D)
over
\overline{Y}
, call it
\psi
, and
\psi
contracts
\mathcal{E}^{+}_{D}(f)
. This gives a commutative diagram

(3.8)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.64651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(X,D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.20052pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.34494pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(Y,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.98158pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\gamma}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.98158pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-13.2778pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\overline{X},\overline{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.49078pt\raise-26.16112pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\psi}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.70378pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 44.70378pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\overline{Y},\overline{B})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
\overline{\gamma}
is log crepant and components of
\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\gamma}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Moreover,
K_{Y}=\psi_{*}K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. By Corollary
3.3

\overline{\gamma}
is an MMP run of the second kind.

∎

The following example shows that for
\overline{\psi}
of the second kind it can happen that
\gamma_{*}^{-1}(\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\psi})
is non-contractible (in the category of algebraic varieties).

Example 3.26

(Non-permutable order of contractions of log exceptional curves of the second kind)
.

Let
\sigma\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a blowup as in Example
2.23
(3) and let
\overline{{l}}
be the proper transform of the cubic. The centers
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
of
\sigma
are chosen so that there exists a contraction of
\overline{{l}}
; denote it by
\overline{\psi}\colon\overline{X}\longrightarrow\overline{Y}
. Now pick a point
p\in\overline{{l}}
not lying over any
p_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. Denote by
\gamma\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
the blowup at
p
and put
{l}=\gamma_{*}^{-1}\overline{{l}}
. Let
E
be the exceptional curve of
\gamma
. Clearly,
{l}
and
E
are log exceptional curves of the second kind on
(X,{l})
and by construction we have a log crepant contraction
\gamma
of
E
followed by the contraction of
\gamma({l})
. If there exists a contraction of
{l}
then the induced morphism contracts the image of
E
, that is, the order of contractions can be changed and we get a commutative diagram (
3.8
). But the existence of the contraction of
{l}
implies that
\sigma(p),p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-linearly dependent in the group law of
\sigma({l})
. Assuming that the field is not an algebraic closure of a finite field we can always pick
p
so that this is impossible; cf. Example
2.23
(2).

Remark 3.27

(Non-contractible components in the exceptional locus)
.

Note that in Example
3.26
we obtain a birational contraction from a smooth surface whose exceptional locus consists of two smooth irreducible curves (which are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, as the surface is smooth), but one of them cannot be contracted onto an algebraic surface.

Lemma 3.28

(Advancing contractions of the second kind)
.

Let
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a pure partial peeling morphism on a log surface and let
\overline{\gamma}\colon(\overline{X},\overline{D})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
be a partial MMP run of the second kind. Assume that
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Then there exists a partial MMP run of the second kind
\gamma\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
with
\alpha_{*}^{-1}\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\gamma}\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}\gamma
onto a log surface with
K_{Y}
-
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and a pure partial peeling morphism
\overline{\alpha}
such that the following diagram commutes

(3.9)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 17.64651pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-17.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(X,D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.97223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\alpha}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.54626pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\gamma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.64651pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(Y,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.98158pt\raise-16.02777pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\alpha}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.98158pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-13.2778pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\overline{X},\overline{D})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.4908pt\raise-26.10558pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\gamma}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.70378pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 44.70378pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\overline{Y},\overline{B})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

If
\alpha
is a peeling then
\overline{\alpha}
is a peeling.

By induction we may assume that
\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\gamma}
consists of a single log exceptional curve
\overline{{l}}
of the first or second kind which is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Since
\alpha
is a partial MMP run, each component of
E=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Let
\overline{D}^{\prime}
be the proper transform of
\overline{D}
on
X
.

We first prove that there exists
\gamma^{\prime}
, the contraction of
{l}:=\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{{l}}
, and that it maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors. Since
\overline{{l}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier,
{l}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. We may assume that
E\neq 0
and that each connected component of
E
meets
{l}
. Put
\widetilde{E}=\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. We have

\widetilde{E}=\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})-(K_{X}+\overline{D}^{\prime}),

so for every component
E_{0}
of
E
we get
E_{0}\cdot\widetilde{E}=-E_{0}\cdot(K_{X}+\overline{D}^{\prime})\leqslant-E_{0}\cdot\overline{D}^{\prime}\leqslant 0
, because
\alpha
is pure. By Lemma
2.10
we obtain
\widetilde{E}\geqslant 0
. We have
\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};\overline{X},\overline{D})<\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};X,D)=\operatorname{coeff}_{E_{0}}(D)\leqslant 1
, so
\operatorname{cf}(\overline{X},\overline{D})\leqslant 1
. We deduce that
0\leqslant\widetilde{E}\leqslant E
and
{l}
is a log exceptional curve of the first or second kind on
(X,\overline{D}^{\prime}+\widetilde{E})
. The latter is a log surface, as
K_{X}+D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and each component of
E
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

If
{l}\cdot\widetilde{E}\neq 0
then
{l}
is not a connected component of
\overline{D}^{\prime}+\widetilde{E}
, hence we are done by Lemma
2.20
. We may thus assume that
{l}\cdot\widetilde{E}=0
. Since
{l}
meets every connected component of
\operatorname{Supp}E
, Lemma
2.10
implies that
\widetilde{E}=0
. We have now
(\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-K_{X})+(\alpha^{*}\overline{D}-\overline{D}^{\prime})=0
, so by Corollary
2.12
we get
\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}
and
\alpha^{*}\overline{D}=\overline{D}^{\prime}
. It follows that
\overline{D}^{\prime}\cdot E=0
. Since
{l}\cdot E>0
, the curve
{l}
is not a component of
\overline{D}^{\prime}
. Again, we are done by Lemma
2.20
.

Let
\alpha^{\prime}=\overline{\gamma}\circ\alpha\circ(\gamma^{\prime})^{-1}
be the induced rational map. It is regular due to the normality of
\gamma^{\prime}(X)
. Since
\gamma^{\prime}
maps
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisors and since for every prime exceptional component
E_{0}
of
E
we have
\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};\alpha^{\prime}(X),\alpha^{\prime}_{*}D)<\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};X,D)
, by Corollary
3.5
(1) the morphism
\alpha^{\prime}
is a partial peeling. Since
K_{\alpha^{\prime}(X)}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, we have a decomposition
\alpha^{\prime}=\overline{\alpha}\circ\alpha^{\prime\prime}
, where
\alpha^{\prime\prime}
is an almost minimalization of
\alpha^{\prime}
. Then
\gamma=\alpha^{\prime\prime}\circ\gamma^{\prime}
and
\overline{\alpha}
are as required.

We have
\overline{\gamma}^{*}(K_{\overline{Y}}+\overline{B})=K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D}
, which implies that a proper transform of a log exceptional curve on
(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
is log exceptional on
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. Therefore, if
\alpha
is a peeling then
\overline{\alpha}
is a peeling.

∎

In an analogy to Definition
3.15
we introduce the following definition.

Definition 3.29

(Redundant and almost log exceptional curves of the second kind)
.

Let
\alpha
be a pure partial peeling of a log surface
(X,D)
with
K_{X}
being
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and let
{l}\subseteq X
be a curve.

(1)

We say that
{l}
is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional of the second kind
if
{l}\nleq D
,
{l}\cdot K_{X}\neq 0
and
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind on
(\alpha(X),\alpha_{*}D)
.

(2)

We say that
{l}
is
\alpha
-redundant of the second kind
if
{l}\leqslant D
,
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
and
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind on
(\alpha(X),\alpha_{*}D)
,

A curve is
almost log exceptional of the second kind
if it is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional of the second kind for some pure partial peeling
\alpha
. A curve is
redundant of the second kind
if it is
\alpha
-redundant of the second kind for some pure partial peeling
\alpha
.

Recall that if
{l}
is an almost log exceptional curve of the first or second kind on
(X,D)
then by Lemma
3.19
we have
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
. In particular, almost log exceptional curves of the second kind and redundant curves of the second kind can be contracted.

The following example shows in particular that in the situation of Lemma
3.28
the morphism
\gamma
may contract more than just
\alpha_{*}^{-1}\operatorname{Exc}\overline{\gamma}
.

Example 3.30

(Non-purity of the induced peeling)
.

Let
X
be a smooth surface containing a chain
[1,2]
, that is, two smooth rational curves
{l}
and
D
, such that
{l}^{2}=-1
,
D^{2}=-2
and
{l}\cdot D=1
. The peeling morphism
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is the contraction of
D
(here
\overline{D}=0
), so it is pure and
\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})\sim K_{X}
. It follows that
{l}
is almost log exceptional of the first kind. After the contraction of
{l}
the image of
D
is a
(-1)
-curve, so the induced peeling contracting the image of
D
is not pure.

Similarly, consider a smooth surface
X
containing a chain
[2,1,0]
. Let
D_{1}=[2]
,
{l}=[1]
and
D_{2}=[0]
be its components. Put
D=D_{1}+D_{2}
. The peeling morphism
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is the contraction of
D_{1}
, so it is pure and
\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})\sim K_{X}+D_{2}
. It follows that now
{l}
is almost log exceptional of the second kind. Again, after the contraction of
{l}
the image of
D
is
[1,-1]
, so the induced peeling contracts the
(-1)
-curve, hence is not pure.

Definition 3.31

(Almost minimalization of the second kind)
.

Let
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a partial MMP run of the second kind with
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
K_{X}
.

(1)

We call
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
a
partial almost minimalization of the second kind of
(X,D)
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\subseteq\operatorname{Supp}D
then
\psi
is a
partial peeling of
(X,D)
of the second kind
and
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
a
partial
\psi
-squeezing of the second kind
.

(3)

If
\psi
is a maximal partial peeling of the second kind then it is called
peeling of the second kind
and
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
is called a
squeezing of the second kind
.

(4)

If
\psi
is maximal then
\psi
(respectively,
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
) is called a
minimalization (respectively, almost minimalization)
of the second kind of
(X,D)
.

(5)

In the above case
(\psi_{\mathrm{am}}(X),(\psi_{\mathrm{am}})_{*}D)
is called an
almost minimal model of
(X,D)
of the second kind
.

Remark 3.32
.

By Corollary
3.3
if
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and
\psi
is a partial MMP run of the second kind then
\psi=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}
, where
\psi_{1}
is a partial MMP run (of the first kind) and
\psi_{2}
is log crepant partial MMP run of the second kind. By (
3.4
) it follows that an almost minimalization of the second kind is an extension of an almost minimalization of the first kind.

4.
Almost minimalization for reduced boundaries

We want to obtain a more explicit description of the process of almost minimalization for log surfaces whose boundary is
uniform
, that is, all coefficients of prime components are equal to some fixed number
r\in[0,1]
. Before we do this we need to review the well-known case
r=1
in detail.

4A.
Discriminants and log canonical subdivisors

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and
D
a reduced divisor on
X
. We introduce or recall (see
[
Fuj82
]
,
[
Miy01
]
) some notions and notation related to the geometry of divisors and specific subdivisors, which will be used later to discuss coefficient divisors and peeling morphisms. We do not assume that
D
is a simple normal crossing divisor. We denote the number of components of
D
by
\#D
.

By
p_{a}(D)
we denote the arithmetic genus of
D
, that is,
p_{a}(D):=\frac{1}{2}D\cdot(K_{X}+D)+1
. We have

p_{a}(D_{1}+D_{2})=p_{a}(D_{1})+p_{a}(D_{2})+D_{1}\cdot D_{2}-1

for any divisors
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
on
X
. Given a reduced subdivisor
T\leqslant D
we call

(4.1)

\beta_{D}(T)=T\cdot(D-T)

the
branching number of
T
in
D
. A
tip of
D
is a component with
\beta_{D}\leqslant 1
and a
branching component of
D
is a component with
\beta_{D}\geqslant 3
. We say that
D
is
rational
if all its components are rational. A component of
D
is
admissible
if it is smooth rational and its self-intersection number is at most
(-2)
.

Let
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
be the components of
D
. We put
Q(D)=[D_{i}\cdot D_{j}]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}
and we call

d(D):=\det(-Q(D)),

where
d(0):=1
, the
discriminant of
D
; it does not depend on the chosen order of components.

Lemma 4.1

(Splitting formula for discriminants)
.

Assume
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
are two reduced divisors on a smooth projective surface which have no common component and which intersect in unique components
T_{1}\leqslant D_{1}
and
T_{2}\leqslant D_{2}
, respectively. Then

(4.2)

d(D_{1}+D_{2})=d(D_{1})d(D_{2})-(T_{1}\cdot T_{2})d(D_{1}-T_{1})d(D_{2}-T_{2}).

The proof follows from the additivity of the determinant function with respect to column addition and its behavior on block-triangular matrices.

∎

In particular, if
D
is a reduced divisor with a tip
D_{1}
and this tip meets a component
D_{2}\leqslant D
then

(4.3)

d(D)=(-D_{1}^{2})d(D-D_{1})-d(D-D_{1}-D_{2}).

If
\operatorname{Supp}D
is connected then we call
D
a
rational tree
if
p_{a}(D)=0
and a we call
D
a
rational cycle
if
p_{a}(D)=1
,
D
has no branching component and is not a smooth elliptic curve. A rational tree is simply a rational snc-divisor with a connected support and with no rational cycle as a subdivisor. Each component of a reducible rational cycle is rational and has
\beta_{D}=2
. A rational cycle is
degenerate
if it is not snc. In this case it is a rational curve with a unique singular point - a node or a simple cusp, a sum of two smooth rational curves intersecting at a unique point with multiplicity two or a triple of smooth rational curves passing through a common point, each two meeting normally, see Figure
2
.

Figure 2
.
Degenerate rational cycles.

A rational tree with no branching component is a
rational chain
. If
D
is a rational chain or a rational cycle, and the components are ordered so that
D_{i}
meets
D_{i+1}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, then we write
D=[-D_{1}^{2},\ldots,-D_{n}^{2}]
in the first case and
D=((-D_{1}^{2},\ldots,-D_{n}^{2}))
in the second case. A sequence consisting of an integer
r
repeated
k
times will be abbreviated by
(r)_{k}
. A rational chain and a rational cycle are
admissible
if they have admissible components and are negative definite. For chains the second condition is in fact redundant and for reducible rational cycles it is equivalent to one of the inequalities
-D_{i}^{2}\geqslant 2
being strict.

A component
T
of
D
is called
superfluous
if it is a
(-1)
-curve meeting at most two other components of
D
, each at most once. Equivalently, after the contraction of
T
the image of
T
is a simple normal crossing point of the image of
D
. Note that a log smooth completion of a smooth quasi-projective surface is
minimal
(does not dominate non-trivially some other log smooth completion) if and only if the boundary contains no superfluous component which is not its connected component.

A rational chain
T
is
ordered
if it has a fixed ordering on its components
T^{(1)},\dots,T^{(m)}
such that
T^{(i)}\cdot T^{(i+1)}=1
for
i\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}
. By
T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}
we denote the same chain with the opposite order of components. The
tip
(a
first tip
, if there is any chance of confusion) of a nonzero ordered chain is by definition
\mathrm{tip}(T)=T^{(1)}
. For
i\geqslant 1
we put

(4.4)

d^{(i)}(T)=d(T^{(i+1)}+\dots+T^{(m)})\quad\text{and}\quad d_{(i)}(T)=d(T^{(1)}+\dots+T^{(i-1)})

with
d^{(i)}(0)=d_{(i)}(0)=0
. We put also
d^{\prime}(T)=d^{(1)}(T)=d(T-\mathrm{tip}(T))
.
Let
T
be an admissible ordered chain. By Lemma
4.1

(4.5)

d(T)=(-\mathrm{tip}(T))^{2}d^{\prime}(T)-d^{\prime}(T-\mathrm{tip}(T)).

We have
0\leqslant d^{\prime}(T)<d(T)
and
\gcd(d(T),d^{\prime}(T))=1
, see
[
Fuj82
, 3.5]
.
We put

(4.6)

\delta(T)=\frac{1}{d(T)}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1]\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{ind}(T)=\frac{d^{\prime}(T)}{d(T)}\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1)

and we call
\operatorname{ind}(T)
the
inductance of
T
.

Given a reduced divisor
D
we now define some specific subdivisors
T\leqslant D
and depict their
extended dual graphs
(see
[
KM98
, Definition 4.6]
). White graph vertices correspond to components of
T
and their weights (if displayed) are negatives of their self-intersection numbers. Black vertices represent
D-T
(they are not necessarily distinct). The number of edges between two vertices is the intersection number of the represented components, see Fig.
4
.

A nonzero rational chain
T\leqslant D
whose all components are non-branching in
D
, that is
\beta_{D}(T)\leqslant 2
, is called a (rational)
twig of
D
if some component of
T
is a tip of
D
(
\beta_{D}\leqslant 1
) and a
segment of
D
otherwise. A segment is
non-degenerate
if
T
meets
D
normally (this holds for instance if
T
has at least three components), for degenerate segments see Figure
3
.

Figure 3
.
Degenerate segments. Thick lines indicate their components.

A twig is a
maximal twig
of
D
if it is not properly contained in another twig of
D
in the sense of supports. A twig whose support is a connected component of
D
is called a
rod
of
D
. A twig which is not a rod comes (and will be considered) with a unique order in which
\mathrm{tip}(T)
is a tip of
D
.

Let
F
be a rational tree with a unique branching component
B
and three maximal twigs
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
. Then we call
F
a
(rational) fork
, we write
F=\langle B;T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}\rangle
and we say that
F
is of type
(-B^{2};d(T_{1}),d(T_{2}),d(T_{3}))
. We put

(4.7)

\delta(F):=\delta(T_{1})+\delta(T_{2})+\delta(T_{3}).

By a
(-2)
-fork (respectively, a
(-2)
-chain)
we mean an fork (respectively, a chain) consisting of
(-2)
-curves. By a
fork of
D
we mean a fork which is a connected components of
D
. By Lemma
4.1

(4.8)

d(F)=d(T_{1})d(T_{2})d(T_{3})(-B^{2}-\operatorname{ind}(T_{1}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})-\operatorname{ind}(T_{2}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})-\operatorname{ind}(T_{3}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})).

A fork
F
with admissible components is called an
admissible fork
if
\delta(F)>1
and is called a
log canonical fork
if
\delta(F)=1
and not all components of
F
are
(-2)
-curves.

A rational tree
T\leqslant D
is called a
bench of
D
if
T
is a connected component of
D
which contains a chain (called a
central chain
)
C=C_{1}+\ldots+C_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
with tips
C_{1}
,
C_{n}
such that
T-C=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4}
,
T_{i}=[2]
for
i=1,2,3,4
,
T_{i}\cdot C_{1}=1
for
i=1,2
and
T_{i}\cdot C_{n}=1
for
i=3,4
. A bench is
log canonical
if the central chain is admissible and does not consist of
(-2)
-curves only.

A rational tree
T\leqslant D
is called a
half-bench of
D
if
T
contains a chain (again called a
central chain
)
C=C_{1}+\ldots+C_{n}
,
n\geqslant 1
with tips
C_{1}
,
C_{n}
such that
T-C=T_{1}+T_{2}
,
T_{i}=[2]
,
T_{i}\cdot C_{1}=1
for
i=1,2
and
T\cdot(D-T)=C_{n}\cdot(D-T)=1
. In particular, a half-bench of
D
is a fork of type
(b;2,2,t)
or a chain
[2,b,2]
for some
b,t\geqslant 2
. We call it a
log canonical
half-bench if
C
is admissible.

Twig

(a)
