.
Log canonical subdivisors.

Remark 4.2
.

(1)

For every admissible chain
T
we have
\delta(T)+\operatorname{ind}(T)\leqslant 1
with the equality for a
(-2)
-chain only. Hence for a fork
F
with admissible twigs and
\delta(F)>1
we infer from (
4.8
) and from Sylvester’s criterion that
F
is negative definite if and only if
F
is admissible (equivalently
-B^{2}\geqslant 2
).

(2)

If a fork with
\delta=1
or a bench consists of
(-2)
-curves then its discriminant vanishes. By (
4.8
) and by elementary properties of determinants it follows that a fork with admissible twigs and
\delta=1
is log canonical (equivalently, is not a
(-2)
-fork) if and only if it is negative definite. Similarly, a bench with an admissible central chain is admissible (equivalently, its central chain is not a
(-2)
-chain) if and only if it is negative definite.

Lemma 4.3

(Log terminal and log canonical subdivisors)
.

Let
p\in\overline{X}
be a germ of a normal singular surface and let
\overline{D}
be a reduced divisor on
\overline{X}
. Let
E
be the exceptional divisor of a minimal resolution
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
. Put
D=\pi_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+E
. We have
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(\pi);\overline{X},\overline{D})<1
(see Section
2B
) if and only if one of the following holds:

(1)

\overline{D}=0
and
E
is either an admissible fork or an admissible rod of
D
,

(2)

\overline{D}\neq 0
and
E
is an admissible twig of
D
.

We have
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(\pi);\overline{X},\overline{D})=1
if and only if one of the following holds:

(3)

\overline{D}=0
and
E
is one of the following:

(a)

a smooth elliptic curve with negative self-intersection number,

(b)

a (possibly degenerate) admissible rational cycle,

(c)

a log canonical fork,

(d)

a log canonical bench.

(4)

\overline{D}\neq 0
,
E\cdot(D-E)=1
and
E
is a log canonical half-bench of
D
.

(5)

\overline{D}\neq 0
,
E\cdot(D-E)=2
and
E
is a segment of
D
(possibly degenerate).

Conversely, each nonzero divisor
E
as above has a negative definite intersection matrix and in cases other than (3a) and (3b) it contracts to a rational singularity.

A direct arithmetic proof independent of the characteristic of the base field is given in
[
Kol92
, 3.2.7, 3.4.1]
; cf.
[
Kol13
, 3.39, 3.40]
. Since we do not assume that
p\in(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is log canonical (we consider a minimal resolution, not a minimal log resolution), in (3b) we allow degenerate cycles; necessary minor corrections of the arguments in
[
Kol92
, (3.1.4), (3.1.5)]
can be done easily. Concerning the rationality of log canonical singularities see also
[
Art62
, 2.3]
and
[
Kol13
, 2.28]
.

∎

Let
{k}
be the base field. In case
\operatorname{char}{k}=0
log terminal singularities are locally analytically isomorphic to quotient singularities, that is, the ones obtained as quotients of
\mathbb{A}^{2}
by the actions of finite subgroups of
\operatorname{GL}(2,{k})
, see
[
Bri68
, Satz 2.10]
, cf.
[
Ish14
, §7.4]
. For instance, for an admissible chain
T
, the action of the cyclic group
\langle\zeta\rangle<({k}^{*},1)
of order
d(T)
given by
\zeta\cdot(x,y)=(\zeta x,\zeta^{d^{\prime}(T)}y)
produces a singularity with
T
as an exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution, see
[
BH+04
, III.5]
.

4B.
Comparing graphs of log terminal singularities

Let
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a minimal resolution of a germ of a log surface
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
at some point
p
. The associated weighted decorated dual graph
\mathcal{G}(\overline{X},\overline{D},p)
is the dual graph of the exceptional divisor
E
of
\pi
with weights of its vertices being negatives of the self-intersection numbers of the corresponding components of
E
and decorations of vertices being equal to the intersections of these components with the proper transform of
\overline{D}
. Such a graph is called
du Val
if and only if
\overline{D}=0
and the germ is canonical. On the set of vertices of this graph we have also the
\mathbb{Q}
-valued function
\operatorname{cf}_{\mathcal{G}}
computing coefficients of the corresponding components of
E
over
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. By a weighted decorated subgraph of
\mathcal{G}(\overline{X},\overline{D},p)
we mean a subgraph od the dual graph of
E
with weights and decorations not bigger than the ones of the original graph.

Lemma 4.4

(Alexeev)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be the weighted decorated dual graphs of two germs of log terminal log surfaces. If
\mathcal{F}
is a subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
not equal to
\mathcal{G}
then
\operatorname{cf}_{\mathcal{F}}<\operatorname{cf}_{\mathcal{G}}|_{\mathcal{F}}
, unless
\mathcal{G}
is du Val, in which case
\operatorname{cf}_{\mathcal{F}}=\operatorname{cf}_{\mathcal{G}}=0
.

See
[
Kol92
, 3.1.3]
and
[
Ale88
, 3.7(ii)-(iv)]
, cf. Lemma
7.6
in the Appendix (Section
7
).

∎

4C.
Barks

To write down explicit compact formulas for the coefficient divisor in case of uniform boundaries we use
barks
(cf.
[
Miy01
, 2.3.5.2]
). Let
T=T^{(1)}+\cdots+T^{(m)}
be an admissible ordered chain. We define the
bark of
T
as (see (
4.4
))

(4.9)

\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}T=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{d^{(i)}(T)}{d(T)}T^{(i)}.

In particular, the coefficients of
\mathrm{tip}(T)
and
T^{(m)}
in
\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}T
are
\operatorname{ind}(T)
and
\delta(T)
, respectively. We put

(4.10)

\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T=\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}(T^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}).

Denoting by
\delta_{i}^{j}
the Kronecker delta, from (
4.5
) we infer that

(4.11)

T^{(i)}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}T=-\delta_{i}^{1}.

Definition 4.5

(The bark)
.

Assume that
X
is a smooth surface and
D
is a reduced divisor on
X
.

(1)

If
E
is a maximal admissible twig of
D
but not a rod of
D
, we put
\operatorname{Bk}_{D}E=\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}E
.

(2)

If
E
is an admissible rod of
D
then we pick any order which makes it an ordered twig and we put
\operatorname{Bk}_{D}E=\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}E+\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}E
.

(3)

If
E
is an admissible fork of
D
we denote its maximal twigs by
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
,
T_{3}
and its central component by
E_{0}
and we put

\operatorname{Bk}_{D}E=u(E_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}T_{i},\quad\text{where}\quad u=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{3}\delta(T_{i})-1}{-E_{0}^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{ind}(T_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})}.

We extend the definition of
\operatorname{Bk}_{D}
additively for disjoint sums of admissible twigs, rods and forks. Finally, we define the
bark of
D
by
\operatorname{Bk}D:=\operatorname{Bk}_{D}(\operatorname{Exc}\pi)
, where
\pi
is the unique pure peeling morphism for
(X,D)
, see Lemma
4.7
.

Remark 4.6
.

In Definition
4.5
(3) we have
0<u\leqslant 1
. To see this note that
\operatorname{ind}(T_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})+\delta(T_{i})\leqslant 1
for
i=1,2,3
, which gives
-E_{0}^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{ind}(T_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})\geqslant-E_{0}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\delta(T_{i})-3\geqslant\sum_{i=1}^{3}\delta(T_{i})-1>0
.

4D.
Peeling, redundant and almost log exceptional curves

We now discuss notions related to almost minimality for reduced boundaries. Many results concerning minimal and almost minimal models of log surfaces with reduced boundary were obtained in the 80s in particular by T. Fujita, M. Miyanishi, S. Tsunoda and F. Sakai, see for instance
[
Fuj82
]
,
[
Fuj79
]
,
[
MT84
]
,
[
Sak84
]
. The results we present below are close to
[
Miy01
, 2.3.3-3.5]
. We do not claim originality, but the proposed formulation and line of reasoning will be used to obtain analogous results for
(1-r)
-log canonical surfaces with uniform boundary
rD
. For a definition of a peeling of the first and second kind see Definitions
3.14
(1) and
3.31
(2).

Lemma 4.7

(Peeling and squeezing for a reduced boundary)
.

Assume that
X
is a smooth surface and
D
is a reduced divisor on
X
.

(1)

A contraction of some number of admissible forks, admissible rods and admissible twigs of
D
is a pure partial peeling. Every pure partial peeling is of this type.

(2)

A contraction of some number of divisors
E
as in Lemma
4.3
(if exists) is a pure partial peeling of the second kind. Every pure partial peeling of the second kind is of this type.

(3)

L\leqslant D
is redundant of the first kind if and only if it is a
(-1)
-curve such that either
\beta_{D}(L)\leqslant 1
or
\beta_{D}(L)=2
and
L
meets some admissible twig of
D
.

(4)

L\leqslant D
is redundant of the second kind if and only if it is a
(-1)
-curve with
\beta_{D}(L)=2
which meets no admissible twig of
D
.

In particular, if
D
contains no superfluous
(-1)
-curve (is snc-minimal) then
(X,D)
is squeezed and if
(X,D)
is squeezed then it has a unique peeling.

(1), (2) If
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the second kind then it is also a minimal resolution of
\overline{X}
, hence
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is as in Lemma
4.3
. Conversely, let
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
denote the contraction as in (1) or (2). We may assume that
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is connected. By Lemma
4.3
for every component
E
of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
we have
\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},\overline{D})\leqslant 1=\operatorname{cf}(E;X,D)
and the inequality is strict in case (1). Since
X
is smooth,
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
has
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier components and
(X,D)
is GMRLC. Then
\alpha
is a partial MMP run of the second kind by Corollary
3.3
and it is of the first kind in case (1).

(3), (4) Assume that
L
is
\alpha
-redundant of the first or second kind for some pure partial peeling
\alpha
(of the first kind). It is a
(-1)
-curve, as
L\cdot K_{X}<0
. Denote by
E
the sum of connected components of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
meeting
L
. Since
X
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial and
\alpha
is a partial MMP (of the first kind),
\alpha(X)
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial. Let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
E+L
. Put
\overline{D}=f_{*}D
and
q=\sigma(E+L)
. If
E=0
then we get
0\geqslant L\cdot(K_{X}+D)=-2+\beta_{D}(L)
, which gives (3) or (4). We may thus assume that
E\neq 0
. By Lemma
2.11
coefficients of components of
E+L
with respect to
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
are smaller than
1
, hence by Corollary
3.3
(1)
\sigma
is a partial peeling of
D+L
. By (1)
D+L
has simple normal crossings in a neighborhood of
E+L
. In case
\overline{X}
is smooth we infer that
\overline{D}
has simple normal crossings at
q
. Assume that
\overline{X}
is singular. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the minimal resolution of singularities and let
\widetilde{D}
be the reduced total transform of
\overline{D}
. Then
\pi
is a pure partial peeling of
\widetilde{D}
and by (1)
\widetilde{D}
has simple normal crossings in a neighborhood of
\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. It follows that
L
contracts to an snc-point of the image of
D
, which means that
L
is a superfluous
(-1)
-curve. This gives (3).

Finally, suppose that
(X,D)
is squeezed and has more than one peeling morphism. By Corollary
3.5
there are connected components
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
of exceptional divisors of these peelings such that
E_{1}\nleq E_{2}
,
E_{2}\nleq E_{1}
and
E_{1}
meets
E_{2}
. In particular,
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
are not connected components of
D
. By (1)
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are admissible twigs of
D
, hence
(E_{1}+E_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
is an admissible rod of
D
. It follows that both peelings contract
(E_{1}+E_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
; a contradiction.

∎

Remark 4.8
.

Assume that
D
as above is connected and squeezed of the second kind (i.e., contains no
(-1)
-curve with
\beta_{D}\leqslant 2
). Then
(X,D)
has a unique peeling of the second kind except in the following cases:

(1)

D
is a bench consisting of
(-2)
-curves,

(2)

D
is a rational cycle (possibly degenerate) which either consists of
(-2)
-curves or is negative definite but non-contractible algebraically.

Suppose that
(X,D)
has at least two distinct peeling morphisms of the second kind. By Corollary
3.5
there are connected components
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
of their exceptional divisors such that
E_{1}\nleq E_{2}
,
E_{2}\nleq E_{1}
and
E=(E_{1}+E_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
is connected. In particular,
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
are not connected components of
D
. It follows that
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
are of type (2), (4) or (5) in Lemma
4.3
. Assume first that, say,
E_{2}
contains a non-nc point of
D
. Then it is of type (5) with
\#E_{2}\leqslant 2
and the non-nc point belongs to
E_{1}
, too. Then
D
is a degenerate rational cycle with two or three components. By the maximality of peelings
E
is not algebraically contractible, hence
D=E
. Since its components have self-intersection numbers at most
(-2)
,
D
is semi-negative definite, which is a special case of (2). We may thus assume that
E
contains only nc points of
D
. Since
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are of type (2), (4) or (5) of Lemma
4.3
,
E
is a chain, a fork of type
(b;2,2,n)
, a bench or a non-degenerate rational cycle, and its components have self-intersection numbers at most
(-2)
. By the maximality of peelings
E
is not algebraically contractible, so it is a bench or a rational cycle, hence
D=E
. This gives (1) or (2).

∎

The following lemma is a generalization of
[
Miy01
, Section 2.3.6-8]
. The original more computational proof is for
D
which is snc.

Lemma 4.9

(Almost log exceptional curves for a reduced boundary)
.

Assume that
X
is a smooth surface and
D
is a reduced divisor on
X
. Let
A
be an
\alpha
-almost log exceptional curve of the first or second kind, where
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling with exceptional divisor
E
. Then
A
is a
(-1)
-curve and one of the following holds.

(1)

A\cdot D\leqslant 1
and if
A
is of the first kind then the inequality is strict or the point of intersection belongs to some admissible twig, rod or fork of
D
,

(2)

A\cdot D=2
and there is a rod
E_{1}
of
D
meeting
A
once, in a tip of
D
. Moreover, if
A
meets another connected component
E_{2}
of
E
then one of the following holds:

(a)

E_{2}
is an admissible twig of
D
. It meets
A
in a tip of
D
or
E_{1}
consists of
(-2)
-curves.

(b)

E_{2}
is a rod of
D
.

Let
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a partial peeling morphism for which
\alpha(A)
is log exceptional of the first or second kind. Since
\alpha
is pure, by Corollary
2.12
we have
\alpha(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}\geqslant A\cdot K_{X}
. We have also
0\geqslant\alpha(A)\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})\geqslant\alpha(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}
. By Lemma
3.19
(1) we have
A\cdot K_{X}<0
. But
A^{2}<0
, so in each case
A
is a
(-1)
-curve. We may assume that
E+A
is connected. We may also assume that
E\neq 0
, as otherwise (1) holds. If
A\cdot D=1
and
A
is of the first kind then we have
\alpha(A)\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})<0=A\cdot(K_{X}+D)
, so the point of intersection of
A
and
D
belongs to
E
, which gives (1).

We may thus assume that
A\cdot D\geqslant 2
. Let
\overline{\gamma}\colon(\overline{X},\overline{D})\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
be the contraction of the log exceptional curve
\alpha(A)
and let
\alpha^{\prime}\colon(Y,B)\longrightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
be the minimal resolution of singularities. Put
E^{\prime}=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha^{\prime}
. Since
X
is smooth, we have a morphism of log surfaces
\gamma\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(Y,B)
, such that
\alpha^{\prime}\circ\gamma=\overline{\gamma}\circ\alpha
. In particular,
\gamma_{*}(E+A)=E^{\prime}
. By Corollary
2.13
,
(\overline{Y},\overline{B})
is log terminal, hence by Lemma
4.7
,
E^{\prime}=0
or
E^{\prime}
is an admissible twig, rod or fork of
B
. In particular, it contains no
(-1)
-curve, hence
\gamma
is a composition of successive contractions of
(-1)
-curves in
E+A
and its images. Moreover, since
B
is a sum of rational trees and is nc in a neighborhood of
E^{\prime}
, every
(-1)
-curve contracted by a blowdown being a factor of
\gamma
is superfluous in the respective image of
D
. It follows that
A\cdot D\leqslant 2
, hence
A\cdot D=2
.

Since
E^{\prime}
is a sum of rational trees, so is
E+A
, hence
A
meets each connected component of
E
at most once. Since
E^{\prime}
is a sum of admissible twigs, rods and forks of
B
, one of the connected components of
E
met by
A
, say
E_{1}
, is necessarily a rod of
D
and it meets
A
in a tip. Assume that
A
meets some other connected component of
E
, say
E_{2}
. If
E_{2}
is not a connected component of
D
then it is a twig of
D
such that
\gamma_{*}(E_{1}+A+E_{2})
is a twig of
B
, which gives (a). We may therefore assume that
E_{2}
is an admissible fork or rod of
D
. Since
\gamma_{*}(E_{1}+A+E_{2})
is an admissible rod or fork of
B
, we get (a) or (b).

∎

Remark 4.10

(Almost log exceptional vs log exceptional)
.

(1)

Let
A
be as in Lemma
4.9
. If
A\cdot D=0
then
A
is log exceptional. If
A\cdot D=1
then it is log exceptional of the second kind. If
A\cdot D=2
then we have
A\cdot(K_{X}+D)>0
.

(2)

In Lemma
4.9
(2) the fact that
A+\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is negative definite and
A
is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional (and hence that after the contraction of
\alpha(A)
all coefficients decrease) gives additional restrictions on the weights of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, see Example
4.11
.

Example 4.11

(An almost log exceptional curve)
.

Consider a smooth projective surface
X
with a rational chain
[n,1,m]
on it with
n\geqslant m\geqslant 2
. Let
A
be the middle
(-1)
-curve and let
D
be the sum of the other two components. The peeling morphism
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},0)
is simply the contraction of
D
. We see that
D+A
is contractible if and only if
n\geqslant 3
. The contraction of
A+D
in each case gives a log terminal singularity and the geometry is as in Lemma
4.9
(2b). But for
A
to be almost log exceptional additional conditions have to be met. Indeed, we have

\alpha(A)\cdot K_{\overline{X}}=A\cdot(K_{X}+D-\operatorname{Bk}D)=1-A\cdot\operatorname{Bk}D=1-\frac{2}{n}-\frac{2}{m},

so
A
is almost log exceptional of the first kind if and only if
m=2
and
n\geqslant 3
or
m=3
and
n\in\{3,4,5\}
and it is almost log exceptional of the second kind if and only if
(m,n)\in\{(3,6),(4,4)\}
.

5.
Almost minimalization for uniform boundaries

As follows from computations in Example
3.17
, to develop a reasonable general description of peeling morphisms and almost minimal models for a log surface for
(X,D)
it is better to add restrictions on the coefficients of
D
. We now study general
uniform
boundaries, that is, boundaries equal to
rD
, where
D
is a reduced divisor and
r\in\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]
. We call
r
simply the
coefficient
of the boundary. Coefficient
r=0
means that no boundary is considered, so there is no peeling and almost minimal is the same as minimal. Coefficient
r=1
was discussed in the previous section in case
X
is smooth. Below we assume that
r\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1]
.

5A.
Varying the boundary coefficient

Lemma 5.1

(Pure peeling varying with
r
)
.

Assume that
r<1
and
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, where
D
is reduced and
K_{X}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier, is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind. Let
r^{\prime}>r
. Then the following hold.

(1)

\alpha\colon(X,r^{\prime}D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r^{\prime}\overline{D})
is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind.

(2)

If
D
is connected and
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(\alpha);\overline{X},r\overline{D})\leqslant r
then
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(\alpha);\overline{X},r^{\prime}\overline{D})\leqslant r^{\prime}
and the inequality is strict, unless
\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}
and
\alpha^{*}\overline{D}=D
.

(3)

If
X
is smooth then every connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is a twig, rod or fork of
D
or it is a
(-2)
-segment of
D
(see Lemma
4.3
(5)).

Since
K_{X}
and
K_{X}+rD
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier,
K_{X}+r^{\prime}D
is
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Since
\alpha
is a peeling of the first or second kind, curves contracted by
\alpha
are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier.

(1) Since
\alpha
is a partial MMP run of the second kind, we have
K+rD\geqslant\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})
with the difference supported on
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. We write this as

(\tfrac{r^{\prime}}{r}-1)K_{X}+K_{X}+r^{\prime}D\geqslant(\tfrac{r^{\prime}}{r}-1)\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}+\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r^{\prime}\overline{D}),

hence

K_{X}+r^{\prime}D\geqslant(\tfrac{r^{\prime}}{r}-1)(\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-K_{X})+\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r^{\prime}\overline{D}).

Since
\alpha
is pure, by Corollary
2.12
we have
\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-K_{X}\geqslant 0
, so
K_{X}+r^{\prime}D\geqslant\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r^{\prime}\overline{D})
. Hence (1) follows form Corollary
3.3
.

(2) Assume that
\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},r^{\prime}\overline{D})=r^{\prime}
for some component
E
of
\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha)
. Then the above inequality shows that
E
is not contained in the support of
\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}-K_{X}
. Since
D
is connected, Corollary
2.12
gives
\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}
. Lemma
2.11
gives
\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r^{\prime}\overline{D})=(1-r^{\prime})\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
, hence
K_{X}+r^{\prime}D=\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r^{\prime}\overline{D})
and we obtain
\alpha^{*}\overline{D}=D
.

(3) Assume that
X
is smooth and
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is connected. Applying part (1) with
r^{\prime}=1
we see that the morphism
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of
(X,D)
of the second kind. If
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(\alpha);\overline{X},\overline{D})<1
then it is of the first kind and we are done by Lemma
4.3
. We may thus assume that
\operatorname{cf}(\mathcal{E}(\alpha);\overline{X},\overline{D})=1
. By (2)
\alpha^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{X}
and
\alpha^{*}\overline{D}=D
. Then
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
consists of
(-2)
-curves with
\beta_{D}=2
. Since
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is negative definite, it is a segment of
D
.

∎

Example 5.2
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log smooth surface with reduced boundary and let
T=[k]
for some
k\geqslant 2
be a tip of
D
with
\beta_{D}(T)=1
. By adjunction we have
T\cdot(K_{X}+rD)=k-2+r(-k+1)
, so the contraction of
T
is a partial peeling of the first (second) kind of
(X,rD)
if and only if
r>1-\frac{1}{k-1}
(respectively,
\geqslant
). For instance, if
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
then the contraction of
T
is a partial peeling of the first kind if and only if
T=[2]
.

A peeling of the first (second) kind can be decomposed as a squeezing of the first (second) kind followed by a pure peeling, see Corollary
3.11
(1). The part consisting of squeezing will be studied in the next section. As for the part consisting of a pure peeling the following corollary together with Lemma
5.4
give an explicit description of the coefficient divisor.

Corollary 5.3

(Uniqueness of peeling for squeezed surfaces)
.

Assume that
X
is a smooth surface,
D
is reduced and
(X,rD)
is squeezed. Then
(X,rD)
has a unique (pure) peeling.

Suppose that
(X,rD)
has more than one peeling morphism. There are connected components
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
of exceptional divisors of these peelings such that
E_{1}\nleq E_{2}
,
E_{2}\nleq E_{1}
and
E_{1}
meets
E_{2}
. By Corollary
3.5
we see that
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
are not connected components of
D
. By Lemma
5.1

E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are admissible twigs of
D
, hence
(E_{1}+E_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
is an admissible rod of
D
.

To reach a contradiction we argue that there exists a peeling contracting
(E_{1}+E_{2})_{\mathrm{red}}
. Let
C
be some common component of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. Write the rod as
T_{1}+C+T_{2}
, where
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are disjoint admissible twigs meeting
C
, contained in
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively. We may assume that
D=T_{1}+C+T_{2}
,
E_{1}=C+T_{1}
and
E_{2}=T_{2}+C
. Let
p_{1}\colon X\longrightarrow Y_{1}
be the contraction of
T_{1}
and
p_{2}\colon Y_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{2}
the contraction of
p_{1}(T_{2})
. Put
C_{1}=p_{1}(C)
and
C_{2}=p_{2}(C_{1})
. It is sufficient to show that
C_{2}
is log exceptional on
(Y_{2},rC_{2})
. Since
p_{2}
is a partial MMP run, we have
p_{2}^{*}(K_{Y_{2}}+rC_{2})=K_{Y_{1}}+r(C_{1}+p_{1}(T_{2}))-A
for some
A\geqslant 0
with
(p_{2})_{*}A=0
. Since
C_{1}
is log exceptional, we get
C_{2}\cdot(K_{Y_{2}}+rC_{2})\leqslant C_{1}\cdot(K_{Y_{1}}+r(C_{1}+p_{1}(T_{2})))<0
. Since
T_{1}+C+T_{2}
is an admissible rod, it is negative definite, which gives
C_{2}^{2}<0
.

∎

5B.
Formulas for the coefficient divisor

Lemma 5.4

(Coefficients for a uniform boundary)
.

Assume that
X
is smooth surface and
D
is a reduced divisor. Let
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a contraction of (some) admissible twigs, rods and forks of
D
. Denote by
T
the sum of connected components of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
which are twigs but not rods of
D
. Then (see Definition
4.5
)

(5.1)

\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha-\operatorname{Bk}_{D}(\operatorname{Exc}\alpha)-(1-r)\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T.

Assume that
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of
(X,rD)
. Then the coefficients of the divisor
\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
belong to
[0,r)
. Moreover,
\operatorname{cf}(U;\overline{X},r\overline{D})=0
for a component
U
or
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
if and only if the connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
containing
U
consists of
(-2)
-curves and is an admissible rod or an admissible fork of
D
.

Assume first that
r=1
. The coefficient divisor is uniquely determined by the equations
U\cdot(K+D-E+\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},\overline{D}))=0
where
U
runs through components of
E:=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. We may assume that
E
is connected. By (
4.11
) in cases (1) and (2) of Definition
4.5
the equations hold for
\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}E
and
\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}E+\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}E
, respectively. In case (3) we see that the divisors
E_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T_{i}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\operatorname{Bk}^{\prime}T_{i}
intersect trivially with all components of
T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}
. Then (
5.1
) follows from the equation
E_{0}\cdot\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},\overline{D})=-E_{0}\cdot(K+D-E)=0
. For
r\neq 1
we need to show that

K_{X}+r\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+E=\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})+\operatorname{Bk}_{D}(E)+(1-r)\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T.

Subtracting the equality for
r=1
and dividing by
1-r
we see that it is sufficient to prove that

(5.2)

\alpha^{*}\overline{D}=\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T,

and hence that
\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T
intersects trivially with every component
U
of
T
. To see this we may assume that
T
is a single admissible twig with components
T^{(i)}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
and
U=T^{(i)}
for some
i
. Then
U\cdot(\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T)=\delta_{i}^{m}+U\cdot\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T=0
by (
4.11
).

Assume that
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling for
(X,rD)
. Then
\operatorname{cf}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})<r
by Corollary
2.13
. Put
G=E-\operatorname{Bk}_{D}(E)+(1-r)\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T
. For each component
E_{j}
of
E
we have

0=E_{j}\cdot\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=E_{j}\cdot(K_{X}+r\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+G)\geqslant E_{j}\cdot G,

because
\alpha
is pure. By Lemma
2.10
we see that
G=0
or
G\geqslant 0
and
\operatorname{Supp}G=\operatorname{Supp}E
. The case
G=0
happens when
E_{j}\cdot K_{X}=E_{j}\cdot\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}=0
for each
j
, that is, when
G
is an admissible rod or fork of
D
consisting of
(-2)
-curves only.

∎

Corollary 5.5

(Characterization of almost log exceptional curves)
.

Assume that
X
is a smooth surface,
D
is a reduced divisor and
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is a pure partial peeling. Then
A\nleq D
is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional of the first (second) kind if and only if
A
is a
(-1)
-curve such that the following hold:

(1)

A+\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is negative definite,

(2)

A\cdot(r\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D}))<1
(
=1
).

Put
\overline{A}=\alpha(A)
. Clearly, (1) is equivalent to
\overline{A}^{2}<0
. We may assume that (1) holds. We have
\overline{A}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=A\cdot(K_{X}+\widetilde{D})
, where
\widetilde{D}=r\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, so we only need to show that if
A
is almost log exceptional of the first or second kind then it is a
(-1)
-curve. By Lemma
3.19
(1)
A\cdot K_{X}<0
. Since
X
is smooth and
A^{2}<0
,
A
is a
(-1)
-curve.

∎

5C.
Redundant and almost log exceptional curves for uniform boundaries

The following characterization is used in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Lemma 5.6

(Redundant curves)
.

Let
(X,rD)
be a log surface with
X
smooth,
D
reduced,
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
be a contraction some admissible twigs
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
of
D
meeting some
(-1)
-curve
{l}\leqslant D
. Put
\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}
,
\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{ind}(T_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})
and
E=\sum_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}
. Then
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the first or second kind if and only if the following inequalities hold

(5.3)

\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}<1,

(5.4)

r\beta_{D-E}({l})\leqslant 2-k+\delta-(1-r)(1-\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}),

where the equality in (
5.4
) holds exactly when
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind.

Put
\overline{{l}}=\alpha_{*}{l}
,
R=\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}-{l}
,
\delta_{i}=\delta(T_{i})=1/d(T_{i})
and
\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}_{i}=\operatorname{ind}(T_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})
. By (
4.11
) we have
\alpha^{*}\overline{{l}}={l}+\sum_{i}\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T_{i}
. We compute
\overline{{l}}^{2}={l}\cdot({l}+\sum_{i}\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T_{i})=-1+\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}},
and

\overline{{l}}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})={l}\cdot\alpha^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+\overline{D})+(r-1){l}\cdot\alpha^{*}\overline{D}={l}\cdot(K_{X}+R+{l}+\sum_{i}(T_{i}-\operatorname{Bk}T_{i}))+

+(r-1){l}\cdot(R+{l}+\sum_{i}\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T_{i})=-2+{l}\cdot R+\sum_{i}(1-\delta_{i})+(r-1)({l}\cdot R-1+\sum_{i}\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}_{i})=

=k-\delta-1+r({l}\cdot R-1)+(r-1)\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}.

It follows that
\overline{{l}}
is log exceptional of the first or second kind if and only if
\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}<1
and
r{l}\cdot R\leqslant 2-k+\delta-(1-r)(1-\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})
, with the equality for the second kind only.

∎

Recall that a divisor on a smooth surface
contracts to a smooth point
if its support is the support of the exceptional divisor of a birational contraction onto a smooth surface. Since a blowup does not change the discriminant, such a divisor is necessarily a rational tree whose discriminant is equal to
1
.

Proposition 5.7

(Description of redundant components)
.

Let
(X,rD)
be a log surface with
X
smooth,
D
reduced,
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\alpha
be a pure partial peeling of
(X,rD)
of the second kind and
{l}\leqslant D
a
(-1)
-curve such that
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the first (second) kind. Denote by
E
the sum of connected components of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
meeting
{l}
and put
R=D-{l}-E
. Then one of the following holds (see Figure
5
).

(1)

r=1
and
\beta_{D}({l})\leqslant 2
, so
{l}
is log exceptional of the first or second kind. If the intersection of
E
and
D-E
is not normal then
E
is a degenerate segment of
D
, so
\#E\leqslant 2
.

(2)

r\neq 1
,
{l}
is log exceptional of the first (second) kind and one of the following holds:

(a)

{l}+E
is a rational chain which contracts to a smooth point. If
E\cdot R\neq 0
then
\alpha
is log crepant, either
E
is a
(-2)
-segment of
D
or
r=\frac{1}{2}
,
{l}
is of the second kind and
{l}+E=[2,1,3]
.

(b)

{l}+E
is a rod or a twig of
D
, and it does not contract to a smooth point.

(3)

r=\frac{1}{2}
,
\beta_{D}({l})=3
,
E\cdot R=0
and
{l}+E
is one of
[3,1,3]
,
[1,(2)_{m-1},3]
,
m\geqslant 1
,
[3,1,2,3]
. In particular, all components of
{l}+E
are log exceptional of the second kind.

(4)

r=\frac{2}{3}
,
\beta_{D}({l})=3
,
E\cdot R=0
and
{l}+E
is one of
[2,1,4]
,
[2,1,3,2]
. In particular,
\alpha
is not of the first kind and
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind.

(5)

r\neq 1
and
E
is a
(-2)
-twig such that
0\leqslant{l}\cdot R-\frac{1}{r}\leqslant\frac{1}{\#E+1}
.

(6)

E
is a sum of two twigs of
D
,
[2]
and
[3]
,
{l}\cdot R=1
and
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{4}{5}
.

Moreover,
{l}\cdot E\leqslant 2
, and the divisor
{l}+E
is snc, unless
r=1
and
{l}+E
is a degenerate cycle.

In cases (5) and (6) the inequalities on the right (respectively, left) become equalities if and only if
\alpha({l})
(respectively,
{l}
) is log exceptional of the second kind.

(3),
r=\frac{1}{2}

(a)

*

[.8]

-3
-3
-3
-2
-3
\vdots
[(2)_{m-1}]
-3

(4),
r=\frac{2}{3}

(b)

*

[.5]

-2
-4
-2
-3
-2

(6),
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{4}{5}

(c)

*

[.3]

-2
-3

Figure 5
.
Proposition
5.7
, cases (3), (4), (6). Thick line indicates
R=D-E-{l}
, dashed line is
{l}
.

If
E=0
then
{l}
is log exceptional of the first (second) kind, so we have
r\beta_{D}({l})\leqslant r+1
, which is a subcase of (1) or (2a). We may therefore assume that
E\neq 0
, hence
r\neq 0
. To avoid confusion below we refer to the cases of Lemma
4.3
as (L1)-(L5). By Lemma
5.1

\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the second kind of
D
, and
E
is as in (L2), (L4) or (L5). Moreover, since
E+{l}
is negative definite, in case (L4)
E
does not consist of
(-2)
-curves and in case (L5) it is not a degenerate
(-2)
-segment, so
r=1
.

Consider the case
r=1
. By Lemma
3.25
we have
\alpha=\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}
, where
\alpha_{1}
is a peeling of the first kind and
\alpha_{2}
is log crepant. It follows that
{l}
is
\alpha_{1}
-redundant, so
\beta_{D}({l})\leqslant 2
by Lemma
4.7
. If the intersection of
E
and
D-E
is not normal then
E
is a degenerate segment of
D
, which gives (1).

We may further assume that
r<1
. Denote the contraction of
\alpha({l})
by
\sigma\colon\alpha(X)\longrightarrow\overline{X}
. Put
\overline{D}=\sigma_{*}\alpha_{*}D
and
q=\sigma(\alpha({l}))\in\overline{X}
.

Assume that
q\in\overline{X}
is singular. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the minimal resolution of singularities. Since
X
is smooth, we get a factorization
\sigma\circ\alpha=\pi\circ\varphi
. Put
\widetilde{D}=\varphi_{*}D
and
\widetilde{E}=\varphi_{*}E
.

(5.5)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Since
\alpha
is a partial peeling of
rD
of the second kind, Corollary
3.5
(2) implies that
\pi
is a partial peeling of
r\widetilde{D}
of the second kind. Since
\pi
is minimal, it is pure, hence
\widetilde{E}
is as in Lemma
4.3
. By Lemma
5.1

\widetilde{E}
is a sum of admissible twigs, rods, forks and
(-2)
-segments of
\widetilde{D}
. If
\beta_{D}({l})\leqslant 2
then, since
E\neq 0
,
{l}
is superfluous in
D
(hence log exceptional of the first kind on
(X,rD)
), which gives (2b). We are left with case
\beta_{D}({l})\geqslant 3
. Then
\varphi({l})
is not a point of simple normal crossings of
\widetilde{D}
, so
\widetilde{E}
is a degenerate
(-2)
-segment of
\widetilde{D}
. If
G
is a component of
E+{l}
not contracted by
\varphi
then, since
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{\widetilde{X}}
and
\pi^{*}\overline{D}=\widetilde{D}
, we have
\operatorname{cf}(G;\overline{X},r\overline{D})=\operatorname{cf}(G;\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})=r=\operatorname{cf}(G;X,rD)
. By Lemma
2.11
it follows in particular that
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind. The fact that
{l}
is not superfluous in
D
restricts possible types of blowing-ups constituting
\varphi^{-1}
. For
\#\widetilde{E}=2
we obtain
{l}+E=[3,1,3]
, which gives
\operatorname{cf}({l};\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})=3r-1
, so
r=\frac{1}{2}
and hence case (3) holds. Assume that
\#\widetilde{E}=1
. Then there exists
m\geqslant 1
such that
{l}+E=[1,(2)_{m-1},3]
or
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{m-2},3]
, where
[3,(2)_{-1},3]:=[4]
. We obtain
\operatorname{cf}({l};\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})=(2m+1)r-m
and
\operatorname{cf}({l};\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})=2m(2r-1)
, hence
r=\frac{1}{2}
and
r=\frac{2m}{4m-1}
, respectively. In the second case the coefficient of the component of the twig
T_{1}=[3,(2)_{m-2},3]
meeting
{l}
equals
u=\frac{2m+r(2m-1)}{4m}
, cf. formula (
5.1
). But since the contraction of
T_{1}
is a peeling of the second kind, we have
u\leqslant r
, hence
r\geqslant\frac{2m}{2m+1}
, which gives
m=1
and
r=\frac{2}{3}
. This gives part of (3) and (4).

We may therefore assume that
q\in\overline{X}
is smooth. By Lemma
4.3
and the above remarks each connected component of
E
is a twig of
D
or a
(-2)
-segment of
D
. By negative definiteness of
E+{l}
in the second case the segment meets
R
and there can be at most one such. Denote it by
E_{0}
. We work under the assumption that
E_{0}=0
and we comment on the case
E_{0}\neq 0
at the end of the proof.

Write
E=T_{1}+\ldots+T_{k}
, where
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
are admissible twigs of
D
and
k\geqslant 1
. It follows that
{l}\cdot E=k
and
E\cdot R=0
. Put
\delta=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}
and
\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{ind}(T_{i}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})
. Since
{l}
is superfluous in
E+{l}
, we have
k\in\{1,2\}
. Assume that
k=1
. Then
E=[(2)_{m}]
for some
m\geqslant 1
. In particular,
\operatorname{ind}(E^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})=1-\frac{1}{m+1}
. The inequality (
5.4
) gives
\beta_{D-E}({l})\leqslant\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{m+1}
, where the equality holds if
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind. Note that
{l}
is log exceptional of the first kind if and only if
\beta_{D}({l})<1+\frac{1}{r}
, that is, if
\beta_{D-E}({l})<\frac{1}{r}
. If the latter inequality holds we get (2a), otherwise we get (5).

We are left with the case
k=2
. Put
d_{i}=d(T_{i})
. We may and shall assume that
d_{1}\geqslant d_{2}
. Since
q\in\overline{X}
is smooth, we have
d({l}+E)=1
. By Lemma
4.1
we obtain
1=d({l}+E)=d_{1}d_{2}(1-\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}})
. Hence
\gcd(d_{1},d_{2})=1
and
1-\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}=\frac{1}{d_{1}d_{2}}>0
. The former implies that
d_{1}-1\geqslant d_{2}\geqslant 2
. By Lemma
5.6
we obtain

d_{1}d_{2}r\ell\cdot R\leqslant d_{1}+d_{2}-1+r.

We may assume that
{l}
is not log exceptional of the first kind, as otherwise we get (2a). This gives
0\leqslant{l}\cdot(K+rD)=-1+r({l}\cdot R+1)
. We obtain

(5.6)

\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R+1}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{d_{1}+d_{2}-1}{d_{1}d_{2}{l}\cdot R-1}.

It follows that
(d_{1}d_{2}{l}\cdot R-1)\leqslant(d_{1}+d_{2}-1)({l}\cdot R+1)
, hence
{l}\cdot R(d_{1}-1)(d_{2}-1)\leqslant d_{1}+d_{2}
. Since
d_{1}-1\geqslant d_{2}
, we obtain
{l}\cdot R(d_{2}-1)\leqslant 2
. Suppose that
{l}\cdot R=2
. Then
d_{2}=2
and
d_{1}\leqslant 4
, hence
d_{1}=3
. Negative definiteness of
{l}+E
implies that
T_{1}=[3]
. The inequality (
5.6
) gives
r\leqslant\frac{4}{11}
. But then
T_{1}\cdot(K+rD)=1-2r>0
, which contradicts the fact the
\alpha
is a peeling of the second kind.

Thus
{l}\cdot R=1
and the above inequality reads as

(5.7)

\frac{1}{2}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{d_{1}+d_{2}-1}{d_{1}d_{2}-1}.

It follows that
(d_{1}-2)(d_{2}-2)\leqslant 3
, hence
d_{2}\leqslant 3
and if
d_{2}=3
then
d_{1}\in\{4,5\}
. Assume that
d_{2}=3
. Since
{l}+E
contracts to a smooth point, we get
T_{2}=[3]
and
T_{1}=[3,2]
or
T_{2}=[2,2]
and
T_{1}=[4]
. But in the second case we get
r\leqslant\frac{6}{11}
, so the contraction of
T_{1}
is not a partial peeling of the second kind of
rD
. In the first case we have
r=\frac{1}{2}
, which gives the remaining part of (3).

Finally, assume that
d_{2}=2
. Since
{l}+E
contracts to a smooth point, we get
T_{1}=[(2)_{m-1},3]
for some
m\geqslant 1
. The coefficient of the
(-3)
-curve equals
u=\frac{m(r+1)}{2m+1}
. Since the contraction of
T_{1}
is a peeling of the second kind, we get
u\leqslant r
, hence
1-\frac{1}{m+1}\leqslant r
. Then (
5.7
) gives
\frac{m}{m+1}\leqslant\frac{2m+2}{4m+1}
, so
m\in\{1,2\}
. For
m=2
we get
r=\frac{2}{3}
, which gives the remaining part of (4). For
m=1
we get
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{4}{5}
, which gives (6).

Finally, consider the case when
E_{0}\neq 0
, that is,
E
has a (unique) connected component
E_{0}
which is a segment of
D
. Write
\alpha=\alpha_{0}\circ\alpha^{\prime}
, where
\alpha^{\prime}
is the contraction of
E^{\prime}:=E-E_{0}
. Since
\alpha_{0}
is log crepant,
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the first (second) kind if
\alpha^{\prime}({l})
is log exceptional of the first (second) kind. Put
R^{\prime}=D-{l}-E^{\prime}
. Then
R^{\prime}=R+E_{0}
. We apply the lemma to
(X,rD)
with
(\alpha^{\prime},E^{\prime},R^{\prime})
instead of
(\alpha,E,R)
and we make conclusions for
(\alpha,E,R)
. Due to the negative definiteness of
{l}+E
, the divisor
{l}+E^{\prime}
is as in case (2a) with
E^{\prime}=0
, (2b), (3) with
{l}+E^{\prime}=[1,3]
and
\#E_{0}=1
or (5) with
E^{\prime}=0
. In the first case
{l}+E
is as in case (2a) with
\alpha
being log crepant and
R\cdot E=1
. In the second case, since
E_{0}\cdot{l}\neq 0
,
{l}+E
is itself a twig of
D
, hence is as in (2b) or (2a). In the third case
{l}+E=[2,1,3]
and
R\cdot E=1
, which is part of case (2a). In the last case, namely, case (5) with
E^{\prime}=0
we have
{l}
which is log exceptional of the first (second) kind,
{l}+E=[1,2,2,\ldots,2]
and
\alpha
is log crepant, which is case (2a).

In cases (2)-(6) the divisor
{l}+E
is a rational chain. It follows that in all cases (1)-(6)
{l}\cdot E\leqslant 2
and
E
is snc. If
{l}+E
is not snc then we are in case (1) with
D=E
and
E
is a degenerate segment of
{l}+E
, hence
{l}+E
is a degenerate cycle.

∎

Proposition 5.8

(Description of almost log exceptional curves)
.

Assume that
X
is smooth,
D
is reduced and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Let
{l}\nleq D
be an
\alpha
-almost log exceptional curve of the first (second) kind for some pure partial peeling
\alpha
of
(X,rD)
of the first (second) kind. Let
E
be the sum of connected components of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
meeting
{l}
. Put
R=D-E
. Then one of the following holds (see Fig.
6
and
7
):

(1)

{l}
is superfluous in
D+{l}
,

(2)

{l}\cdot D=2
,
r=\frac{1}{2}
,
{l}+E=[1,(2)_{m-1},3]
,
m\geqslant 1
and
R
meets
E+{l}
at the point
{l}\cap E
. In particular, all components of
{l}+E
are log exceptional of the second kind.

(3)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
r=\frac{m}{2m+1}
,
{l}+E=[1,(2)_{m-1},3]
for some
m\geqslant 1
, and
R\cdot E=0
. In particular,
\alpha
is not of the first kind and
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind.

(4)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
E\cdot R=0
and
{l}+E
is one of
[3,1,3]
,
[2,1,4]
or
[2,1,3,(2)_{m-2},3]
for some
m\geqslant 2
. In the first case
r=\frac{1}{3}
and in the remaining cases
r=\frac{1}{2}
. In particular,
\alpha
is not of the first kind and
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind.

(5)

r<1
,
{l}
is log exceptional of the first (second) kind and
{l}+E
contracts to a smooth point.

(6)

0<r<1
,
{l}+E=[1,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
and
E
is a twig or a rod of
D
. Moreover,
\frac{1}{r}\leqslant{l}\cdot D\leqslant\frac{1}{r}+s
, where at least one of the inequalities is strict,
s=1
if
E
is a rod,
s=1-\frac{1}{k}
if
E
is a twig and
{l}
meets it in a tip of
D
and
s=\frac{1}{k}
otherwise.

(7)

{l}\cdot D=4
,
E\cdot R=0
,
r=\frac{1}{3}
,
{l}+E=[2,1,3]
,
\alpha
is not of the first kind and
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind.

(8)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
E\cdot R=0
,
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{k-1}]
and
\frac{1}{3}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{k+1}{2k+1}
for some
k\geqslant 1
.

(9)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
E\cdot R=1
,
\frac{k}{2k+1}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{2(k+1)}{3(2k+1)}
and
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\in\{1,2\}
, where
R
meets the rod
[2]
.

(10)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
E\cdot R=1
,
r=\frac{1}{2}
and
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{k-1}]
and for some
k\geqslant 1
, where
R
meets the last component of
[3,(2)_{k-1}]
. Moreover,
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind.

(11)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
E\cdot R=0
,
r=\frac{1}{2}
and
{l}+E=[3,1,2,3,2]
or
{l}+E=[4,1,2,2]
. Moreover,
\alpha({l})
is of the second kind.

(12)

{l}\cdot D=3
,
E\cdot R=0
,
\frac{2}{5}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{7}{15}
and
{l}+E=[3,1,2,3]
.

In cases (6), (8), (10) and (12) the inequalities on the right (respectively, left) become equalities if and only if
\alpha({l})
(respectively,
{l}
) is log exceptional of the second kind. In case (9) the inequality on right becomes an equality if and only if
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind.

(2),
r=\frac{1}{2}

Proposition
5.8
, cases (2)-(4), (7), (9)-(11);
\alpha({l})
of the second kind. Thick line indicates
