Figure 7
.
Proposition
5.8
, cases (1), (6), (8), (12);
{l}
not log exceptional. Thick line indicates
R=D-E
, dashed line is
{l}
.

Consider the case
r=1
. By Lemma
3.25
we have
\alpha=\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}
, where
\alpha_{1}
is a peeling of the first kind and
\alpha_{2}
is a log crepant peeling of the second kind. Then
{l}
is
\alpha_{1}
-almost log exceptional, so by Lemma
4.9
it is superfluous in
D+{l}
. Therefore, we may and will assume that
r<1
and that
{l}
is not superfluous in
D+{l}
. Let
\sigma\colon\alpha(X)\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
\alpha({l})
and
q\in\overline{X}
the image of
\alpha({l})
.

Assume that
q\in\overline{X}
is singular. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the minimal resolution of singularities and
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\widetilde{X}
the induced morphism. Put
\overline{D}=\sigma_{*}\alpha_{*}D
,
\widetilde{E}=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
, and
\widetilde{D}=\varphi_{*}D
; see diagram (
5.5
).
By Lemma
5.1
(3) every connected component of
E
is a twig, rod or fork of
D
or it is a
(-2)
-segment of
D
. Since
{l}
is not superfluous in
D+{l}
, the divisor
\widetilde{D}
is not snc at
\varphi({l})
. Since
\sigma\circ\alpha=\pi\circ\varphi
is a partial MMP run of the second kind,
\pi
is a partial peeling of the second kind by Corollary
3.5
. By Lemma
5.1

\widetilde{E}
is a degenerate
(-2)
-segment of
\widetilde{D}
, cf. Figure
3
. Let
G
be a component of
\varphi_{*}^{-1}\widetilde{E}
. Since
\pi^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{\widetilde{X}}
and
\pi^{*}\overline{D}=\widetilde{D}
, we have
\operatorname{cf}(G;\overline{X},r\overline{D})=\operatorname{cf}(G;\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})=r=\operatorname{cf}(G;X,rD)
. By Lemma
2.11
it follows that
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind. The fact that
{l}
is not superfluous in
D+{l}
restricts possible types of blowing-ups constituting
\varphi^{-1}
. If
\#\widetilde{E}=2
we get
{l}+E=[3,1,3]
and hence
r=\frac{1}{3}
. Assume that
\#\widetilde{E}=1
. Then
{l}+E=[1,(2)_{m-1},3]
or
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{m-2},3]
for some
m\geqslant 1
, where
[3,(2)_{-1},3]:=[4]
. In the first case we get
r=\frac{1}{2}
if
E
is a twig of
D
and
r=\frac{m}{2m+1}
if
E
is a rod of
D
. In the second case we get
r=\frac{1}{2}
. This gives (2), (3) and (4).

We may therefore assume that
q\in\overline{X}
is smooth. We may also assume that we are not in case (5), so
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+rD)>0
(respectively,
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+rD)\geqslant 0
). In particular,
E\neq 0
(hence
r\neq 0
) and
{l}\cdot D>\frac{1}{r}
(respectively,
{l}\cdot D\geqslant\frac{1}{r}
). Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the blowup of
q\in\overline{X}
, let
U=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
denote the exceptional component and
\varphi\colon X\longrightarrow\widetilde{X}
the induced morphism. Again, put
\overline{D}=\sigma_{*}\alpha_{*}D
,
\widetilde{E}=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
, and
\widetilde{D}=\varphi_{*}D
. We have
\varphi_{*}^{-1}U\leqslant E
, as otherwise we would have
\alpha(X)=\widetilde{X}
, which is impossible, as
\alpha
is pure. Put
\mu=\pi_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}\cdot U=\operatorname{mult}_{q}\overline{D}
and write
\varphi^{*}U=w{l}+\sum w_{i}E_{i}
, where the sum runs over all components
E_{i}
of
E
. Since
q\in\overline{X}
is smooth,
\mu
,
w
and all
w_{i}
are positive integers. We have
\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=K+r\pi_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+(r\mu-1)U
. Since
\sigma\circ\alpha
is a partial MMP run of the second kind,
\operatorname{cf}(U;\overline{X},r\overline{D})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(U,X,rD)=r
, hence
\mu\leqslant 1+\frac{1}{r}
. We have therefore

(5.8)

\mu-1\leqslant\frac{1}{r}\leqslant{l}\cdot D.

Moreover, at least one of the inequalities is strict. Indeed, otherwise
{l}
is log exceptional of the second kind and at the same time
\operatorname{cf}(U;\overline{X},r\overline{D})=\operatorname{cf}(U,X,rD)
, so by Lemma
2.11

\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the second kind, contrary to our assumptions. Put
R=D-E
. Since
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the first or second kind, we have
0\geqslant\alpha({l})\cdot(K_{\alpha(X)}+r\alpha_{*}D)={l}\cdot(K_{X}+E-\operatorname{Bk}E)+r{l}\cdot\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}(R)
, hence
{l}\cdot(E-\operatorname{Bk}E)+r{l}\cdot\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}(R)\leqslant 1
. We obtain

(5.9)

\frac{1}{r}{l}\cdot(E-\operatorname{Bk}E)+{l}\cdot\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}(R)\leqslant\frac{1}{r}\leqslant{l}\cdot D.

Again, at least one of the inequalities is strict.

Assume that
w=1
. Then
{l}+E=[1,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
, hence
\operatorname{Bk}E=E
. The divisor
E
is not a segment of
D
, as otherwise
\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}R=D
and we would have equalities in (
5.9
). Thus
E
is a twig or a rod of
D
. The inequalities (
5.9
) give additional restrictions on
{l}\cdot D
. We get (6).

We may now assume that
w\geqslant 2
. Assume that
\mu\geqslant 4
. Then
r\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
. For every component
U
of
E
we have
\operatorname{cf}(U,\overline{X},r\overline{D})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(X,rD)\leqslant r
. For a rod
U=[3]
of
D
have
\operatorname{cf}(U,\overline{X},\overline{D})=\frac{1}{3}
, so by Lemma
4.4
connected components of
E
consist of
(-2)
-curves or are equal to
[3]
, and in the latter case
r=\frac{1}{3}
and
\alpha
is not of the first kind. If
r\neq\frac{1}{3}
then due to negative definiteness of
{l}+E
we get
{l}+E=[1,2,\ldots,2]
, contrary to the assumption
w\geqslant 2
. Thus
r=\frac{1}{3}
and
\mu=4
. Since
w\geqslant 2
, it follows that
{l}+E=[2,1,3]
and
\alpha
is not of the first kind. The inequality (
5.9
) reads as
1+{l}\cdot\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}(R)\leqslant 3<{l}\cdot D
, which gives
{l}\cdot(\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}(R)-R)\leqslant 0
. This is possible only if
\alpha^{*}\alpha_{*}(R)=R
, that is,
R\cdot E=0
. Then
R\cdot w{l}=\mu=4
, so
{l}\cdot R=2
. This gives (7).

We may now assume that
\mu\leqslant 3
. Note that since
E+{l}
contracts to a smooth point,
{l}
is superfluous in
E+{l}
. But
{l}
is not superfluous in
D+{l}
, so we infer that
{l}\cdot R\geqslant 1
. We have
w\geqslant 2
and
\mu=R\cdot(w{l}+\sum w_{i}E_{i})\geqslant wR\cdot{l}+R\cdot E\geqslant 2R\cdot{l}+R\cdot E\geqslant 2
. It follows that
{l}\cdot R=1
and
\mu\geqslant w+R\cdot E
. Since
{l}
is not superfluous in
D
, the divisor
E
has two connected components. We obtain
{l}\cdot D=3
. Assume that
\mu=2
. Then
R\cdot E=0
and
w=2
, which gives
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
. The inequality (
5.9
) reads as
\frac{1}{r}(1-\frac{k+1}{2k+1})+1\leqslant\frac{1}{r}\leqslant 3
. This gives (8).

We are left with the case
\mu=3
. We have
{l}\cdot R=1
and
R\cdot\sum w_{i}E_{i}=3-w
. By (
5.8
)
\frac{1}{3}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Since
\sigma\circ\alpha
is a partial MMP run of the second kind, for every component
E_{i}
of
E
we have

(5.10)

\operatorname{cf}(E_{i};\alpha(X),r\alpha_{*}D)\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(E_{i};X,rD)=r.

Assume that
w=2
. Then again
{l}+E=[2,1,3,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
and
R\cdot E=1
. Let
E_{0}\leqslant E
be the
(-3)
-curve and
E_{1}=[2]
the
(-2)
-rod of
E
. If
R
meets
E_{1}
then by (
5.1
) we get
\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};\alpha(X),r\alpha_{*}D)=\frac{k}{2k+1}\leqslant r
. In this case by (
5.9
) we have
r\leqslant\frac{2k+2}{6k+3}
, which gives (9). If
R
meets
[3,(2)_{k-1}]
not in
E_{0}
then
k\geqslant 2
and the above inequalities give
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{k+1}{4k}
, which is impossible. Thus
R
meets
E_{0}
. The above inequalities imply that
r=\frac{1}{2}
and
k=1
, which gives (10). Finally, assume that
w=3
. Now
R\cdot E=0
. Since
E
has two connected components and
\mu=3
, the divisor
{l}+E
is a chain, either
[3,1,2,3]
or
[2,2,1,4,(2)_{k-1}]
or
[3,1,2,3,(2)_{k}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
. In the second and third case we obtain
k=1
and
r=\frac{1}{2}
, which gives (11). In the first case
\frac{2}{5}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{7}{15}
, which gives (12).

∎

5D.
Almost minimalization for uniform
\varepsilon
-dlt and
\varepsilon
-lc surfaces

For definitions of GMRLC,
\varepsilon
-dlt and
\varepsilon
-lc log surfaces see Definitions
2.1
and
2.7
. We recall that an
(1-r)
-lc log surface is in particular log canonical, hence GMRLC (for
r\neq 1
it is dlt, hence
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial). It follows that the canonical divisor, components of the boundary and all log exceptional curves of the first kind are
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier. Also, the image of a GMRLC log surface after a contraction of any log exceptional curve of the first or second kind is a GMRLC log surface.

Lemma 5.9
.

Assume that
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, where
\overline{D}
is reduced and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, is
(1-r)
-lc. Let
\alpha\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a proper birational morphism from a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial (normal) surface. Put
D=\alpha_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. Then
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is a partial peeling of the second kind. If
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is
(1-r)
-dlt then
\alpha
is of the first kind, unless
r=1
and some center of
\alpha
is a normal crossing point of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
.

Let
E
be a prime component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
. We have
\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},r\overline{D})\leqslant r=\operatorname{cf}(E;X,rD)
, hence
\alpha
is a partial peeling of the second kind by Corollary
3.3
(2). Assume that
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is
(1-r)
-dlt and that either
r<1
or
r=1
and
\alpha(E)
is not a normal crossing point of
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. It is sufficient to argue that
\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},r\overline{D})<r
, because then
\alpha
is of the first kind by Corollary
3.3
(1). Composing with a resolution of singularities of
X
we may assume that
\alpha
is a log resolution. If
r<1
then we are done by the definition of a
(1-r)
-dlt surface, cf. Remark
2.8
. Assume that
r=1
and write
\alpha=\pi\circ\alpha^{\prime}
, where
\pi
is the minimal log resolution of the singularity
\alpha(E)\in\overline{X}
. If
\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},r\overline{D})=1
then
\alpha^{\prime}
contracts
E
and by Lemma
2.11

\pi
is the identity, hence
\alpha(E)
is a normal crossing point on
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
.

∎

Remark 5.10
.

In Lemma
5.9
the log surface
(X,rD)
is not necessarily
(1-r)
-lc. For instance, for
r=0
consider a smooth projective surface containing the chain
[1,3,1]
. Let
X
be its image after the contraction of the middle curve. Then
X
is not canonical and the contraction of the remaining part of the chain maps it onto a smooth surface
\overline{X}
.

Corollary 5.11

(Resolutions of
(1-r)
-log canonical surfaces)
.

Let
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, where
\overline{D}
is reduced and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, be a germ of a
(1-r)
-lc log surface. Assume that
\overline{X}
is singular. Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
, where
D
is the reduced total transform of
\overline{D}
, be the minimal resolution. Then the exceptional divisor
E
is as in Lemma
4.3
and the following hold.

(1)

If
r=1
then
D
is snc or
D=E
and
E
is a nodal rational curve.

(2)

If
r<1
then
E
is as in case (1), (2) or (5) of Lemma
4.3
and in case (5)
E
consists of
(-2)
-curves.

(3)

Assume that
r<1
and
D
is not snc. Then
E=[(2)_{k}]
, where
k\in\{1,2\}
, is a degenerate segment and
D-E\neq 0
. Moreover, denoting the unique common point of
E
and
D-E
by
q
, we have:

(a)

k=2
,
q\in D-E
is smooth and
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.

(b)

k=1
,
q\in D-E
has multiplicity
2
and
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
,

(c)

k=1
,
q\in D-E
is smooth and
r\leqslant\frac{2}{3}
,

If
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt then the inequalities are strict.

(4)

(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-lc. It is
(1-r)
-dlt if
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is
(1-r)
-dlt.

The morphism
(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is a pure partial peeling of the second kind by Lemma
5.9
.

(1) If
r=1
and
D
is not snc then by Lemma
4.3

E
is a degenerate segment or a degenerate cycle, hence the minimal resolution is log crepant. For cases other than the rational nodal curve we easily find in the minimal log resolution curves with coefficients bigger than
1
. This gives (1).

(2), (3) Part (2) follows from Lemma
5.1
. The additional numerical restrictions in case (5) come from a straightforward computation of coefficients of exceptional divisors of the minimal log resolution dominating the minimal resolution. For instance, in the case when
q\in D-E
is a cusp, writing the exceptional divisor of the log resolution
\pi\colon(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
as
E_{0}+E_{1}+\ldots+E_{n}+E_{n+1}=[3,(2)_{n-2},3,1,2]
, where
n\geqslant 2
, we have

(5.11)

\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=K_{\widetilde{X}}+r\pi_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(i(2r-1)+r)E_{i}+2n(2r-1)E_{n}+n(2r-1)E_{n+1},

so the coefficients of exceptional components are not bigger than
r
if and only if
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
.

(4) We may assume that
D
is not snc, as otherwise the claim holds. If
r=1
then
D=E
is a nodal rational curve and if
r<1
then by Lemma
5.1
(3)
E
is a degenerate
(-2)
-segment of
D
. In both cases the minimal resolution is log crepant, so the claim holds.

∎

We discuss the effect of squeezing on log singularities for surfaces of type
(X,rD)
, with
X
smooth and
D
reduced. A partial squeezing is an almost minimalization of a partial peeling (which is a partial MMP run). Since partial squeezing itself is not a partial MMP run on
(X,rD)
, it does not necessarily respect
(1-r)
-(divisorial) log terminality. This is exactly the case in the following example.

Example 5.12

(Partial squeezing does not respect
(1-r)
-log terminality of
(X,rD)
)
.

Let
r\in(0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
be such that the interval
[\frac{1}{r},\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{n}]
contains some positive integer
N
. Blowing up over a point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
we create a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibered surface
X
with a unique degenerate fiber
F=[2,\ldots,2,1,n]
and a section
U
which is a
(-1)
-curve meeting a tip of
F
belonging to a maximal
(-2)
-chain
\Delta\subset F
. Let
\alpha\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
\Delta
and let
D=F_{1}+\ldots+F_{N}+U+\Delta
, where
F_{1},\ldots,F_{N}
are distinct non-degenerate fibers.

\textstyle{{\overset{0}{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\overset{0}{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\ldots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\overset{0}{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(N\text{\ vertices})}
\textstyle{{\underset{1}{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{2}{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\ldots}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\underset{2}{\circ}}}
\textstyle{(n\text{\ vertices})}

Figure 8
.
The dual graph of
D
in Example
5.12
.

Put
\overline{D}=\alpha_{*}D
and
\overline{U}=\alpha(U)
. Then
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is a pure peeling, hence
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is
(1-r)
-dlt. We have (cf. (
5.1
))

\overline{U}^{2}=-\frac{1}{n}<0\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{U}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=r(N-\frac{1}{n})-1\leqslant 0,

so
\overline{U}
is log exceptional of the first or second kind and hence
U
is
\alpha
-redundant of the first or second kind. Let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the contraction of
U
. Put
D^{\prime}=\sigma_{*}D
. Then
\operatorname{cf}(U;X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})=-1+r+rN\geqslant r
, so while
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt,
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is not. If
\frac{1}{r}<N<\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{n}
then
U
is
\alpha
-redundant of the first kind and
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is not even
(1-r)
-lc.

We note that although
\overline{U}
is log exceptional on
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
and
U
is redundant on
(X,rD)
, we have
\overline{U}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+r(\overline{D}-\overline{U}))=rN-1\geqslant 0
, so
\overline{U}
is not log exceptional on
(\overline{X},r(\overline{D}-\overline{U}))
and hence
U
is not almost log exceptional on
(X,r(D-U))
.

Example 5.13

(Example with
\psi=\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
)
.

Consider a log surface
(X,rD)
with
X
smooth,
r\in(0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
,
D
reduced containing a twig
E=[(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
, and containing a
(-1)
-curve
{l}
such that
{l}\cdot E=1
and for
R=D-{l}-E
we have
0\leqslant{l}\cdot R-\frac{1}{r}<\frac{1}{k}
; see case (5) of Proposition
5.7
. Let
\alpha
be the contraction of
E
, let
\psi
be the contraction of
E+{l}
and let
E_{1},\ldots,E_{k-1}
be the subsequent components of
E
enumerated so that
E_{1}
is a tip of
D
and
E_{k-1}
meets
{l}
.

{l}
-1
R

E_{k-1}
\vdots

[(2)_{k-1}]

E_{1}

Figure 9
.
The graph of
D
in Example
5.13
.

We have
{l}\cdot(K+rD)=-1+r{l}\cdot R\geqslant 0
, so
{l}
is not log exceptional of the first kind. On the other hand,
\alpha({l})\cdot(K_{\alpha(X)}+r\alpha_{*}D)=-1+r({l}\cdot R-\frac{1}{k})<0
, so
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the first kind, that is,
{l}
is
\alpha
-redundant on
(X,rD)
. Therefore, contracting subsequently
E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{k-1},{l}
we see that
\psi
is a partial MMP run, a partial peeling of
(X,rD)
. On the other hand, under this contraction the image of
{l}
is a smooth point of
\psi(X)
, hence
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}=\psi
, i.e.
\psi
is the almost minimalization morphism, too, namely a
K_{X}
-MMP run. The order of contractions witnessing the latter is, of course, the opposite one:
{l},E_{k-1},\ldots,E_{1}
.

Corollary 5.14

(The effect of squeezing on log singularities)
.

Assume that
X
is smooth,
D
is reduced,
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
and
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt (
(1-r)
-lc). Let
\sigma\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
be a partial squeezing of the first (second) kind. Then
X^{\prime}
is smooth and the following hold:

(1)

If
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
contains no redundant curve of the first kind meeting
\operatorname{Supp}\sigma_{*}(\operatorname{Exc}\sigma)
(in particular, if
\sigma
is a squeezing) then it is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc).

(2)

If
\frac{1}{r}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
then
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is
(1-r)
-lc.

Since
X
is smooth, redundant curves of the first and second kind are in particular
(-1)
-curves, so the smoothness of
X^{\prime}
is clear. We may assume that
\sigma\neq\mathrm{id}
, hence
r\neq 0
. Let
\widetilde{E}
be the exceptional divisor of some pure partial peeling morphism
\alpha
of the first (second) kind and let
{l}\leqslant D
be an
\alpha
-redundant curve of the first (first or second) kind. Let
E
be the sum of connected components of
\widetilde{E}
meeting
{l}
. By Corollary
3.5
and (
3.4
) we may assume that
\widetilde{E}=E
, so we have
{l}\leqslant\operatorname{Exc}\sigma\leqslant{l}+E
. Denote by
\sigma^{\prime}\colon X\longrightarrow Y
the contraction of
{l}
and by
\overline{\sigma}
the contraction of
\alpha({l})
. We may assume that
E\neq 0
and that
{l}
is not log exceptional of the first or second kind, as otherwise we are done by Corollary
2.13
and induction. If
r=1
then we are in case (1) of Proposition
5.7
with
E\neq 0
, which implies that
{l}
meets two distinct components of
D
and hence we are done by induction. We may thus assume that
r<1
. We are therefore in case (4), (5) or (6) of Proposition
5.7
with strict inequalities on the left in the defining conditions for the two latter cases. If
\frac{1}{r}
is an integer this gives a contradiction.

It remains to prove (1). Let
q=\overline{\sigma}\circ\alpha({l})
. Assume that
q\in\overline{X}
is smooth. We have
(\overline{\sigma}\circ\alpha)_{\mathrm{am}}=\overline{\sigma}\circ\alpha
, so
\overline{\sigma}\circ\alpha
is a partial squeezing of the first (second) kind. By Lemma
3.2
it factors through
\sigma
. By the assumption on
\sigma
we have in fact
\sigma=\overline{\sigma}\circ\alpha
, so
\sigma
is a peeling of
rD
and hence
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc) by Corollary
2.13
. Assume that
q\in\overline{X}
is singular. Then we are in case (4) of Proposition
5.7
with
E=U_{1}+U_{2}
, where
U_{1}=[2]
and
U_{2}=[4]
. The curve
\sigma^{\prime}(U_{1})
is log exceptional of the first kind, hence by assumption
\sigma
contracts exactly
{l}+U_{1}
. Contracting these curves in the reversed order we see that
\sigma
is at the same time a peeling of the second kind, hence we are done.

∎

We have seen in Example
3.10
that a composition of pure peeling morphisms does not have to be pure. In the following lemma we study this problem in more detail.

Lemma 5.15

(Non-purity after taking a minimal resolution)
.

Assume that
(X,rD)
, where
D
is a connected reduced divisor and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc). Denote by
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow X
the minimal resolution of singularities and put
\widetilde{D}=\pi^{-1}_{*}(D)+\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. Let
\alpha
be a pure partial peeling of
(X,rD)
of the first (second) kind. Then
\widetilde{\alpha}:=\alpha\circ\pi
is a partial peeling of
(\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})
of the first (respectively, second) kind. If
\widetilde{\alpha}
is not pure then the following hold.

(1)

Every connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\alpha}_{\mathrm{am}}
is a non-degenerate segment or a non-maximal twig of
\widetilde{D}
.

(2)

If
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is irreducible then
\pi_{*}^{-1}\operatorname{Exc}\alpha\cdot\operatorname{Exc}\pi=2
and
\pi_{*}^{-1}\operatorname{Exc}\alpha\cdot\operatorname{Bk}(\operatorname{Exc}\pi)\leqslant 1
.

(3)

If
r<1
or
\pi
contracts no segment of
\widetilde{D}
then
D
is irreducible.

By Lemma
5.9

\pi
is a peeling of the first (second) kind of
(\widetilde{X},r\widetilde{D})
, hence so is
\widetilde{\alpha}=\alpha\circ\pi
. Assume that
\widetilde{\alpha}
is not pure. In particular,
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha\neq 0
.

(1), (2) Let
Y\longrightarrow\alpha(X)
be the minimal resolution of singularities of
\alpha(X)
. Clearly,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{\mathrm{am}}\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow Y
is the induced morphism between resolutions. A partial MMP run decreases coefficients, and by Lemma
3.8
if we decompose a pure peeling into two peelings then both of them are pure. It follows that by induction we may assume that
{l}:=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is irreducible. Put
\widetilde{}{l}=\alpha_{*}^{-1}{l}
,
E=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
and
R=\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{}{l}-E
. Since
D
is connected, each connected component of
E
meets
{l}
. Since
\pi
is pure,
\widetilde{}{l}
is the only redundant component of
\widetilde{D}
. By Lemma
4.3
connected components of
E
are twigs, half-benches and segments of
\widetilde{D}
. Since
\alpha
is pure,
{l}\cdot K_{X}\geqslant 0
. Note that twigs and segments are chains and half-benches are chains of type
[2,b,2]
or admissible forks of type
(b;2,2,n)
, for some
b,n\geqslant 2
. We have
{l}\cdot K_{X}=\widetilde{}{l}\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+E-\operatorname{Bk}E)<\widetilde{}{l}\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+E)=\widetilde{}{l}\cdot E-1
. By Proposition
5.7

\widetilde{}{l}\cdot E\leqslant 2
, hence
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot E=2
and
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}E\leqslant 1
. It follows that
\widetilde{}{l}
meets no
(-2)
-rod of
E
. Indeed, if
E_{0}
is a
(-2)
-chain which is a connected component of
E
then
\operatorname{Bk}E_{0}=E_{0}
, hence
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}(E-E_{0})\leqslant 0
, which is impossible. By Corollary
5.11

\widetilde{D}
is snc at the points of
E
. We infer that we are in case (1) or (2) of Proposition
5.7
and we have
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot R=0
. Therefore, connected components of
E
are non-degenerate segments, half-benches and twigs of
\widetilde{D}
. Moreover,
\widetilde{}{l}
is superfluous in
\widetilde{D}
, unless
r=1
and
\widetilde{D}=E+\widetilde{}{l}
is a rational cycle consisting of two components, where
E^{2}\leqslant-5
due to the negative definiteness of
E+\widetilde{}{l}
.

We have
\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\alpha}_{\mathrm{am}}\leqslant E+\widetilde{}{l}
. From the description of the connected components of
E
we see that
\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\alpha}_{\mathrm{am}}
is a non-degenerate segment or a twig of
\widetilde{D}
. Suppose that it is a maximal twig (possibly a rod) of
\widetilde{D}
. Then it is equal to
E_{1}+{l}+E_{2}
, where
E_{1}
is a chain and a connected component of
E
, and
E_{2}
is either a chain which is a connected component of
E
or the long twig contained in the central chain of a half-bench meeting
\widetilde{}{l}
. Using Definition
4.5
we rewrite the inequality
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}E\leqslant 1
as
\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}(E_{1})+\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}(E_{2})+\frac{1}{d(E_{1})}+\frac{u}{d(E_{2})}\leqslant 1
, where
u=1
if
E_{2}
is a connected component of
E
and
0<u\leqslant 1
is as in Definition
4.5
(3) otherwise. By Lemma
4.1
we have

(5.12)

1-\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}(E_{1})-\operatorname{ind}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}(E_{2})=\frac{d(E_{1}+{l}+E_{2})}{d(E_{1})d(E_{2})},

so we obtain
ud(E_{1})+d(E_{2})\leqslant d(E_{1}+{l}+E_{2})
. Since
E_{1}+{l}+E_{2}
contracts to a smooth point, we have
d(E_{1}+{l}+E_{2})=1
, which is a contradiction, as
d(E_{1}),d(E_{2})\geqslant 2
.

(3) We may assume that
\alpha=\operatorname{ctr}_{{l}}\circ\gamma
, where
\widetilde{\gamma}:=\gamma\circ\pi
is pure and
{l}
is such that
\widetilde{}{l}:=\widetilde{\gamma}_{*}^{-1}{l}
is redundant. Put
E=\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\gamma}
and
E^{\prime}=\operatorname{Exc}\pi
. By the proof of (1) and (2)
\widetilde{}{l}
is a superfluous component of
\widetilde{D}
with
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot E=2
,
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}E\leqslant 1
and
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot R=0
. Suppose that
r<1
and a connected component of
E^{\prime}
is a segment of
\widetilde{D}
, call it
T^{\prime}
. Then by Lemma
5.1
(3) the segment consists of
(-2)
-curves, so
\operatorname{Bk}T^{\prime}=T^{\prime}
and hence the inequality
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot\operatorname{Bk}E\leqslant 1
fails. We may therefore assume that
\pi
contracts no segment of
\widetilde{D}
. By Lemma
4.3
connected components of
E
are segments, half-benches and twigs of
\widetilde{D}
. Suppose that some connected component of
E
, call it
T
, is a segment of
\widetilde{D}
. Since no connected component of
E^{\prime}
is a segment of
\widetilde{D}
, we infer that
E^{\prime}
is disjoint from
T
. Then
T+\widetilde{}{l}
is a segment of
\widetilde{D}
, hence it does not meet half-benches and twigs of
\widetilde{D}
, which by the connectedness of
E+\widetilde{}{l}
gives
E=T
. But then
\pi=\mathrm{id}
and
\alpha
is not pure; a contradiction.

Therefore, connected components of
E
are half-benches and twigs of
\widetilde{D}
. Since
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot R=0
, it follows that
R=0
, hence
D=\pi_{*}(E-E^{\prime})+{l}
. Since
\alpha
is pure, we have
\gamma_{*}^{-1}{l}\cdot K_{X}\geqslant 0
, so by the computation done in the proof of (1) and (2) we get in particular
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot E^{\prime}=2
. It follows that
E^{\prime}=E
, so
D
is irreducible.

∎

As we have seen in Example
5.13
, it can happen than given an MMP run
\psi
on a log surface
(X,rD)
its almost minimalization
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is also an MMP run for the same log surface (by definition it is an MMP run on the surface
X
). In this case, due to properties of the MMP run, if
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt or
(1-r)
-lc then so is its almost minimal model. In Lemma
5.17
we will show that this situation is a rule when
r<\frac{1}{2}
. The following example shows on the other hand, that for
r\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
one cannot expect
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
to be an MMP run on the log surface in general.

Example 5.16

(Almost minimalization may not be an MMP run)
.

We modify Example
3.10
.
Consider a smooth projective surface
X
with a divisor
[n_{1},1,n_{2}]
on it, where
n_{1}\geqslant n_{2}\geqslant 2
are positive integers such that
\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}}>\frac{1}{2}
and
(n_{1},n_{2})\neq(2,2)
. Let
{l}
be the
(-1)
-curve and let
D_{i}
,
i=1,2
be the component with self-intersection
-n_{i}
. Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
{l}+D_{1}+D_{2}
and let
\alpha\colon X\longrightarrow Y
be the contraction of
D_{1}+D_{2}
. Then
\sigma=\psi\circ\alpha^{-1}\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{X}
is the contraction of
\alpha_{*}{l}
. We have
\alpha^{*}K_{Y}=K_{X}+(1-\frac{2}{n_{1}})D_{1}+(1-\frac{2}{n_{2}})D_{2}
, hence
\alpha_{*}{l}\cdot K_{Y}=1-\frac{2}{n_{1}}-\frac{2}{n_{2}}<0
. It follows that
K_{X}
is
\alpha
-nef and
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional of the first kind.

Assume that
r>1-\frac{2}{n_{1}}
. Then
\alpha
is a peeling of
D=D_{1}+D_{2}
and
\psi
is an MMP run on
(X,rD)
. If
n_{2}=2
then
\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}={l}+D_{2}
, hence
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is an MMP run on
(X,rD)
. But take
n_{2}=3
. Then
n_{1}\in\{3,4,5\}
and the bound on
r
reads as
r>\frac{1}{3}
. We have now
\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}={l}
and since
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+rD)=-1+2r
, for
r\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
the almost minimalization
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is not an MMP run on
(X,rD)
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
(X,rD)
, with
D
reduced and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, be a log surface which is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc). Let
\psi=\operatorname{ctr}_{\alpha({l})}\circ\alpha
, where
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind of
rD
and
{l}\subseteq X
is such that
\alpha({l})
is log exceptional on
(\alpha(X),r\alpha_{*}D)
of the first (second) kind with
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
. Let
\widetilde{}{l}
and
\widetilde{D}
denote the proper transform of
{l}
and the reduced total transform of
D
on the minimal resolution of singularities of
X
, respectively. If
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is not an MMP run of the first (second) kind on
(X,rD)
then the following conditions hold:

(1)

\psi({l})\in\psi(X)
is a singular point,

(2)

The image of
\widetilde{}{l}
on the minimal resolution of singularities of
\psi(X)
is a normal crossing point of the image of
\widetilde{D}
, simple normal crossing if
r<1
, and hence
\widetilde{}{l}
is a superfluous component of
(\widetilde{D}+\widetilde{}{l})_{\mathrm{red}}
.

(3)

If
{l}\leqslant D
then
r=1
,
{l}
is log exceptional of the second kind and the minimal resolution of singularities of
X
is log crepant in a neighborhood of
{l}
.

(4)

If
{l}\nleq D
then
r\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
; in case
r=\frac{1}{2}
the curve
{l}
is log exceptional of the second kind and the minimal resolution of singularities is log crepant in a neighborhood of
{l}
.

Let
\overline{X}=\psi(X)
and
\overline{D}=\psi_{*}D
. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow X
be the minimal resolution of singularities and let
\widetilde{\alpha}=\alpha\circ\pi
. Put
\widetilde{}{l}=\pi_{*}^{-1}{l}
,
\widetilde{D}=\pi_{*}^{-1}D+\operatorname{Exc}\pi
,
\widetilde{\psi}:=\psi\circ\pi
and
E=\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\alpha}
. Let
q=\psi({l})
. By Lemma
5.9

\widetilde{\alpha}
is a partial peeling of
r\widetilde{D}
of the first (second) kind. We may assume that
E+\widetilde{}{l}
is connected, and hence that
\overline{X}\setminus\{q\}
is smooth. Since
\pi
is pure, we have
\widetilde{}{l}\cdot K_{\widetilde{X}}\leqslant{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
, so
\widetilde{}{l}
is a
(-1)
-curve.

Let
\sigma=\operatorname{ctr}_{{l}}
. By Corollary
3.5

\psi\circ\sigma^{-1}\colon(\sigma(X),r\sigma_{*}D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is a partial peeling of the first (second) kind. The curve
{l}
is not log exceptional of the first (second) kind, because otherwise
(\sigma(X),r\sigma_{*}D)
is
(1-r)
-dlt (
(1-r)
-lc), which
by (
3.4
) implies that
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is an MMP run of the first (second) kind. Since
\pi
is a peeling of the second kind,
{l}\cdot(K_{X}+rD)\leqslant\widetilde{}{l}\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+r\widetilde{D})
and the inequality is strict, unless
\pi
is log crepant in a neighborhood of
{l}
. In particular,
\widetilde{}{l}
is not log exceptional of the first (second) kind.

Suppose that
q\in\overline{X}
is smooth. Then
\overline{X}
is smooth, so
\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}}
, hence
\widetilde{\psi}
is a partial
K_{\widetilde{X}}
-MMP run of the first (second) kind. By Corollary
3.5

\psi
is a partial
K_{X}
-MMP run of the first (second) kind, too. Thus
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}=\psi
, so
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is an MMP run of the first (second) kind; a contradiction. This gives (1).

Put
\widetilde{D}^{-}=\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{}{l}
if
\widetilde{}{l}\leqslant\widetilde{D}
and
\widetilde{D}^{-}=\widetilde{D}
otherwise. Let
\theta\colon Y\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the minimal resolution of singularities of
\overline{X}
. Then
\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}}\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow Y
is the induced morphism of resolutions.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\textnormal{am}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{\min}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{ctr}_{\alpha({l})}}

Suppose that
\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}}({l})
is not a normal crossing point of
(\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}})_{*}\widetilde{D}
. By Corollary
5.11

r<1
and
\operatorname{Exc}\theta
is a
(-2)
-segment, hence
\theta
is crepant and log crepant. Let
\theta^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\longrightarrow\psi_{\mathrm{am}}(X)
be a minimal resolution of singularities. Then
\psi_{\min}\circ\theta^{\prime}
is some resolution of singularities of
\overline{X}
, hence
\psi_{\min}\circ\theta^{\prime}=\theta\circ\varphi
for some partial
K_{Y^{\prime}}
-MMP
\varphi
. Put
X^{\prime}=\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}(X)
. Since
\psi_{\min}
is pure and
\theta^{\prime}
is minimal, we have
\psi_{\min}^{*}K_{\overline{X}}\geqslant K_{X^{\prime}}
and
(\theta^{\prime})^{*}K_{X^{\prime}}\geqslant K_{Y^{\prime}}
, hence
(\psi_{\min}\circ\theta^{\prime})^{*}K_{\overline{X}}\geqslant K_{Y}^{\prime}
. On the other hand,
\varphi
is a
K_{Y^{\prime}}
-MMP, so we have
K_{Y^{\prime}}\geqslant\varphi^{*}K_{Y}
. But
\theta^{*}K_{\overline{X}}=K_{Y}
, hence
K_{Y^{\prime}}\geqslant(\theta\circ\varphi)^{*}K_{\overline{X}}
. We infer that
\theta\circ\varphi
is crepant, hence
\varphi
is crepant. It follows that
\varphi=\mathrm{id}_{Y^{\prime}}
. Since
\theta
is crepant and log crepant, so is
\psi_{\min}
, too. It follows that for every component
G
of
\operatorname{Exc}\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
we have
\operatorname{cf}(G;\psi_{\mathrm{am}}(X),r(\psi_{\mathrm{am}})_{*}D)=\operatorname{cf}(G;\overline{X},r\overline{D})
. Since
\psi
is an MMP run of the first (second) kind, it follows by Lemma
2.11
that so is
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
, too; a contradiction.

Thus
\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}}({l})
is a normal crossing point of
(\widetilde{\psi}_{\mathrm{am}})_{*}\widetilde{D}
. If it is not simple normal crossing then by Corollary
5.11

r=1
and
\operatorname{Exc}\theta
is a nodal rational curve. It follows that in any case
\widetilde{}{l}
is superfluous in
\widetilde{D}^{-}+\widetilde{}{l}
. Indeed, suppose otherwise. Then
\widetilde{D}^{-}=E
and
E+\widetilde{}{l}
is a rational cycle consisting of two components. Then
E^{2}\leqslant-5
due to the negative definiteness of
E+\widetilde{}{l}
. If
\widetilde{}{l}\leqslant\widetilde{D}
then both
E
and
\widetilde{}{l}
are log exceptional of the second kind, contrary to the assumption on
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
. Thus
\widetilde{}{l}\nleq\widetilde{D}
and

\alpha({l})\cdot(K_{\alpha(X)}+\alpha_{*}D)=\alpha({l})\cdot K_{\alpha(X)}={l}\cdot(K_{X}+(1+\tfrac{2}{E^{2}})E)=1+\tfrac{4}{E^{2}}>0;

a contradiction. This gives (2).

(3) Consider the case
\widetilde{}{l}\leqslant\widetilde{D}
. We have

0\leqslant{l}\cdot(K_{X}+rD)\leqslant\widetilde{}{l}\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+r\widetilde{D})=-1+r(\beta_{\widetilde{D}}(\widetilde{}{l})-1),

so by (2)
\beta_{\widetilde{D}}(\widetilde{}{l})=2
,
r=1
,
{l}
is log exceptional of the second kind and
\pi
is log crepant in a neighborhood of
{l}
.

(4) Consider the case
\widetilde{}{l}\not\leqslant\widetilde{D}
. By (2) we have now

0\leqslant{l}\cdot(K_{X}+rD)\leqslant\widetilde{}{l}\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+r\widetilde{D})=-1+r\widetilde{}{l}\cdot\widetilde{D}\leqslant 2r-1,

hence
r\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. If the equality holds then we argue as in (3).

∎

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
(X,rD)
, with
D
reduced and
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
, be a log surface which is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc). Assume that
\psi
is a partial MMP run on
(X,rD)
of the first (respectively, second) kind. If
r<\frac{1}{2}
or
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\leqslant D
and
r\neq 1
(respectively, if
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
or
\operatorname{Exc}\psi\leqslant D
) then
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is an MMP run of the first (respectively, second) kind on
(X,rD)
.

Using Lemma
3.7
, by induction we may assume that
\psi=\operatorname{ctr}_{\alpha({l})}\circ\alpha
, where
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind of
rD
and
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
. Now the proposition follows from Lemma
5.17
.

∎

5E.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
and a discussion of assumptions

The following examples show that in Theorem
1.1
(1),(2) the assumption that
X
is smooth or
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
cannot be omitted, as otherwise the
(1-r)
-log terminality and
(1-r)
-log canonicity may be destroyed by an almost minimalization. In particular, unlike in Proposition
5.18
,
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
does not have to be an MMP run for
(X,rD)
.

Example 5.19

(Non-
(1-r)
-lc almost minimal models of
(X,rD)
for
r\neq 1
)
.

Let
a\geqslant 2
be an integer and let
r\in(1-\frac{1}{3a-4},1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Consider a log smooth surface
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{D})
, where
\widetilde{D}=[3,1,3,a,0]
. Denote by
T_{1},\ldots,T_{5}
subsequent components of
\widetilde{D}
and by
\sigma\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow X
the contraction of
T_{3}+T_{4}
. Put
D=\sigma_{*}(\widetilde{D}-T_{2})
,
{l}=\sigma(T_{2})
and

, see Figure

.

.

Let
\alpha
be the contraction of

and
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
the contraction of
E+{l}
. We have

. By (
5.1
) we have
\operatorname{cf}(T_{3};X,rD)=\frac{2a-2+r}{3a-1}
, and

. Since

, we infer that

(1-r)
-dlt and
\alpha

, we get

. We compute
\alpha({l})\cdot(K_{\alpha(X)}+r\alpha_{*}D)=\frac{r-4/3}{3a-1}<0
. We have
\operatorname{ctr}_{l}(E)\cdot K_{\operatorname{ctr}_{l}(X)}\geqslant 0
, so
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}

{l}
. We conclude that:

(a)

is

-dlt,

(b)

{l}
is

(X,rD)
,

(c)

(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime}):=\psi_{\mathrm{am}}(X,rD)
, the almost minimal model of
(X,rD)
, is not
(1-r)
-dlt; it is not
(1-r)
-lc, unless
r=1
or
a=2
,

(d)

\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
is not an MMP run for
(X,rD)
.

(e)

(X,rD)

and not

.

Part (d) is a consequence of (a) and (c). For (c) we compute
\operatorname{cf}(T_{3};X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})=1-\frac{3}{2a-1}(1-r)\geqslant r
and
\operatorname{cf}(T_{4};X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})=1-\frac{(a+1)}{2a-1}(1-r)\geqslant r
, so
(X,rD)
is not
(1-r)
-dlt and it is not even
(1-r)
-lc, unless
r=1
or
a=2
. For (e) note that
{l}^{2}=-1+\frac{a}{3a-1}=\frac{1-2a}{3a-1}
, so

{l}\cdot(K_{X}+r(D+{l}))=\tfrac{1}{3a-1}(3ar-a-1+r(1-2a))=\tfrac{(r-1)(a+1)}{3a-1}\leqslant 0,

so
{l}
is log exceptional on
(X,r(D+{l}))
of the first kind if
r\neq 1
and of the second kind otherwise. It follows that if
(X,r(D+{l}))
is
(1-r)
-lc for
r\neq 1
or if it is
(1-r)
-dlt for
r=1
then so is
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
, too, contrary to part (c).

Example 5.20

(Non-
(1-r)
-lc almost minimal models of

for

)
.

Let

\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}

and three maximal twigs -

,

; see Figure

.
