.
Example
5.20

Denote the resulting surface by
\widetilde{X}
and put
\widetilde{D}=B+T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}-L-L^{\prime}+\widetilde{F}
, where
\widetilde{F}
is some smooth fiber. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow X
be the contraction of
B+T_{2}+T_{3}-L-L^{\prime}
. Put
F=\pi(\widetilde{F})
,
D=\pi_{*}\widetilde{D}=\pi(T_{1})+F
and
{l}=\pi(L)
. Then
(X,D)
is dlt and
\rho(X)=4
. We have
d(B+T_{2})=3n+4
and

{l}\cdot(K_{X}+D)=L\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+\widetilde{D}-\operatorname{Bk}_{\widetilde{D}}\operatorname{Exc}\pi)=1-\tfrac{2n}{2n+1}-\tfrac{1}{d(B+T_{2})}>0.

Since
\pi(T_{1})\cdot(K_{X}+D)<0
, the contraction of
\pi(T_{1})
, which we denote by
\alpha
, is a peeling of
D
. We have
d(T_{1}+B+T_{2})=9(n+1)
. We compute

\alpha({l})\cdot K_{\alpha(X)}=L\cdot(K_{\widetilde{X}}+\widetilde{D}-\operatorname{Bk}\widetilde{D})=\tfrac{1}{2n+1}-\tfrac{d(T_{1})+d(T_{2})}{d(T_{1}+B+T_{2})}=\tfrac{1}{2n+1}-\tfrac{2}{3n+3}<0.

Since
\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{F}+L
is negative definite, we infer that
{l}
is
\alpha
-almost log exceptional but not log exceptional. Let
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the contraction of
\pi(T_{1})+{l}
. Note that
\psi\circ\pi(T_{1})
necessarily a log terminal singularity (the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution is a fork of type
(2;2,3,3)
). We have
\rho(\overline{X})=2
and
\overline{D}=\psi(F)
, which implies that
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
is minimal, hence
\psi
is an MMP. Let
\sigma\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
be the contraction of
{l}
. We have
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
and
\sigma\circ\pi(T_{1})\cdot K_{X^{\prime}}\geqslant 0
, so
\sigma
is the almost minimalization of
\psi
. The singular point of
X^{\prime}
is a canonical singularity with the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution
[2,2,2,2]
. But the latter chain meets the proper transform of
D
in the middle component, hence
(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, the almost minimal model of
(X,D)
, is not log canonical.

Theorem
1.1
follows from the following result.

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
(X,D)
be a log surface with a reduced boundary and let
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-lc and that
X
is smooth or
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Let
\psi\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
be a partial MMP run of the second kind and let
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
be the almost minimalization of
\psi
. Put
X^{\prime}=\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}(X)
and
D^{\prime}=(\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}})_{*}D
. Then the following hold.

(1)

(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is
(1-r)
-lc.

(2)

If
\psi
is of the first kind and
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt then
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is
(1-r)
-dlt.

(3)

If
X
is smooth and
\frac{1}{r}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
then
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
can be decomposed into elementary steps so that every intermediate model is
(1-r)
-lc.

We argue by induction on
\#\operatorname{Exc}\psi
. By Lemma
3.7
we may assume that
\psi=\operatorname{ctr}_{\alpha({l})}\circ\alpha
, where
K_{X}
is
\alpha
-nef and
{l}\subseteq X
is such that
{l}\cdot K_{X}<0
. By Corollary
3.11

\alpha=\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}
, where
\alpha_{1}
is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind and
\alpha_{2}
is crepant and log crepant. Then
\alpha_{1}({l})
is log exceptional on
(\alpha_{1}(X),(\alpha_{1})_{*}D)
. By induction and by Corollary
3.5
we may assume that
\alpha_{2}=\mathrm{id}
, i.e.
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind. Let
\pi\colon\widetilde{X}\longrightarrow X
be the minimal resolution of singularities and let
\widetilde{\alpha}=\alpha\circ\pi
. Put
\widetilde{}{l}=\pi_{*}^{-1}{l}
,
\widetilde{D}=\pi_{*}^{-1}D+\operatorname{Exc}\pi
,
\widetilde{\psi}:=\psi\circ\pi
and
E=\operatorname{Exc}\widetilde{\alpha}
. Let
q=\psi({l})
. We denote the contraction of
{l}
by
\sigma
.

(1), (2) Note that (1) holds if
\psi
is log crepant. Indeed, then
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
is log crepant, and since
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-lc, so is its image under
\psi_{\mathrm{am}}
, too. By Remark
3.32
it follows that we may further assume that
\psi
is of the first kind. Then
\alpha
and
\alpha({l})
are of the first kind. We need to show that if
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc) then so is
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
, too.

Clearly, we may assume that
E+\widetilde{}{l}
is connected, hence
\overline{X}\setminus\{q\}
is smooth. We may assume that
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is not an MMP run on
(X,rD)
, as otherwise the claim follows from Lemma
2.11
. In particular,
E\neq 0
. We are thus in the situation of Lemma
5.17
. Then
q\in\overline{X}
is a singular point and
\widetilde{}{l}
is a superfluous component of
(\widetilde{D}+\widetilde{}{l})_{\mathrm{red}}
.

Consider the case
{l}\leqslant D
. By Lemma
5.17
(3)
r=1
,
{l}
is of the second kind and
\pi
is log crepant in a neighborhood of
{l}
. In particular,
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
is of the second kind. Thus we are in the situation when
\psi
is of the first kind and
(X,D)
is dlt, hence
\pi
is the identity. It follows that
X
is smooth in a neighborhood of
{l}
and
{l}
is superfluous in
D
, which implies that
(\sigma(X),\sigma_{*}D)
is dlt. Then
\psi^{\prime}:=\psi\circ\sigma^{-1}
is a partial MMP run on
(\sigma(X),\sigma_{*}D)
of the first kind and the theorem follows in this case by induction on
\#\operatorname{Exc}\psi
.

We may thus assume that
{l}\nleq D
. Consequently, we may assume that
(X,r(D+{l}))
is not
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively, not
(1-r)
-lc). Indeed, by Lemma
3.19
(2)
\psi
is an MMP run on
(X,r(D+{l}))
, so the theorem follows in this case from the case we have just settled.

Since
\widetilde{}{l}
is superfluous in
(\widetilde{D}+\widetilde{}{l})_{\mathrm{red}}
, we get a contradiction if
X
is smooth. By assumptions of the theorem and by Lemma
5.17
(4) we are left with the case
r=\frac{1}{2}
with
\psi
of the first kind and
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
being
\frac{1}{2}
-log terminal. Again, since
\pi
is log crepant in a neighborhood of
{l}
, it is in fact the identity, so
X
is smooth in the neighborhood of
{l}
. It follows that the image of
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
remains
\frac{1}{2}
-log terminal after the contraction of
{l}
, so the theorem follows again from the case when
{l}\leqslant D
.

(3) For
r=0
the claim is clear, so assume that
X
is smooth and
\frac{1}{r}\in\mathbb{N}
. By induction it is sufficient to argue that
(\sigma(X),r\sigma_{*}D)
is
(1-r)
-lc. By Corollary
2.13
we may assume that
{l}
is not log exceptional of the first or second kind. In particular,
r\neq 0
. Also, if
\alpha
is log crepant then we are done. By Remark
3.32
we may therefore assume that
\alpha
is of the first kind. By Lemma
5.14
(2) we may also assume that
{l}\nleq D
. Since
\alpha
is of the first kind, we are in case (1), (6), (8), or (10)-(12) of Proposition
5.8
. In case (1) the claim is clear. In cases (8) and (10)-(12) we have
r=\frac{1}{2}
,
{l}
meets
D
normally and
\operatorname{cf}({l};\sigma(X),r\sigma_{*}D)=\frac{1}{2}
, so we are done.

We are left with case (6) with
\frac{1}{r}<{l}\cdot D\leqslant\frac{1}{r}+s\leqslant\frac{1}{r}+1
and
0<r<1
. Since
\frac{1}{r}
is an integer, we get
s=1
and
{l}\cdot D=\frac{1}{r}+1
. Then
\operatorname{cf}({l};\sigma(X),r\sigma_{*}D)=r
. This time
{l}
can meet
D
non-normally. To show that
(\sigma(X),r\sigma_{*}D)
is
(1-r)
-lc, it remains to argue that for every exceptional prime divisor
U
over
X
we have
\operatorname{cf}(U;X,r(D+{l}))\leqslant r
. Let
Y\longrightarrow X
be a minimal log resolution such that
U
is a divisor on
Y
and let
\tau
be the contraction of
U
. Since
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-lc, it is sufficient to consider the case when the image of
U
on
X
is contained in
{l}\cap R
. Denote the reduced total transform of
D+{l}
on
Y
by
B
and put
Y^{\prime}=\tau(Y)
and
B^{\prime}=\tau_{*}B
. We have
\tau^{*}(K_{Y^{\prime}}+rB^{\prime})=K_{Y}+r\tau_{*}^{-1}B^{\prime}+u\cdot U
with
u=\operatorname{cf}(U;X,r(D+{l}))=r\tau_{*}^{-1}B^{\prime}\cdot U-1=r\operatorname{mult}_{\tau(U)}(\tau_{*}B)-1
, where
\operatorname{mult}_{q}(\tau_{*}B)
denotes the multiplicity of the divisor
\tau_{*}B
at the point
q
. Put
R=D-E
. Since
r=\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R}
, we see that
u\leqslant r
if and only if
\operatorname{mult}_{\tau(U)}(\tau_{*}B)\leqslant{l}\cdot R+1
. We are therefore done by Lemma
5.22
applied to
R+{l}
.

∎

We denote the multiplicity of a divisor
D
at
q
by
\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D)
and the local intersection index of divisors
D
and
B
at a point
q
by
D\cdot_{q}B
.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
D
be a reduced divisor on a smooth projective surface
X
. Let
\tau\colon(Y,D^{Y})\longrightarrow(X,D)
be a proper birational morphism from a smooth surface, where
D^{Y}
is the reduced total transform of
D
. If
D
has a smooth component
L
then for every
q\in Y
over
L\cap(D-L)
we have

(5.13)

\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D^{Y})\leqslant L\cdot_{\tau(q)}(D-L)+1.

We argue by induction with respect to
\#\operatorname{Exc}\tau
. If
\tau=\mathrm{id}
then, since
L
is smooth at
q
, we have
\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D-L)\leqslant L\cdot_{q}(D-L)
, so we are done. Assume that
\#\operatorname{Exc}\tau\neq 0
and write
\tau=\sigma\circ\tau^{\prime}
, where
\sigma\colon Z\longrightarrow X
is a blowup. Put
q^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime}(q)
and
U=\operatorname{Exc}\sigma
. Write
D^{Z}=D^{\prime}+L^{\prime}
, where
L^{\prime}
is the proper transform of
L
. By assumption
L\cdot_{\tau(q)}(D-L)\geqslant 1
, so to prove the above inequality we may assume that
q
is not a normal crossing point of
D^{Y}
and hence we may assume that all exceptional curves of
\tau^{\prime}
lie over
D^{\prime}-U
. In particular,
q^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}-U
and we have
D^{Y}=D^{\prime Y}+L^{\prime}
. Then
\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D^{Y})=\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D^{\prime Y})+[q\in L^{\prime}]
, where by definition
[q\in L^{\prime}]
equals
1
if
q\in L^{\prime}
and equals
0
otherwise. By induction we obtain

\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D^{Y})\leqslant U\cdot_{q^{\prime}}(D^{\prime}-U)+1+[q\in L^{\prime}].

The total transform of
L
on
Z
is
L^{\prime}+U
and the proper transform of
D-L
is
D^{\prime}-U
, so by the projection formula

L\cdot_{\tau(q)}(D-L)=\sum_{s\in U}(L^{\prime}+U)\cdot_{s}(D^{\prime}-U)\geqslant(L^{\prime}+U)\cdot_{q^{\prime}}(D^{\prime}-U),

which gives
\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D^{Y})\leqslant L\cdot_{\tau(q)}(D-L)+1+[q\in L^{\prime}]-L^{\prime}\cdot_{q^{\prime}}(D^{\prime}-U)
. But since
q^{\prime}\in D^{\prime}-U
, we have
[q\in L^{\prime}]\leqslant L^{\prime}\cdot_{q^{\prime}}(D^{\prime}-U)
, which completes the proof.

∎

Remark 5.23
.

Assume that a log surface
(X,rD)
, with
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
and
D
reduced is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc). Decompose an almost minimalization of
(X,rD)
as in Lemma
3.20
. If
X
is smooth or
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
then Theorem
5.21
implies that each of the intermediate models
(X_{i},D_{i})
,
i\geqslant 1
in that lemma is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc).

The following two examples show that in Theorem
1.1
(3) the assumption
\frac{1}{r}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
cannot be omitted, as otherwise the intermediate models may be non-
(1-r)
-lc.

Example 5.24

(Almost minimalization of
(X,rD)
with non-
(1-r)
-lc intermediate models;
r>\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Let
\overline{D}\subseteq\overline{X}
be a cuspidal cubic on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with the cusp at
p\in\overline{D}
and let
\psi\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be the minimal log resolution, with
D
being the reduced total transform of
\overline{D}
. We have
\operatorname{Exc}\psi=E_{1}+{l}+E_{2}=[2,1,3]
and
(D-\operatorname{Exc}\psi)\cdot{l}=1
. Assume that
r\in[\frac{1}{2},\frac{4}{5}]
, as in case (6) of Proposition
5.7
, see Fig.
12
.

-2
-3

Figure 12
.
Example
5.24
. Thick line is
D-\operatorname{Exc}\psi
, dashed line is
{l}
;
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant r\leqslant\frac{4}{5}
.

Let
\alpha
be the contraction of
E_{1}+E_{2}
. It is a pure peeling of the first kind if
r\neq\frac{1}{2}
and of the second kind otherwise. Since
\alpha({l})\cdot\alpha_{*}(K_{X}+rD)=\frac{5}{6}(r-\frac{4}{5})
,
{l}
is
\alpha
-redundant of the first kind if
r\neq\frac{4}{5}
and of the second kind otherwise. Thus
\psi
is an MMP run (complete, as
\rho(\mathbb{P}^{2})=1
) of the first kind if
r\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{4}{5})
and of the second kind if
r=\frac{1}{2}
or
r=\frac{4}{5}
. The almost minimalization is by definition a
K_{X}
-MMP over
\overline{X}
. The unique decomposition of
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}
as a
K_{X}
-MMP is
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}=\sigma_{3}\circ\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}
, where
\sigma_{1}
contracts
{l}
,
\sigma_{2}
contracts
\sigma_{1}(E_{1})
and
\sigma_{3}
contracts
\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1}(E_{2})
. Since
p\in\overline{X}
is smooth,
\psi
is a minimalization and (its own) almost minimalization at the same time, that is,
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}=\psi
, and hence the same holds for
\sigma_{3}\circ\sigma_{2}
and for
\sigma_{1}
. The intermediate models in the process of almost minimalization are
(X_{i},rD_{i})=(\sigma_{i}(X_{i-1}),r(\sigma_{i})_{*}D_{i-1})
,
i=1,2,3
, where
(X_{0},D_{0})=(X,D)
and
(X_{3},D_{3})=(\overline{X},\overline{D})
. We compute

(5.14)

\operatorname{cf}({l};X_{3},rD_{3})=6r-4<\operatorname{cf}({l};X_{2},rD_{2})=4r-2<\operatorname{cf}({l};X_{1},rD_{1})=3r-1,

(5.15)

\operatorname{cf}(E_{1};X_{3},rD_{3})=3r-2<\operatorname{cf}(E_{1};X_{2},rD_{2})=2r-1

and

(5.16)

\operatorname{cf}(E_{2};X_{3},rD_{3})=2r-1

Since
\psi
is an MMP run of the second kind,
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, which is a minimal and at the same time an almost minimal model, is
(1-r)
-lc, which is visible explicitly from the above computation, as
2r-1<r
,
3r-2<r
and
6r-4\leqslant r
. However, in general the intermediate models in the process of almost minimalization are not
(1-r)
-lc. Indeed, we have
\operatorname{cf}({l};X_{1},rD_{1})=3r-1\geqslant r
, as
r\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
, so
(X_{1},rD_{1})
is not
(1-r)
-lc for
r\neq\frac{1}{2}
and not
(1-r)
-dlt for
r=\frac{1}{2}
.

Remark 5.25

(Almost minimalization of
(X,rD)
with non-log canonical intermediate models)
.

We note that in the (unique) process of almost minimalization of the
(1-r)
-lc surface
(X,rD)
in Example
5.24
the intermediate models may be even non-lc. Indeed, we have
\operatorname{cf}(E_{1};X_{1},rD_{1})=3r-1>1
for
r>\frac{2}{3}
, so this happens for all
r\in(\frac{2}{3},\frac{4}{5}]
.

Example 5.26

(Almost minimalization of
(X,rD)
with non-
(1-r)
-lc intermediate models;
r\leqslant\frac{2}{3}
)
.

Let
m\geqslant 2
and
k\geqslant 2
be integers. Pick
m+1
fibers
F_{1},\ldots,F_{m},F_{\infty}
of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration of
\mathbb{F}_{1}=\operatorname{Proj}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1))
. Let
\overline{{l}}
be the
(-1)
-section. Blow up over
F_{\infty}\setminus{l}
so that the reduced total transform of
F_{\infty}
is the chain
M+E_{1}+\ldots+E_{k-1}+M^{\prime}=[1,(2)_{k-1},1]
. Let
X
be the resulting surface. Let
R
and
{l}
be the proper transforms of
F_{1}+\ldots+F_{m}
and
\overline{{l}}
.
Put
D=R+{l}+E
, where
E=E_{1}+\ldots+E_{k-1}
, see Figure
13
. Then
\rho(X)=2+k
and
{l}\cdot R=m
.

\cdots
\vdots

[(2)_{k-1}]

Figure 13
.
Example
5.26
. Thick line is
R
, dashed line is
{l}
;
\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R}<r\leqslant\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R-1/k}
.

Let
\psi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be the contraction of
{l}+E
, let
\overline{D}=\psi_{*}D
and let
\alpha
be the contraction of
E
. Then
\overline{X}
is smooth and
\rho(\overline{X})=2
. Since
(X,D)
is log smooth,
(X,rD)
is
(1-r)
-dlt for every
r\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. Assume that, as in case (5) of Proposition
5.7
,
\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R}<r\leqslant\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R-1/k}
. With this choice of
r
we check that
{l}
is not log exceptional on
(X,rD)
, but it is
\alpha
-redundant. Since
\overline{X}
is smooth, we have
\psi_{{\mathrm{am}}}=\psi
. Moreover, the log surface
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is now minimal. Indeed, suppose that there exists a log exceptional curve
L
on it. Since components of
\overline{D}
have positive self-intersection numbers,
L\nleq\overline{D}
. The inequality
L\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})<0
implies that
L
is a
(-1)
-curve with
L\cdot\psi_{*}F_{1}=\frac{1}{m}L\cdot\overline{D}<\frac{1}{rm}<1
. After the contraction of
L
components of
\overline{D}
become lines on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
intersecting each other with multiplicity
k\geqslant 2
; a contradiction. Thus
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is minimal and
\psi
is an MMP run.

Let
\sigma\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
be the contraction of
{l}
and let
D^{\prime}=\sigma_{*}D
. The log surface
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is an intermediate model in the process of almost minimalization of
(X,rD)
. We have
\operatorname{cf}({l};X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})=r+r{l}\cdot R-1>r
, so this model is not
(1-r)
-lc. Still, since
{l}\cdot R\geqslant 2
, we have
\frac{1}{{l}\cdot R-1/k}<\frac{2}{{l}\cdot R+1}
, hence
\operatorname{cf}({l};X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})=r+r{l}\cdot R-1<1
, so
(X^{\prime},rD^{\prime})
is dlt.

6.
Uniform boundaries with coefficient
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}

Recall that exceptional divisors of minimal resolutions of canonical (du Val) singularities are
(-2)
-chains and admissible
(-2)
-forks. Their Dynkin diagrams are
\mathrm{A}_{k}
for
k\geqslant 1
,
\mathrm{D}_{k}
for
k\geqslant 1
and
\mathrm{E}_{k}
for
k=6,7,8
. We say that a singular point is of
type
\mathrm{A}^{\star}_{k}
,
k\geqslant 1
if the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution is
E=[3,(2)_{k-1}]
for some
k\geqslant 1
. If
E
is a connected component of
D
we say that it is an
\mathrm{A}^{\star}_{k}
-rod of
D
. A singularity is of
type
\mathrm{A}^{\star}
if it is of type
\mathrm{A}^{\star}_{k}
for some
k\geqslant 1
. If the base field has characteristic zero then an
\mathrm{A}^{\star}_{k}
-singularity is exactly a Hirzebruch–Jung surface singularity
\tfrac{1}{2k+1}(1,k)
, that is, a germ of
\{z^{2k+1}=xy^{k}\}
, see
[
BH+04
, III.5]
. For the definition of the coefficient of
(X,D)
see Section
2B
.

Lemma 6.1

(Log surface germs with
\operatorname{cf}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Let
(\overline{X},\overline{D})
be a germ of a log surface. Let
E
be the exceptional divisor of the minimal resolution of singularity
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
. Put
D=\pi_{*}^{-1}D+E
.
Then the following hold:

(1)

\operatorname{cf}(\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})<\frac{1}{2}
if and only if
one of the following holds:

(a)

\overline{D}=0
and
E
is a
(-2)
-fork or a
(-2)
-rod of
D
or a rod of
D
with
E=[3,2,\ldots,2]
,

(b)

\overline{D}\neq 0
and
E
is a
(-2)
-twig of
D
.

(2)

\operatorname{cf}(\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})=\frac{1}{2}
if and only if one of the following holds:

(a)

\overline{D}=0
and
E=[4]
,
E=[3,2,\ldots,2,3]
,
E=[2,3,2]
or
E=\langle[2];[2],[2],[3,2,\ldots,2]\rangle
,

(b)

\overline{D}\neq 0
and
E
is a
(-2)
-segment of
D
or a twig of
D
with
E=[3,2,\ldots,2]
.

Assume first that
\overline{D}=0
. We check that for
E
is as in (2a) we have
\operatorname{cf}(\overline{X},0)=\frac{1}{2}
. Then we infer from Lemma
4.4
that if
\operatorname{cf}(E;\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
then the associated weighted decorated graph of the germ
(\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})
does not have a proper subgraph associated with the listed exceptional divisors. In particular, all components have self-intersection numbers not lower than
(-3)
and there is at most one equal to
(-3)
, necessarily a tip of
D
. It follows easily that the list is complete and that (1a) holds. Assume that
\overline{D}\neq 0
and that
E
is not a
(-2)
-segment of
D
. By Corollary
5.11
(2)
E
is a twig of
D
. It remains to show that if
E
contains a curve
E_{0}
which is not a
(-2)
-curve then
\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};\overline{X},r\overline{D})\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
and the equality holds if only if
E=[3,2,\ldots,2]
. By Lemma
4.4
we may assume that
E=[3,2,\ldots,2]
. Then
\operatorname{cf}(E_{0};\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})=\frac{1}{2}
by (
5.1
).

∎

The coefficient of an exceptional prime divisor
U\leqslant E
over
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is its coefficient in
\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, where by definition
K_{X}+r\pi_{*}^{-1}\overline{D}+\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})\sim\pi^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D}).
By Lemma
5.4
if
\pi
contracts rods, twigs, forks and
(-2)
-segments of
D
then

(6.1)

\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})=E-\operatorname{Bk}_{D}(E)-(1-r)\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}T,

where
T
is the sum of twigs which are not rods. Here is a complete computation of coefficients for the above graphs using the above equation and the explicit formulas computing the barks like (
4.9
).

Lemma 6.2

(Formulas for coefficients,
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Let the notation be as in Lemma
6.1
and let
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\cap\mathbb{Q}
. The following formulas for the coefficients over
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
hold.

(1a)

If
E
is a
(-2)
-fork or a
(-2)
-rod of
D
then
\operatorname{cf}_{X}=0
.

(1b)

If
E
is a rod of
D
and
E=E_{1}+\ldots+E_{k}=[3,(2)_{k-1}]
then
\operatorname{cf}(E_{i})=\frac{k+1-i}{2k+1}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.

(1c)

If
E=E_{1}+\ldots+E_{k}
is a
(-2)
-twig of
D
then
\operatorname{cf}(E_{i})=\frac{ir}{k+1}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.

(2a)

If
E
is a rod of
D
and
E=[4]
or
E=[3,2,\ldots,2,3]
then
\operatorname{cf}_{X}=\frac{1}{2}E
.

(2b)

If
E
is a rod or a fork of
D
and
E=[2,3,2]
or
E=\langle[2];[2],[2],[3,2,\ldots,2]\rangle
then
\operatorname{cf}_{X}=\frac{1}{2}E-\frac{1}{4}T_{0}
, where
T_{0}
is the sum of
(-2)
-tips of
D
contained in
E
.

(2c)

If
E
is
(-2)
-segment of
D
then
\operatorname{cf}_{X}=rE
.

(2d)

If
E
is a twig of
D
and
E=[3,2,\ldots,2]
then
\operatorname{cf}_{X}\geqslant rE
and the equality holds only for
r=\frac{1}{2}
.

Corollary 6.3

(Peeling for
(X,rD)
with
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and
D
a reduced divisor on
X
. Let
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]\cap\mathbb{Q}
and let
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
be a birational contraction. Then the following hold.

(1)

If
r=\frac{1}{2}
then
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the first (second) kind if and only if every connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is as in part (1) (respectively, part (1) or (2)) of Lemma
6.1
.

(2)

If
r<\frac{1}{2}
then
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the first kind if and only if every connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is as in Lemma
6.2
part (1a), (1c) or (1b) with
k<\frac{r}{1-2r}
.

(3)

If
r<\frac{1}{2}
then
\alpha
is a pure partial peeling of the second kind if and only if every connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is as in Lemma
6.2
part (1a), (1c), (2c) or (1b) with
k\leqslant\frac{r}{1-2r}
.

(4)

A curve
L\leqslant D
is
\alpha
-redundant of the first or second kind for some pure partial peeling
\alpha
if and only if it is a
(-1)
-curve meeting at most one
(-2)
-twig
\Delta
contracted by
\alpha
and such that

(6.2)

\beta_{D-\Delta}(L)\leqslant\frac{1}{r}+\frac{1}{d(\Delta)},

where the equality holds exactly when
L
is of the second kind.

(1)-(3). We may assume that
E=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is connected and nonzero. If
\alpha
is a peeling of the second kind then for every component
U
of
E
we have
\operatorname{cf}(U;\overline{X},r\overline{D})\leqslant\operatorname{cf}(U;X,rD)=r
by Lemma
2.11
and the inequality is strict if
\alpha
is of the first kind. Note that in case (1b) the maximal coefficient is
\operatorname{cf}(E_{1})=\frac{k}{k+1}
and
\operatorname{cf}(E_{1})\leqslant r
if and only if
k\leqslant\frac{r}{1-2r}
. Hence we are done by the above computations. The converse implication follows from Corollary
3.3
.

(4) Let
\alpha\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
be a pure partial peeling (of the first kind) and
L\leqslant D
be such that
\overline{L}:=\alpha(L)
is log exceptional of the first or second kind. By Lemma
3.19
,
L
is a
(-1)
-curve. By parts (1) and (2) we may assume that
\Delta:=\operatorname{Exc}\alpha
is a sum of
(-2)
-twigs meeting
L
. Since
\Delta+L
is negative definite,
\Delta
is a single
(-2)
-twig meeting
L
, possibly zero. We compute

\overline{L}\cdot(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=-1+r(\beta_{D-\Delta}(L)-\frac{1}{d(\Delta)})
, which gives (4).

∎

Note that a partial squeezing of
(X,rD)
for
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
is a composition of contractions of
(-1)
-curves as in Corollary
6.3
(4).

Remark 6.4
.

We note that the condition
k<\frac{r}{1-2r}
is empty for
r=\frac{1}{2}
and gives
k=0
for
r\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
. Also, while for
r=1
peeling contracts all admissible twigs, for
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
only
(-2)
-twigs are contracted. On the other hand, while an snc-minimal divisor
D
is automatically squeezed, it is not so for
r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. In fact, the smaller
r
is the more
(-1)
-curves in
D
are contracted by a squeezing morphism for
(X,rD)
.

Notation 6.5
.

Assume that
X
is smooth,
D
is reduced and
0<r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. We define:

(1)

\Gamma
as the sum of all
(-2)
-rods and admissible
(-2)
-forks of
D
,

(2)

\Lambda
as the sum of all
\mathrm{A}^{\star}_{k}
-rods of
D
with
k<\frac{r}{1-2r}
.

(3)

\Delta
as the sum of maximal
(-2)
-twigs in
D-\Gamma-\Lambda
,

For a connected component of
\Lambda
, say
\Lambda_{0}=[3,(2)_{k-1}]
we fix the order of components as written, that is,
[3]
is the first component.

Corollary 6.6
.

Assume that
X
is smooth,
D
is reduced and
0<r\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Let
\alpha\colon(X,rD)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
be a (unique) maximal pure partial peeling. Then:

(1)

\operatorname{Exc}\alpha=\Gamma+\Lambda+\Delta
,

(2)

\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},r\overline{D})=\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}\Lambda+r\operatorname{Bk}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\top}}\Delta
.

Part (1) follows from Corollary
6.3
and part (2) from Lemma
6.2
.

∎

Remark 6.7

(Multiplicity for
(1-r)
-dlt singularities)
.

Assume that
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
, with
\overline{D}
reduced, is
(1-r)
-dlt (respectively,
(1-r)
-lc) and
p\in\overline{X}
is smooth. Then

(6.3)

\operatorname{mult}_{p}(\overline{D})<1+\frac{1}{r}\quad(\text{respectively, \ }\operatorname{mult}_{p}(\overline{D})\leqslant 1+\frac{1}{r})

If
\frac{1}{r}
is an integer then for every infinitely near point
q
of
p
we have

(6.4)

\operatorname{mult}_{q}(D)\leqslant 1+\frac{1}{r},

where
D
is the reduced total transform of
\overline{D}
.

Let
\sigma
be a blowup at
p
with exceptional divisor
E
. Then

K_{X}+rD-\sigma^{*}(K_{\overline{X}}+r\overline{D})=(1-rE\cdot D)E=-r(\operatorname{mult}_{p}(\overline{D})-1-\tfrac{1}{r})E,

which gives (
6.3
). Assume that
\frac{1}{r}
is an integer. Let
\alpha\colon(X,D)\longrightarrow(X,D)
be a minimal log resolution. By Lemma
5.9

\alpha
is an peeling of the second kind. Since
p\in\overline{X}
is smooth, it is also a squeezing of the second kind. Then by Corollary
5.14
(2) each step of
\alpha
considered as squeezing leads to an
(1-r)
-lc log surface. Thus the above computation gives (
6.4
).

∎

If
\frac{1}{r}
is not an integer then the condition (
6.4
) may fail for infinitely near points of
p
.

Example 6.8
.

Let
(X,D)\longrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{2},D)
be the minimal log resolution of a cuspidal planar cubic. The log surface
(\overline{X},r\overline{D})
is
(1-r)
-lc for
r\leqslant\frac{4}{5}
, see Example
5.24
. Since one of the the infinitely near points of the cusp has multiplicity
3
, the inequality (
6.4
) fails for
r\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{4}{5}]
.

We now describe almost log exceptional curves for
r=\frac{1}{2}
. A similar characterization is possible for smaller
r
, but the number of cases grows as
r
decreases. Since on squeezed log surfaces of type
(X,rD)
the peeling morphism is unique by Corollary
5.3
, we may and will speak about
almost log exceptional curves
meaning that they are almost log exceptional with respect to this unique peeling.

Lemma 6.9

(Almost log exceptional curves for
r=\frac{1}{2}
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth surface and
D
a reduced divisor. Let
E
be the exceptional divisor of a (unique) maximal pure partial peeling of
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
. We have
E=\Gamma+\Lambda+\Delta
(see Notation
6.5
and Corollary
6.6
). A curve
A\not\leqslant D
is almost log exceptional of the first kind on
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
if and only if it is a
(-1)
-curve such that one of the following holds (see Fig.
14
):

(1)

A\cdot D\leqslant 1
. If
A\cdot T=1
for some component
T\leqslant E
then
T
is a tip of
\Delta
(not necessarily of
D
) or of a rod of
\Gamma+\Lambda
or it is the middle component of
[2,2,3]
- a connected component of
\Lambda
.

(2)

A\cdot D=2
, and
A
meets two different components
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
of
D
, such that

(a)

T_{1}\leqslant D-E
and
T_{2}
is a tip of
\Delta
, of
\Lambda
, or of a
(-2)
-rod of
\Gamma
,

(b)

T_{1}\leqslant D-E
and
T_{2}
is the middle curve of a connected component
[2,2,3]
of
\Lambda
,

(c)

T_{1}
,
T_{2}
are
(-3)
-curves in
\Lambda
,

(d)

T_{1}
is a
(-3)
-curve in
\Lambda
, and
T_{2}=[2]
is a connected component of
\Delta
or
\Gamma
.

(3)

A\cdot D=3
and
A\cdot(D-E)=1
,
A
meets a connected component
[2]
of
\Gamma
and a
(-3)
-curve in
\Lambda
.

A curve
A\nleq D
is almost log exceptional of the second kind on
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
if and only if it is a
(-1)
-curve such that (see Fig.
15
)

(4)

A\cdot D=2
and
A\cdot E=0
or

(5)

A\cdot D=3
and
A
meets
E
once, in a tip of a rod in
\Gamma
.

(1)

(a)

*

[]

\vdots
\Delta
\vdots
\Delta
\vdots
\Gamma
\vdots
\Lambda
-3
\vdots
\Lambda
-3
\Lambda
-3

(2a)

(b)

*

[0.75]

\vdots
\Delta
\vdots
\Delta
\vdots
\Gamma
-3
\vdots
\Lambda
\vdots
\Lambda
-3

(2b)

(c)

*

[0.15]

\Lambda
-3

(2c)

(d)

*

[0.2]

-3
\vdots
\Lambda
-3
\vdots
\Lambda

(2d)

(e)

*

[0.4]

-3
\vdots
\Lambda
\Gamma
-3
\vdots
\Lambda
\Delta

(3)

(f)

*

[0.2]

-3
\vdots
\Lambda
\Gamma

Figure 14
.
Lemma
6.9
(1)-(3). Thick line is
R-E
, dashed line is
A
.

(4)

(a)

*

[]

(5)

(b)

*

[]

\vdots
\Gamma
\vdots
\Gamma
\vdots
\Gamma
\vdots
\Gamma

Figure 15
.
Lemma
6.9
(4)-(5). Thick line is
R-E
, dashed line is
A
.

Let
\alpha\colon(X,\frac{1}{2}D)\longrightarrow(\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})
be the unique maximal pure partial peeling morphism. Put
R=D-E
and
E^{\flat}=\operatorname{cf}_{X}(\overline{X},\frac{1}{2}\overline{D})
. Let
A
be an almost log exceptional curve of the first or second kind on
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
. By Lemma
3.19
(3),
A
is an
\alpha
-redundant
(-1)
-curve of the first kind on
(X,\frac{1}{2}(D+A))
. By Corollary
6.3
(4) it meets at most one
(-2)
-twig
\Delta_{A}
of
D+A
and this twig satisfies

A\cdot(D-\Delta_{A})<2+\frac{1}{d(\Delta_{A})}.

It follows that
A\cdot D\leqslant 2+A\cdot\Delta_{A}\leqslant 3
. Moreover, since
\Delta_{A}
is a twig of
D+A
met by
A
, it is a
(-2)
-rod of
D
(in particular a connected component of
\Gamma
) met by
A
in a tip.

The negative definiteness of
E+A
implies that
A
does not meet a
(-2)
-fork in
\Gamma
and if it meets
\Delta+\Gamma
then only once, in a tip. Similarly, if it meets
\Lambda
then each connected component at most once, either in a tip or in the middle component of
[2,2,3]
. For
A\cdot D\leqslant 1
we get (1). We may thus assume that
A\cdot D\in\{2,3\}
. If
A\cdot E=0
then
A\cdot(K_{X}+\frac{1}{2}R+E^{\flat})=-1+\frac{1}{2}A\cdot D\geqslant 0
, which gives (4). We may thus assume that
A\cdot E\neq 0
, too.

Consider the case
A\cdot D=2
. Note that the coefficients of
E^{\flat}
are all smaller than
\frac{1}{2}
. We infer that
A
is of the first kind, because otherwise
A\cdot(R+2E^{\flat})=-2A\cdot K_{X}=2
, hence
A\cdot E>2A\cdot E^{\flat}=2-A\cdot R=A\cdot E
, which is impossible. Let
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
be the components of
D
meeting
A
. By the negative definiteness of
E+A
, we see that
T_{1}\neq T_{2}
and that in case
A\cdot R=1
we have (2a) or (2b). Similarly, in case
A\cdot R=0
we get (2c) or (2d).

Consider the case
A\cdot D=3
. Then
A\cdot\Delta_{A}=1
and
A\cdot(D-\Delta_{A})=2
. In particular,
A\cdot R\leqslant 2
. The negative definiteness of
E+A
implies that
A\cdot(\Gamma-\Delta_{A})=A\cdot\Delta=0
and
A\cdot R\in\{1,2\}
, hence we get (3) or (5). The
(-1)
-curve
A
is log exceptional of the second kind on
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
if and only if
A\cdot E^{\flat}=1-\frac{1}{2}A\cdot R
, which holds for (5) and fails for (3).

∎

The characterization in Lemma
6.9
gives the following corollary.

Corollary 6.10
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective surface and
D
a reduced divisor which contains no superfluous
(-1)
-curve (for instance,
D
is snc-minimal or
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
is squeezed). If a curve
A\not\leqslant D
is almost log exceptional of the first kind on
(X,\frac{1}{2}D)
then
A\cap(X\setminus D)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
\mathbb{A}^{*}
or
\mathbb{A}^{**}=\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0,1\}
. In the last case
A
meets three connected components of
D
(in particular,
X\setminus D
is not affine), each once in the sense of intersection theory; one of them is
[2]
, and the second one is
[3,2,\ldots,2]
and
A
meets the
(-3)
-curve.

7.
Appendix: Graphs, discriminants and coefficients

For the convenience of the reader we recall some terminology and elementary results concerning discriminants and coefficients (equivalently, log discrepancies) in graph-theoretic terms.

Let
D
be an undirected graph with vertices
|D|=\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
of weight
w_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
and unique edges between
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
for (not necessarily all)
i\neq j
of weight
w_{i,j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. We put
D_{i}\cdot D_{j}=w_{i,j}
if
i\neq j
and
D_{i}^{2}:=D_{i}\cdot D_{i}=-w_{i}
otherwise and we extend this product linearly to
\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{|D|\}
. For instance,
D
could be the dual graph of a reduced divisor on a smooth projective surface with ’
\cdot
’ being the intersection product. Put
Q(D)=[D_{i}\cdot D_{j}]_{i,j\leqslant n}
and
d(\emptyset)=1
and call

d(D):=\det(-Q(D))

the
discriminant of
D
; it does not depend on the chosen order of vertices. By a
weighted subgraph
of
D
we mean a subgraph of the underlying unweighted graph of
D
together with the associated integral weights of vertices and positive weights of edges not exceeding the weights of respective vertices and edges of
D
. Given
T
- a subset of vertices of
|D|
or a subgraph of
D
, we denote by
D-T
the subgraph remaining from
D
after removing vertices belonging to
T
and edges incident to them.
We call

\beta_{D}(T)=\sum_{U\in T}U\cdot\sum_{V\in|D-T|}V

the
branching number of
T
in
D
. A vertex with
\beta_{D}\leqslant 1
is called a
tip
of
D
. A
twig
of
D
is a connected subgraph
T
of
D
containing a tip of
D
and such that
\beta_{D}\leqslant 2
for all vertices of
T
. It is a
maximal twig
of
D
if
|T|
is a maximal subset of
|D|
with this property. Assume
D
is connected. If
\beta_{D}\leqslant 2
for every vertex of
D
then
D
is called a
chain
in case at least one of these inequalities is strict and is
circular
otherwise. Clearly, a twig is a chain. If
D
has no circular subgraph (in particular all its edges have weight
1
) then it is called a
tree
. A tree with only one branching component is a
fork
.

The following lemma follows from the additivity of the determinant function with respect to column addition and its behavior on block-triangular matrices.

Lemma 7.1
.

Assume that for a weighted graph
D
as above we have two subgraphs
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
such that
|D|
is a disjoint sum of
|D_{1}|
and
|D_{2}|
and such that there exist unique vertices
T_{1}\in|D_{1}|
in
T_{2}\in|D_{2}|
joined by an edge in
D
. Then

(7.1)

d(D)=d(D_{1})d(D_{2})-(T_{1}\cdot T_{2})d(D_{1}-T_{1})d(D_{2}-T_{2}).

In particular, if
T_{1}
is a tip of
D
and
T_{2}
is the vertex adjacent to
T_{1}
then

(7.2)

d(D)=(-T_{1}^{2})d(D-T_{1})-d(D-T_{1}-T_{2}).

To study graphs associated with exceptional divisors of resolutions of surface singularities we use some additional notions. A
decorated weighted graph
is a triple
(|D|,\cdot,\vartheta)
where
(|D|,\cdot)
is a weighted graph as above and
\vartheta
, the
decoration
, is a function
\vartheta\colon|D|\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{\geqslant 0}
. By a weighted decorated subgraph we mean a weighted subgraph together with a decoration not exceeding the original decoration.

Remark
.

Given a resolution
\pi\colon X\longrightarrow\overline{X}
of a singular projective surface
\overline{X}
and an effective divisor
\overline{D}
on
\overline{X}
, on the weighted graph
E
associated to the exceptional divisor of
\pi
we have a decoration function defined on vertices
E_{j}\in|E|
by

\vartheta(E_{j})=2p_{a}(E_{j})+E_{j}\cdot\pi^{-1}_{*}\overline{D},

where
p_{a}
denotes the arithmetic genus.

Definition 7.2
.

A weighted decorated graph
E=(|E|,\cdot,\vartheta)
is called a
resolution graph
if all its weights and decorations are non-negative and
Q(E)
is negative definite.

A resolution graph is
minimal
if all vertex weights are at least
2
. If they are all equal to
2
and additionally
\vartheta=0
(as a function) then the graph is called
du Val
. Every connected du Val resolution graph has Dynkin type A-D-E and comes from a du Val surface singularity. Any minimal chain
E
is a resolution graph by (
7.2
). The latter formula implies also that if
E_{1}
is a tip of a minimal chain
E
then
d(E-E_{1})<d(E)
. A minimal twig is called
admissible
. A minimal fork
E
with branching component
B
and maximal twigs
T_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
is
admissible
if
\beta_{E}(B)=3
and
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}>1
. We note that an admissible fork
E
has a negative definite intersection matrix. Indeed, by Sylvester’s criterion it is sufficient to argue that
d(E)>0
. Denoting by
C_{i}
the vertex of
T_{i}
adjacent to
B
, by Lemma
7.1

(7.3)

d(E)=d(T_{1})d(T_{2})d(T_{3})(-B^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d(T_{i}-C_{i})}{d(T_{i})}).

Since
d(T_{i}-C_{i})\leqslant d(T_{i})-1
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{d(T_{i}-C_{i})}{d(T_{i})}\leqslant 3-\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{1}{d(T_{i})}<2\leqslant-B^{2}
, hence
d(E)>0
.

To every resolution graph
E
we associate the
coefficient function

\operatorname{cf}_{E}
on the set of vertices defined uniquely by the equations

(7.4)

\sum_{i}\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{i})(-E_{i}\cdot E_{j})=k_{E}(E_{j}),\text{\quad where\quad}k_{E}(E_{j})=\vartheta(E_{j})-E_{j}\cdot E_{j}-2,\ \ j=1,\ldots,n.

This agrees with equations for coefficients of prime divisors for resolution graphs coming from birational morphisms of log surfaces. For a minimal connected resolution graph we have
k_{E}\geqslant 0
and the inequality is strict unless
E
is du Val. We write the equations equivalently as

(7.5)

\sum_{i}(1-\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{i}))(-E_{i}\cdot E_{j})=u_{E}(E_{j}),\text{\quad where\quad}u_{E}(E_{j})=2-\beta_{E}(E_{j})-\vartheta(E_{j}),\ \ j=1,\ldots,n.

Lemma 7.3

(Negativity Lemma,
[
KM98
, 3.39]
)
.

Let
E
be a connected resolution graph with vertices
E_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(1)

If
A=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}E_{j}
is such that
(-A)\cdot E_{i}\geqslant 0
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
then either
A=0
or
a_{j}>0
for every
j=1,\ldots,n
.

(2)

All entries of
(-Q(E))^{-1}
are positive.

(3)

If
E
is minimal then either
\operatorname{cf}_{E}>0
or
E
is du Val and then
\operatorname{cf}_{E}=0
.

(1) Write
A=A^{+}-A^{-}
where
A^{+}
and
A^{-}
have non-negative coefficients and contain no common vertex. We have
(A^{-})^{2}=(A^{+}-A)\cdot A^{-}\geqslant(-A)\cdot A^{-}\geqslant 0
, so
A^{-}=0
, because
Q(E)
is negative definite. Thus
A\geqslant 0
. Suppose that
A\neq 0
and
A
does not contain some vertex of
E
. By the connectedness of
E
there is a vertex
E_{i_{0}}
of
E
not contained in
A
but meeting
A
. Then
A\cdot E_{i_{0}}>0
; a contradiction.

(2) Apply (1) to
A
such that
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]^{T}
is a column of the matrix
-Q(E)
.

(3) Since
k_{E}(E_{j})\geqslant 0
, the statement is a consequence of part (2) and (
7.4
).

∎

If
E
is a tree then between any two vertices
E_{i}
,
E_{j}
,
i\neq j
there exists a unique shortest path in the graph joining them, which we denote by
\operatorname{path}(E_{i},E_{j})
.

Lemma 7.4

(
[
Kol92
, 3.1.10]
)
.

If a resolution graph
E=(\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\},\cdot,\vartheta)
is a tree then

(7.6)

d(E)(1-\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{j}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{E}(E_{i})\cdot d(E-\operatorname{path}(E_{i},E_{j})),

where
u_{E}(E_{j})=2-\beta_{E}(E_{j})-\vartheta(E_{j}).

Denote by
M^{[i,j]}
the
(i,j)
-th cofactor of a square matrix
M
. Given (
7.5
) it is sufficient to argue that

(7.7)

(-Q(E))^{[i,j]}=d(E-\operatorname{path}(E_{i},E_{j})).

The proof is by induction on
n
. We may assume that
n\geqslant 2
,
E_{n}
is a tip of
E
joined by an edge with
E_{n-1}
and
i\leqslant j
. If
j=n
then by induction
(-Q(E))^{[i,n]}=(-Q(E-E_{n}))^{[i,n-1]}=d(E-E_{n}-\operatorname{path}(E_{i},E_{n-1}))=d(E-\operatorname{path}(E_{i},E_{n}))
. If
i,j<n
then the formula is obtained by applying successively expansion of the determinant along a column, inductive assumption and then (
7.2
).

∎

We say that
E
is
\varepsilon
-dlt if
\operatorname{cf}_{E}<1-\varepsilon
. It is
terminal
if it is
1
-dlt. The following lemma follows from
[
Kol92
, 3.1.3]
and
[
Ale88
, 3.7(ii)-(iv)]
, cf.
[
KM99
, L.1(2) p.147]
. We add some details in the proof.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
E
be a connected minimal resolution graph and
F
a proper minimal subgraph of
E
. Assume that one of the following holds:

(1)

F
has the same vertex weights as
E
,

(2)

F
arises from
E
by decreasing the weight of a single vertex
E_{0}
and we have
d(F)>0
and
{\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{0})\leqslant 1}
.

Then
F
is a resolution graph and
\operatorname{cf}_{F}\leqslant\operatorname{cf}_{E}.
Moreover, either the inequality is strict or
\operatorname{cf}_{E}=\operatorname{cf}_{F}
and
E
(and hence
F
) is du Val in case (1) or
\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{0})=1
in case (2).

Let
|E|=\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{n}\}
and
|F|=\{E_{1},\ldots,E_{n^{\prime}}\}
,
n^{\prime}\leqslant n
. Put
x_{i}=\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{i})
,
e_{ij}:=-Q(E)_{i,j}=-E_{i}\cdot_{E}E_{j}
,
k_{i}=k_{E}(E_{i})
and
\vartheta_{i}=\vartheta_{E}(E_{i})
. Similarly, put
x_{i}^{\prime}=\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{i})
,
e_{ij}^{\prime}=-E_{i}\cdot_{F}E_{j}
,
k_{i}^{\prime}=k_{F}(E_{i})
and
\vartheta_{i}^{\prime}=\vartheta_{F}(E_{i})
. Due to Sylvester’s criterion, to prove that
-Q(F)
is positive definite it is sufficient to argue that
d(F)>d(E)
when
F
arises from
E
by decreasing the
(n-1,n)
-th (and
(n,n-1)
-th) entry by
u>0
. Via symmetric Gaussian elimination we change all other non-diagonal entries to zero. The operation keeps non-diagonal entries non-negative and (due to the positivity of leading principal minors) keeps diagonal ones positive, hence reduces the proof to the easy case
n=2
.

For
E
the equations (
7.5
) give

\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}e_{ij}=k_{j},\quad j=1,\ldots,n

and analogously for
F
. Put
x_{i}^{\prime}=k_{i}^{\prime}=e_{ij}^{\prime}=0
if
i>n^{\prime}
. Subtracting the equations we get:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime})=\Delta_{j},\quad\text{where}\quad\Delta_{j}:=k_{j}-k_{j}^{\prime}+\sum_{i=1}^{n^{\prime}}x_{i}^{\prime}(e_{ij}^{\prime}-e_{ij}),\quad j=1,\ldots,n.

If
E
is du Val then
k_{E}=0
, hence
k_{F}=0
and
F
is du Val, so
\operatorname{cf}_{F}=\operatorname{cf}_{E}=0
. We may therefore assume that
E
is not du Val. By Lemma
7.3
all entries of
(-Q(E))^{-1}
are positive. Since
E
is connected, we get
\operatorname{cf}_{E}>0
, so
x_{i}>0
for each
i
. To prove the inequality
\operatorname{cf}_{F}<\operatorname{cf}_{E}
, which is equivalent to the conjunction of inequalities
x_{i}-x_{i}^{\prime}>0
for each
i
, it is sufficient to show that the numbers
\Delta_{j}
are non-negative and not all of them vanish.

If
F
arises from
E
by decreasing some decoration, then
\Delta_{j}=k_{j}-k_{j}^{\prime}=\vartheta_{j}-\vartheta_{j}^{\prime}\geqslant 0
and the latter inequality is strict for some
j
, so we are done.

Assume that
F
arises from
E
by removing some vertex, say the last one. Then
\Delta_{j}=k_{j}-k_{j}^{\prime}=0
for
j<n
and
\Delta_{n}=k_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}x_{i}^{\prime}(-e_{in})
. We have
k_{n},x_{i}^{\prime},-e_{in}\geqslant 0
, so
\Delta_{n}\geqslant 0
. Suppose that the equality holds. Then
k_{n}=0
and
x_{i}^{\prime}\cdot e_{in}=0
for each
i<n
. Since
E
is not du Val and since
k_{n}=0
,
F
has a connected component which is not du Val. Since
E
is connected, this component contains some
E_{i}
with
e_{in}\neq 0
. Then
x_{i}^{\prime}\cdot e_{in}\neq 0
by Lemma
7.3
; a contradiction.

Assume that
F
arises from
E
by decreasing the weight of an edge, say
e_{n-1,n}-e^{\prime}_{n-1,n}=u<0
. Then
\Delta_{j}=0
for
j\leqslant n-2
,
\Delta_{n-1}=-ux^{\prime}_{n}\geqslant 0
and
\Delta_{n}=-ux^{\prime}_{n-1}\geqslant 0
. Suppose that
x^{\prime}_{n}=x^{\prime}_{n-1}=0
. The definition implies that
F
has at most two connected components and if it has two then one contains
E_{n-1}
and the other
E_{n}
. Since
x^{\prime}_{n}=x^{\prime}_{n-1}=0
, we infer that
F
is du Val. But then
E
is not du Val; a contradiction.

Finally, assume that
F
arises by decreasing the weight of a vertex. Put
E_{n}:=E_{0}
and
e_{n,n}-e^{\prime}_{n,n}=u>0
. Then
\Delta_{j}=0
for
j\leqslant n-1
and
\Delta_{n}=k_{n}-k^{\prime}_{n}-ux^{\prime}_{n}=u(1-x^{\prime}_{n})=u(1-\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{n}))
. By assumption
\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{n})\leqslant 1
, so we have
\operatorname{cf}_{F}\leqslant\operatorname{cf}_{E}
. Moreover, the inequality is strict if
\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{n})<1
and it is an equality if
\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{n})=1
.

∎

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
E
be a connected minimal resolution graph which is log terminal, i.e.
\operatorname{cf}_{E}<1
. Then
\vartheta_{E}<2
and
E
is a chain or an admissible fork. For every proper minimal subgraph
F
one has
\operatorname{cf}_{F}\leqslant\operatorname{cf}_{E}
and the inequality is strict, unless
E
is du Val. Moreover, if
F
arises from
E
by lowering some vertex weights only, then
d(F)<d(E)
.

The classification of log terminal graphs is well-known and follows from the above Lemmas
4.1
and
7.5
. (Note that for a circular graph resolution with
\vartheta=0
one has
\operatorname{cf}=1
by (
7.5
), which implies that
E
is a tree. Then using (
7.6
) one checks that
E
does not contain a subgraph with two branching vertices). We infer that
E
is a chain or an admissible fork and hence the same holds for every connected component of
F
, too. In particular,
d(F)>0
. The inequality
\vartheta<2
holds, because it holds for a graph with one vertex. Assume that
E
is not du Val and let
F
be a subgraph of
E
. By Lemma
7.5
, to show that
\operatorname{cf}_{F}<\operatorname{cf}_{E}
we may assume that
F
arises from
E
be decreasing the weight of a single vertex
E_{0}
. By (
7.6
) the product
d(E)(1-\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{0}))
does not depend on the weight of
E_{0}
, so

d(F)(1-\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{0}))=d(E)(1-\operatorname{cf}_{E}(E_{0}))>0.

We get
\operatorname{cf}_{F}(E_{0})<1
and hence
\operatorname{cf}_{F}<\operatorname{cf}_{E}<1
by Lemma
7.5
. We have
d(F)<d(E)
in this case, too.

∎
